### 本测试块考点概述 (Block Overview):
本测试块涵盖急性排斥反应（Acute rejection）与慢性排斥反应（Chronic rejection）病理组织学与机制鉴别：急性细胞性排斥反应（Acute cellular rejection：发生于术后数周至数月内，受者初始幼稚T细胞被供者树突状细胞直接抗原递呈激活，宿主CD8+ CTL浸润移植物血管内皮与实质细胞导致实质水肿伴间质致密单个核细胞淋巴细胞浸润，组织学特征：肾移植肾小管炎Tubulitis与内皮炎Endothelialitis，大剂量静脉注射甲泼尼龙糖皮质激素脉冲冲击治疗高度有效）；急性抗体/体液性排斥反应（受者从头生成特异性抗体结合移植物内皮，活化补体微血管病变，组织学特征性标志：微血管C4d沉积免疫组化染色强阳性）；慢性排斥反应（发生于数月至数年之后，不可逆隐匿性进行性功能衰退，机制为慢性低度细胞与体液免疫长期联合损伤，血管内皮慢性增生伴平滑肌移行肥厚，组织学特征：动脉加速粥样硬化伴闭塞性血管内膜增生Fibrointimal hyperplasia、移植物广泛间质纤维化萎缩；肺移植闭塞性细支气管炎Bronchiolitis obliterans、肝移植消失胆管综合征Vanishing bile duct、心脏移植加速性移植物冠状动脉硬化）。

### Question 051: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 01 (grafts)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute graft rejection, specifically the role of T-cell activation, antigen presentation, and cytokine release in mediating cellular damage to transplanted organs. (理解移植排斥反应的免疫机制，特别是T细胞活化、抗原呈递和细胞因子释放在介导移植器官细胞损伤中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates predominantly composed of lymphocytes and macrophages, and evidence of tubular epithelial cell injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows increased activation markers (CD69, CD25) on CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. Serum levels of IL-2 and IFN-γ are significantly elevated.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the patient's acute kidney graft rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial injury and thrombosis.
(B) Direct cytotoxicity by recipient NK cells recognizing donor HLA class I molecules.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against donor tubular epithelial cells presenting alloantigens via MHC class I molecules.
(D) Formation of immune complexes depositing in the glomeruli, causing glomerular damage.
(E) Neutrophil-mediated inflammation triggered by bacterial contamination of the graft.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Graft Type:** Kidney transplant from a deceased donor with unknown HLA type. HLA mismatch is a major risk factor for rejection.
- **Timeline:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute cellular rejection (ACR). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, and chronic rejection develops over months to years.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, often due to rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte and macrophage infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell injury. This histological pattern is highly suggestive of acute cellular rejection (ACR).
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d deposition is classically associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), it can also be seen in ACR, particularly in cases of mixed rejection or as a consequence of T-cell activation stimulating complement. However, the predominant histological findings point strongly towards ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased activation markers (CD69, CD25) on CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. This indicates T-cell activation, a hallmark of ACR.
- **Cytokine Levels:** Elevated IL-2 and IFN-γ. These are key cytokines produced by activated T cells (especially Th1 cells and cytotoxic T lymphocytes) that mediate cellular rejection. IL-2 promotes T-cell proliferation, and IFN-γ activates macrophages and enhances MHC expression.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism. Given the clinical presentation, timeline, biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular injury), T-cell activation markers, and elevated Th1 cytokines, T-cell mediated cytotoxicity is the most fitting mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T-cell mediated immune response against the transplanted organ. It typically occurs within the first few weeks to months post-transplant.
- **Mechanism:**
    1.  **Antigen Presentation:** Donor HLA molecules (alloantigens) on the graft's cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells) are recognized as foreign by the recipient's immune system.
    2.  **T-cell Activation:** Recipient T cells (both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes - CTLs) recognize these alloantigens presented on MHC molecules (MHC class II for CD4+ T cells, MHC class I for CD8+ T cells) by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft or draining lymph nodes.
    3.  **Cytokine Release:** Activated T cells release cytokines like IL-2 (promoting T-cell proliferation and activation) and IFN-γ (activating macrophages, increasing MHC expression, promoting inflammation).
    4.  **Cellular Infiltration:** T cells and macrophages infiltrate the graft parenchyma, particularly the interstitium and tubules.
    5.  **Direct Cytotoxicity:** CD8+ CTLs directly kill donor cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells) expressing alloantigens on MHC class I molecules via perforin/granzyme pathways or Fas/FasL interactions.
    6.  **Indirect Damage:** CD4+ T cells orchestrate the immune response by releasing cytokines that activate macrophages and other inflammatory cells, contributing to tissue damage. Macrophages also release cytotoxic mediators.
- **Histopathology:** The characteristic findings of ACR include interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltrates (predominantly T cells), macrophage accumulation, and damage to graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cell injury, endothelialitis).
- **Flow Cytometry/Cytokines:** Increased T-cell activation markers (CD69, CD25) and elevated levels of T-cell derived cytokines (IL-2, IFN-γ) confirm ongoing T-cell activation and response.
- **C4d Deposition:** While classically associated with AMR, C4d deposition can occur in ACR due to complement activation triggered by T-cell interactions or antibody production. Its presence doesn't exclude ACR, especially with the strong histological and cellular evidence presented.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial injury and thrombosis:** This describes Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR). AMR typically presents later than 3 weeks (though can occur earlier), often involves donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), and histologically shows microvascular inflammation (endothelialitis), C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, and sometimes thrombosis. While C4d is present here, the predominant histological findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular injury) and the strong evidence of T-cell activation (flow cytometry, cytokines) make ACR more likely. AMR is less likely to cause diffuse tubular injury as the primary feature.
- (B) **Direct cytotoxicity by recipient NK cells recognizing donor HLA class I molecules:** NK cells can contribute to rejection, especially if donor cells lack HLA class I molecules (which would normally inhibit NK cells) or express ligands that activate NK cells. However, NK cell-mediated rejection is less common than T-cell mediated ACR and typically doesn't present with the classic histological features and T-cell activation markers seen here. NK cells primarily target cells lacking MHC class I, not those expressing foreign MHC class I.
- (D) **Formation of immune complexes depositing in the glomeruli, causing glomerular damage:** This describes immune complex glomerulonephritis, which can occur post-transplant but is not the primary mechanism of acute graft rejection affecting the tubules and interstitium as described. It would typically present with proteinuria, hematuria, and signs of glomerular injury on biopsy, not the diffuse interstitial and tubular changes seen here.
- (E) **Neutrophil-mediated inflammation triggered by bacterial contamination of the graft:** This describes a bacterial infection of the graft, often occurring early post-transplant. It would present with fever, pain, and graft dysfunction, but the biopsy would show neutrophilic infiltrates, abscess formation, and potentially bacterial organisms, not the lymphocytic infiltrate characteristic of ACR. The clinical picture and biopsy findings strongly argue against infection as the primary cause.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | NK Cell Rejection | Immune Complex Glomerulonephritis | Bacterial Infection |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Timeline**            | Weeks to months                | Days to years                     | Variable          | Variable                          | Early post-transplant |
| **Primary Mediator**    | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)           | Antibodies (DSAs)                 | NK cells          | Immune Complexes                 | Bacteria           |
| **Histology**           | Lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular injury | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition | Variable          | Glomerular deposits, injury       | Neutrophilic infiltrate, abscesses |
| **Key Cytokines**       | IL-2, IFN-γ                    | Variable                          | Variable          | Variable                          | Variable           |
| **T-cell Activation**   | Yes (CD69, CD25 high)          | Variable                          | Variable          | No                                | No                 |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Possible                       | Characteristic                    | No                | No                                | No                 |
| **Mechanism**           | T-cell cytotoxicity, cytokines | Complement activation, ADCC       | Lysis, cytokines | Glomerular inflammation           | Bacterial toxins, enzymes |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily T-cell mediated, occurring typically within weeks of transplantation. Key features include lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury, T-cell activation markers, and elevated Th1 cytokines (IL-2, IFN-γ). Differentiate ACR from AMR (antibody-mediated, endothelialitis, C4d deposition) and infection (neutrophils, bacteria).

### Question 052: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 02 (transplanted)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of opportunistic infections in immunocompromised transplant recipients, specifically focusing on the role of T-cell deficiency in susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like *Pneumocystis jirovecii* (PJP) and the characteristic histopathological findings. (理解移植受者免疫功能低下时机会感染的病原体，特别是T细胞缺陷导致对胞内病原体如 *Pneumocystis jirovecii* 的易感性，以及其特征性组织病理学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male patient, 6 months post-renal transplant, presents with worsening dyspnea, non-productive cough, and fever (38.5°C). He is on chronic immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. His CD4+ T-cell count is 150 cells/µL (normal >500 cells/µL). Chest X-ray shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis reveals numerous organisms staining positive with Gomori methenamine silver (GMS) stain, appearing as foamy, cup-shaped structures within alveolar macrophages. The patient is started on trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX). Which of the following mechanisms best explains the patient's susceptibility to this infection?

(A) Impaired complement activation leading to decreased opsonization of extracellular bacteria.
(B) Deficiency in B-cell function resulting in reduced antibody production against encapsulated organisms.
(C) Reduced cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) activity against virus-infected cells.
(D) Decreased CD4+ T-cell mediated immunity impairing macrophage activation and clearance of intracellular pathogens.
(E) Impaired neutrophil chemotaxis and phagocytosis due to corticosteroid use.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, 6 months post-renal transplant. This immediately flags him as immunocompromised due to immunosuppressive therapy.
- **Symptoms:** Dyspnea, non-productive cough, fever. These are classic symptoms of pneumonia.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. These drugs suppress different arms of the immune system, notably T-cells (tacrolimus, mycophenolate) and overall inflammation/cellular immunity (prednisone).
- **CD4+ T-cell Count:** 150 cells/µL. This is severely low (<200 cells/µL is considered AIDS-defining for HIV, and similarly indicates profound T-cell deficiency in transplant patients), indicating significant cellular immunodeficiency.
- **Chest X-ray:** Diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. This pattern is characteristic of atypical pneumonias, including PJP.
- **BAL Findings:** Organisms staining positive with GMS, appearing as foamy, cup-shaped structures within alveolar macrophages. This is the pathognomonic description of *Pneumocystis jirovecii*.
- **Treatment:** TMP-SMX. This is the first-line treatment for PJP.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining susceptibility to this infection. Given the patient's history, immunosuppression, low CD4 count, and the identified pathogen (*Pneumocystis jirovecii*), the question targets the specific immune deficiency responsible for PJP susceptibility.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- ***Pneumocystis jirovecii* (PJP):** An opportunistic fungus (formerly considered a protozoan) that causes pneumonia primarily in immunocompromised individuals, especially those with T-cell deficiencies (e.g., HIV/AIDS, transplant recipients on immunosuppression).
- **Pathogenesis:** *P. jirovecii* is an intracellular pathogen that resides within alveolar macrophages and pneumocytes. Clearance of this organism relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, specifically the activation of macrophages by CD4+ T helper cells (Th1 response).
- **Immune Response:** CD4+ T cells recognize *P. jirovecii* antigens presented by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like macrophages. Upon recognition, CD4+ T cells release cytokines, primarily interferon-gamma (IFN-γ), which activates macrophages. Activated macrophages are crucial for phagocytosing and killing *P. jirovecii*.
- **Immunocompromise:** In patients with low CD4+ T-cell counts (<200 cells/µL), this Th1 response is severely impaired. Macrophages are not adequately activated by IFN-γ, leading to ineffective clearance of the intracellular *P. jirovecii*. The organism proliferates within the alveoli, causing inflammation and the characteristic interstitial pneumonia.
- **Other Immune Components:** While other immune components like B cells (antibodies) and neutrophils play roles in general immunity, they are not the primary defense mechanism against *P. jirovecii*. Complement activation is more relevant for extracellular bacteria. CTLs are primarily involved in eliminating virus-infected cells or tumor cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired complement activation leading to decreased opsonization of extracellular bacteria. This mechanism is relevant for infections caused by encapsulated extracellular bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). While transplant patients are susceptible to these, the specific pathogen identified (*P. jirovecii*) is an intracellular fungus, and its clearance primarily depends on cell-mediated immunity, not complement-mediated opsonization.
- (B) Deficiency in B-cell function resulting in reduced antibody production against encapsulated organisms. B-cell deficiency or impaired antibody production primarily increases susceptibility to encapsulated extracellular bacteria. While immunosuppressants can affect B cells, the primary defect leading to PJP is T-cell deficiency. Antibodies are not considered crucial for controlling *P. jirovecii*.
- (C) Reduced cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) activity against virus-infected cells. CTLs (CD8+ T cells) are responsible for killing virus-infected cells and tumor cells. While immunosuppression can affect CTL function, the primary immune defect responsible for PJP susceptibility is the deficiency in CD4+ T helper cells, which orchestrate macrophage activation.
- (E) Impaired neutrophil chemotaxis and phagocytosis due to corticosteroid use. Corticosteroids can impair neutrophil function, increasing susceptibility to bacterial and fungal infections. However, the primary defense against *P. jirovecii* is macrophage-mediated, activated by CD4+ T cells. While neutrophils might play a minor role, their impairment is not the main reason for PJP susceptibility in this context, which is overwhelmingly linked to T-cell deficiency.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | *Pneumocystis jirovecii* Pneumonia (PJP) | Encapsulated Bacterial Infections (e.g., *S. pneumoniae*) | Viral Infections (e.g., CMV) | Fungal Infections (e.g., *Aspergillus*) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Immune Defect** | CD4+ T-cell deficiency                  | B-cell/Antibody deficiency, Complement deficiency      | CTL (CD8+) deficiency        | Neutrophil deficiency, T-cell deficiency |
| **Pathogen Type**       | Intracellular fungus                     | Extracellular bacteria                                 | Intracellular virus           | Extracellular/Intracellular fungus      |
| **Key Immune Cell**     | Macrophage (activated by CD4+ T cells)   | Phagocytes (opsonized by Ab/Complement)                | CTLs                         | Neutrophils, Macrophages                |
| **Typical Patient**     | HIV/AIDS, Transplant recipients          | Asplenia, B-cell defects, Complement defects           | Immunocompromised (various)   | Neutropenia, Immunocompromised          |
| **Histopathology**      | Foamy cysts in alveolar macrophages       | Varies depending on organism                           | Inclusion bodies, cytopathic effect | Hyphae, granulomas                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Opportunistic infections in transplant recipients are strongly linked to the degree of immunosuppression, particularly T-cell deficiency. *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP) is a classic example of an infection occurring when CD4+ T-cell counts fall below 200 cells/µL, due to impaired macrophage activation. Remember that PJP is characterized by foamy, cup-shaped organisms seen on GMS stain within macrophages. Differentiate PJP susceptibility (T-cell defect) from susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (B-cell/Ab defect) or viruses (CTL defect).

### Question 053: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 03 (between)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically focusing on the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) in directly damaging graft cells (direct pathway) and the role of recipient antibodies against donor HLA antigens (indirect pathway) leading to complement activation and antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）通过直接途径损害移植细胞，以及受体抗体对抗体介导的间接途径，导致补体激活和抗体依赖性细胞介导的细胞毒性（ADCC）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an HLA mismatch at the A locus. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, and a significant decrease in urine output. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy is performed. Histopathology reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell injury, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the tubules and interstitium. The patient's serum contains anti-HLA class I antibodies.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) targeting donor endothelial cells.
(B) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated donor tubular epithelial cells.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(D) Complement-mediated injury due to deposition of C1q in the glomeruli, leading to glomerulonephritis.
(E) Formation of immune complexes containing donor-specific antibodies that deposit in the graft interstitium, causing inflammation.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, rising creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction/rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates: Suggests cellular rejection.
    - Tubular epithelial cell injury: Direct damage to graft cells.
    - Endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries: Inflammation of endothelial cells, often seen in both TCMR and AMR.
    - C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries: A marker of complement activation, strongly suggestive of AMR (indirect pathway).
    - Predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating tubules and interstitium: Indicates a significant T-cell mediated component, specifically cytotoxic T cells.
- **Serology:** Presence of anti-HLA class I antibodies. Confirms the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), a hallmark of AMR.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism responsible for graft injury. The combination of CD8+ T cell infiltration, tubular injury, endothelialitis, C4d deposition, and DSAs points towards a mixed acute rejection episode, but the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism given the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate directly attacking graft cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by recipient T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) on graft cells.
    - **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells (mainly CD8+) directly recognize intact donor MHC class I molecules presented on graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells). This leads to direct cytotoxicity (perforin/granzyme release) and apoptosis of graft cells. CD4+ T cells can also participate by recognizing donor MHC class II or processed donor antigens presented by recipient APCs.
    - **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (shed from graft cells or taken up) and present peptides derived from donor MHC via recipient MHC class II molecules to recipient CD4+ T cells. These activated CD4+ T cells then help activate recipient CD8+ T cells or B cells.
- **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by pre-formed or de novo donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on graft endothelial cells.
    - **Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies (typically IgM or IgG subclasses that efficiently activate complement) bind to donor HLA antigens, activating the classical complement pathway. This leads to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), causing direct lysis of endothelial cells. C4d deposition is a marker of complement activation.
    - **Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** Antibodies (typically IgG) bind to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. NK cells (and other effector cells like macrophages, neutrophils) express Fc receptors (e.g., CD16) that bind to the Fc portion of the bound antibody. This triggers the release of cytotoxic granules (perforin/granzymes) from the NK cell, leading to endothelial cell injury.
    - **Other Mechanisms:** Antibody-mediated inflammation, microvascular injury, thrombosis.
- **In this case:** The biopsy shows significant CD8+ T cell infiltration directly targeting tubules and interstitium, consistent with the direct pathway of ACR. While C4d deposition and DSAs indicate AMR, the prominent cellular infiltrate and direct attack on tubules by CD8+ cells point strongly towards a significant T-cell mediated component. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism causing the observed *graft injury*, and the direct cytotoxic action of CD8+ T cells fits the histological findings of tubular injury and interstitial inflammation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) targeting donor endothelial cells. While C4d deposition and DSAs are present, suggesting AMR, the primary histological finding described is tubular epithelial cell injury and interstitial inflammation with CD8+ T cell infiltration. CDC primarily targets endothelial cells, leading to endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis), which is not the main feature described. The question asks for the mechanism responsible for the *overall* graft injury, and the direct T-cell attack on tubules seems more prominent based on the description.
- (B) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated donor tubular epithelial cells. ADCC is a mechanism of AMR, but it typically targets endothelial cells. While antibodies are present, the biopsy highlights CD8+ T cell infiltration attacking tubules, not NK cells attacking antibody-coated tubules. ADCC primarily affects endothelial cells.
- (D) Complement-mediated injury due to deposition of C1q in the glomeruli, leading to glomerulonephritis. C4d deposition was found in *peritubular capillaries*, not glomeruli. Glomerulonephritis is a specific pattern of injury not described in the vignette. C1q deposition is an earlier step in complement activation, while C4d is a marker of the classical pathway activation downstream of C1.
- (E) Formation of immune complexes containing donor-specific antibodies that deposit in the graft interstitium, causing inflammation. Immune complex deposition is a mechanism seen in some forms of AMR, but it typically leads to interstitial inflammation and fibrosis, often without prominent endothelialitis or C4d deposition in capillaries. The vignette describes lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury, and endothelialitis, along with C4d deposition, which are more characteristic of direct AMR (CDC/ADCC) or TCMR. The prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate points away from immune complex deposition as the primary mechanism.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (Direct Pathway) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (CDC) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (ADCC) |
|-------------------------|-------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator**    | CD8+ T cells                              | Donor-Specific Antibodies (IgM/IgG)       | Donor-Specific Antibodies (IgG)          |
| **Target Cells**        | Graft Parenchymal Cells (Tubules, Endothelium) | Graft Endothelial Cells                  | Graft Endothelial Cells                  |
| **Mechanism**           | Direct Cytotoxicity (Perforin/Granzyme)    | Complement Activation (MAC)              | NK Cell Cytotoxicity (FcR binding)       |
| **Key Histology**       | Tubulitis, Interstitial Inflammation, Endothelialitis | Endothelialitis, Microvascular Inflammation | Endothelialitis, Microvascular Inflammation |
| **Key Immunopathology** | CD8+ T cell infiltrate                    | C4d deposition in capillaries            | C4d deposition in capillaries            |
| **DSAs Present?**       | Not required (can be indirect pathway)     | Yes                                      | Yes                                      |
| **C4d Deposition?**     | Usually absent                            | Yes (peritubular capillaries)            | Yes (peritubular capillaries)            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection can be broadly classified as cellular (T-cell mediated) or antibody-mediated. Cellular rejection involves T cells (especially CD8+) directly attacking graft cells. Antibody-mediated rejection involves DSAs activating complement (CDC) or recruiting effector cells (ADCC), primarily targeting endothelial cells. Histopathology (tubulitis, endothelialitis, C4d deposition) and serology (DSAs) help differentiate. This case shows features of both (CD8+ infiltrate + C4d + DSAs), representing mixed rejection, but the direct cytotoxic attack by CD8+ T cells on tubules is a prominent mechanism causing the observed graft injury. Differentiate ACR from AMR based on dominant cell types, target cells, and immunopathology markers.

<a id='question-054'></a>

### Question 054: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 04 (genetically)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic basis of complement deficiencies, specifically C2 deficiency, and its association with autoimmune diseases like systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), and its relevance in the context of transplantation and immune regulation. (理解补体缺乏症，特别是C2缺乏症的遗传基础，及其与自身免疫性疾病如系统性红斑狼疮（SLE）的关联，以及在移植和免疫调节背景下的相关性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to her rheumatologist complaining of recurrent joint pain, photosensitivity, and a malar rash. Her symptoms began approximately 2 years ago. Laboratory tests reveal a positive antinuclear antibody (ANA) titer, low serum complement levels (C3 and C4), and elevated anti-dsDNA antibodies. A family history reveals that her mother also has similar symptoms and was diagnosed with SLE at age 45. Genetic testing is performed on the patient and her mother. The patient is found to be homozygous for a null allele of the C2 gene, resulting in a complete absence of C2 protein. Her mother is heterozygous for the same null allele and another normal C2 allele. The patient is subsequently evaluated for a kidney transplant due to developing lupus nephritis. Pre-transplant testing reveals that she has antibodies against HLA-DR3, which is expressed on the donor kidney. Given her genetic background and autoimmune disease, what is the most likely immunological consequence related to her C2 deficiency that could impact her post-transplant outcome, particularly concerning antibody-mediated rejection?

(A) Increased formation of membrane attack complex (MAC) leading to enhanced clearance of antibody-coated cells.
(B) Impaired formation of C3 convertase on the classical pathway, leading to decreased opsonization and phagocytosis of immune complexes.
(C) Enhanced activation of the alternative pathway, compensating for the lack of classical pathway activation.
(D) Increased susceptibility to bacterial infections due to impaired formation of C5 convertase.
(E) Decreased production of C4, leading to reduced formation of C3 convertase on the lectin pathway.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with symptoms (joint pain, photosensitivity, malar rash) and lab findings (positive ANA, low C3/C4, anti-dsDNA) consistent with SLE.
*   **Family History:** Mother with SLE, suggesting a genetic predisposition.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Patient homozygous for a null C2 allele (C2 deficiency); mother heterozygous. This establishes the specific genetic defect.
*   **Clinical Context:** Patient needs a kidney transplant for lupus nephritis.
*   **Transplant Consideration:** Patient has anti-HLA-DR3 antibodies, indicating potential for antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks about the immunological consequence of C2 deficiency relevant to post-transplant AMR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is crucial for innate and adaptive immunity. It involves three pathways (classical, alternative, lectin) converging on C3 convertase (C3bBb or C4b2a) and C5 convertase (C4b2a3b or C3bBb3b).
*   **Classical Pathway:** Activated by antigen-antibody complexes (IgG, IgM). Requires C1q, C4, and C2. C1q binds to antibody, activating C1r and C1s, which cleave C4 into C4a and C4b, and C2 into C2a and C2b. C4b and C2a combine to form the classical pathway C3 convertase (C4b2a).
*   **Lectin Pathway:** Activated by mannose-binding lectin (MBL) binding to microbial surfaces. MBL-associated serine proteases (MASPs) cleave C4 and C2, forming the C3 convertase (C4b2a), identical to the classical pathway.
*   **Alternative Pathway:** Activated spontaneously on microbial surfaces or by certain immune complexes. Involves C3, Factor B, Factor D, and properdin. Forms the C3 convertase (C3bBb).
*   **C2 Deficiency:** A complete deficiency of C2 protein prevents the formation of the C3 convertase (C4b2a) on both the classical and lectin pathways.
*   **Consequences of C2 Deficiency:**
    *   Impaired clearance of immune complexes: C3 convertase is needed to cleave C3 into C3a (anaphylatoxin) and C3b (opsonin). C3b coats immune complexes, facilitating their phagocytosis by complement receptors (e.g., CR1) on macrophages and erythrocytes. Deficiency leads to reduced opsonization and clearance.
    *   Increased risk of SLE: Impaired clearance of immune complexes contributes to the deposition of these complexes in tissues (e.g., kidneys, joints), leading to chronic inflammation and autoimmunity characteristic of SLE.
    *   Increased susceptibility to certain infections (e.g., encapsulated bacteria): Although the alternative pathway can partially compensate, impaired opsonization increases susceptibility.
    *   Impact on Transplantation (AMR): In the context of transplantation, pre-formed antibodies (like anti-HLA) bind to the graft endothelium. Activation of the classical pathway (via these antibody-coated cells) is crucial for recruiting inflammatory cells and causing damage. C2 deficiency impairs this activation, potentially *reducing* the efficiency of classical pathway-mediated AMR. However, the *impaired clearance of immune complexes* (formed by donor antigens and recipient antibodies) is a significant factor. These complexes deposit in the graft, activating the alternative pathway and potentially contributing to chronic inflammation and damage. The question asks about the consequence *relevant to post-transplant outcome concerning antibody-mediated rejection*. The impaired clearance of immune complexes (due to lack of C3 convertase formation) is a key factor linking C2 deficiency to SLE and potentially influencing graft survival by promoting inflammation.

*   **Evaluating the Options:**
    *   (A) Incorrect. C2 deficiency *impairs* MAC formation because C5 convertase formation is less efficient (though the alternative pathway can still form it), and the overall complement cascade is dysregulated.
    *   (B) Correct. C2 is essential for forming the C3 convertase (C4b2a) on the classical and lectin pathways. Its deficiency directly impairs this step, leading to decreased C3b production, reduced opsonization, and impaired clearance of immune complexes. This is a central mechanism linking C2 deficiency to SLE and potentially influencing transplant outcomes.
    *   (C) Incorrect. While the alternative pathway can compensate to some extent, C2 deficiency primarily affects the classical and lectin pathways. It doesn't inherently *enhance* the alternative pathway; rather, the alternative pathway might become relatively more important but is still affected by the overall dysregulation.
    *   (D) Incorrect. While C2 deficiency can increase susceptibility to infections, the primary defect is in the classical/lectin pathways' C3 convertase formation, not C5 convertase formation itself (though C5 convertase formation is indirectly affected). The alternative pathway can still form C5 convertase.
    *   (E) Incorrect. C2 deficiency directly affects the classical and lectin pathways, not C4 production. C4 is produced normally, but it cannot effectively combine with C2a to form the C3 convertase.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This option describes the opposite effect. C2 deficiency leads to *decreased* MAC formation due to impaired C3 convertase and subsequent C5 convertase activity, not enhanced clearance. Test-takers might confuse the role of complement in cell lysis.
- (B): This option correctly identifies the core defect: C2 is required for C3 convertase formation on the classical and lectin pathways. Its absence impairs this crucial step, leading to reduced opsonization and clearance of immune complexes, a key feature in SLE pathogenesis and relevant to transplant rejection.
- (C): This option is incorrect. While the alternative pathway can partially compensate, C2 deficiency doesn't inherently enhance it. The primary defect lies in the classical/lectin pathways. Test-takers might mistakenly believe that if one pathway is blocked, another must be upregulated.
- (D): This option focuses on infection susceptibility, which is a consequence of C2 deficiency, but it misidentifies the primary defect as affecting C5 convertase formation. The main issue is impaired C3 convertase formation, leading to reduced opsonization. Test-takers might focus on the general role of complement in infection defense.
- (E): This option incorrectly links C2 deficiency to decreased C4 production. C4 is produced normally; the problem is the inability to form the functional C3 convertase (C4b2a) using C2. Test-takers might confuse the components of the classical pathway.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | C2 Deficiency                                  | C3 Deficiency                                  | C5 Deficiency                                  | Alternative Pathway Deficiency (e.g., Factor D) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Lack of C2 protein                             | Lack of C3 protein                             | Lack of C5 protein                             | Defect in alternative pathway components      |
| **Affected Pathways**   | Classical, Lectin                             | All pathways (converges on C3)                 | Terminal pathway (MAC formation)               | Alternative pathway                            |
| **C3 Convertase**       | Impaired (C4b2a formation blocked)             | Absent (C3 is a component)                     | Normal formation                             | Normal formation                             |
| **C5 Convertase**       | Impaired (less efficient formation)            | Absent (C3 is a component)                     | Normal formation                             | Normal formation                             |
| **MAC Formation**       | Impaired                                       | Impaired                                       | Absent                                         | Normal formation                             |
| **Opsonization (C3b)**  | Impaired                                       | Absent                                         | Normal formation                             | Normal formation                             |
| **Immune Complex Clearance** | Impaired                                       | Absent                                         | Normal formation                             | Normal formation                             |
| **Infection Risk**      | Increased (esp. encapsulated bacteria)         | Markedly increased (esp. encapsulated bacteria) | Increased (esp. Neisseria)                     | Increased (esp. Neisseria)                     |
| **Autoimmunity (SLE)**  | Increased risk                                 | Increased risk                                 | Less strongly associated                       | Not typically associated                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
C2 deficiency impairs the classical and lectin complement pathways by preventing C3 convertase formation, leading to reduced opsonization, impaired immune complex clearance, and increased risk of SLE. In transplantation, this can affect antibody-mediated rejection by impairing the clearance of antibody-donor antigen complexes. Key differentials include other early complement deficiencies (C1, C4) which also increase SLE risk, and C3 deficiency which has a more severe impact on opsonization and infection risk. C5 deficiency primarily affects MAC formation and Neisseria susceptibility.

<a id='question-055'></a>

### Question 055: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 05 (different)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) in recognizing and destroying allogeneic cells, and the resulting histopathological changes. (理解器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）在识别和破坏异体细胞中的作用，以及由此产生的组织病理学变化。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an A blood type, while the recipient is blood type B. The patient is started on standard immunosuppression including a calcineurin inhibitor (cyclosporine), an antimetabolite (mycophenolate mofetil), and corticosteroids (prednisone). Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, and decreased urine output. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy is performed. Histopathology reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells, including lymphocytes and macrophages. Many tubules show signs of epithelial cell injury and necrosis, with sloughed cells forming casts within the tubular lumen. Scattered eosinophils are also present. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy sample reveals a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the tubules and interstitium, along with CD4+ T cells primarily in the interstitium.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed tubular epithelial cell damage in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by pre-formed anti-donor antibodies binding to MHC class I molecules on tubular cells.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) initiated by donor-specific antibodies binding to MHC class II molecules on tubular cells, leading to membrane attack complex formation.
(C) Direct cytotoxic attack by CD8+ T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules presented on tubular epithelial cells.
(D) Macrophage-mediated inflammation triggered by cytokines released from CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on tubular cells.
(E) Neutrophil-mediated injury resulting from activation of the alternative complement pathway by donor endothelial cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetic nephropathy, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
- **ABO Incompatibility:** Donor is A, recipient is B. This is a major ABO incompatibility, which significantly increases the risk of hyperacute or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), although standard immunosuppression is given.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, which can be cellular (T-cell mediated) or antibody-mediated. Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, while chronic rejection develops over months to years.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, rising creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Histopathology:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cell infiltration (lymphocytes, macrophages), tubular epithelial cell injury/necrosis, tubular casts, scattered eosinophils. This pattern is consistent with acute rejection, potentially both cellular and antibody-mediated components.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of complement activation (specifically C4b), indicating evidence of antibody-mediated complement activation, often seen in AMR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells infiltrating tubules/interstitium, CD4+ T cells in interstitium. This points strongly towards a T-cell mediated component, specifically acute cellular rejection (ACR). CD8+ T cells are cytotoxic and directly target allogeneic cells. CD4+ T cells orchestrate the immune response.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of *tubular epithelial cell damage*. The vignette provides evidence for both AMR (C4d deposition, ABO incompatibility) and ACR (CD8+ T cell infiltration, timeline). However, the question specifically asks about damage to *tubular epithelial cells*. CD8+ T cells directly target cells presenting foreign MHC class I molecules, which are expressed on virtually all nucleated cells, including tubular epithelial cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes.
    - **Sensitization Phase:** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) recognize donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) presented by donor antigen-presenting cells (APCs) or recipient APCs that have processed donor antigens. This occurs in secondary lymphoid organs.
    - **Effector Phase:** Activated T cells migrate to the graft.
        - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on APCs. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages, recruit other inflammatory cells, and enhance MHC expression on graft cells, making them more susceptible to CD8+ T cell attack. They also provide help to B cells (though less relevant for direct tubular damage in ACR).
        - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules presented on *all* graft cells, including tubular epithelial cells. They directly kill these cells through mechanisms like:
            - **Perforin/Granzyme Pathway:** CD8+ T cells release perforin (forms pores in target cell membrane) and granzymes (enter through pores, activate caspases leading to apoptosis).
            - **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CD8+ T cells express Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to Fas receptor (CD95) on target cells, triggering apoptosis.
- **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (often pre-formed or rapidly generated) against donor antigens (e.g., HLA, ABO).
    - **Mechanisms:**
        - **Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies bind to MHC class II molecules on endothelial cells (primarily), activating the classical complement pathway, leading to MAC formation and cell lysis.
        - **Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** Antibodies bind to target cells (e.g., endothelial cells), and NK cells or other effector cells with Fc receptors bind to the antibody Fc region, triggering cytotoxicity.
        - **Inflammation:** Antibody binding can activate complement and Fc receptors on inflammatory cells (macrophages, neutrophils), leading to release of inflammatory mediators and tissue damage.
- **C4d Deposition:** Indicates complement activation, strongly suggesting an AMR component.
- **ABO Incompatibility:** Leads to pre-formed anti-A or anti-B antibodies in the recipient (in this case, anti-A antibodies in the B recipient). These antibodies can bind to donor endothelial cells, triggering AMR.
- **Tubular Epithelial Cell Damage:** While AMR primarily targets endothelial cells (causing microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition), severe AMR can also affect tubules. However, the direct killing of tubular epithelial cells is the hallmark of CD8+ T cell-mediated ACR. The flow cytometry showing abundant CD8+ T cells infiltrating the tubules strongly supports this mechanism as a major contributor to the observed tubular damage.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC)** mediated by pre-formed anti-donor antibodies binding to MHC class I molecules on tubular cells. While ABO incompatibility leads to pre-formed antibodies (anti-A), these antibodies primarily target ABO antigens, which are expressed on endothelial cells, not typically MHC class I on tubular cells. Furthermore, ADCC is a mechanism of AMR, but the primary target in ABO-incompatible AMR is endothelial cells, leading to microvascular injury (C4d deposition). Direct ADCC against tubular cells is less common than CD8+ T cell killing in ACR. The vignette emphasizes CD8+ T cell infiltration *into* the tubules.
- (B) **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC)** initiated by donor-specific antibodies binding to MHC class II molecules on tubular cells, leading to membrane attack complex formation. CDC is a mechanism of AMR. While antibodies might bind to MHC class II on tubular cells, the primary target of AMR-mediated CDC is endothelial cells. The vignette shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries (endothelial cells), supporting AMR targeting endothelium. Direct CDC against tubular epithelial cells is less characteristic of the primary mechanism of tubular damage in this mixed rejection scenario, especially given the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltration.
- (D) **Macrophage-mediated inflammation triggered by cytokines released from CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on tubular cells.** CD4+ T cells *do* play a role in ACR by releasing cytokines that activate macrophages and enhance MHC expression. Macrophages contribute to inflammation and tissue damage. However, this describes an *indirect* mechanism mediated by CD4+ T cells activating macrophages. The question asks for the mechanism of *direct* tubular epithelial cell damage. CD8+ T cells provide the direct cytotoxic attack on tubular cells.
- (E) **Neutrophil-mediated injury resulting from activation of the alternative complement pathway by donor endothelial cells.** Neutrophils are typically associated with acute bacterial infections or severe inflammatory responses, not the primary mechanism of ACR or AMR in this context. While complement activation (indicated by C4d) can attract neutrophils, the dominant cell types described in the histopathology are lymphocytes and macrophages, and the flow cytometry highlights T cells. The alternative complement pathway is less commonly implicated in AMR compared to the classical pathway activated by antibodies.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                  | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                               |
| :----------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediator**     | T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells, CD4+ helper T cells)     | Antibodies (pre-formed or de novo)                                   |
| **Target Cells**         | All nucleated cells (esp. endothelial & epithelial cells)       | Primarily endothelial cells (microvasculature)                       |
| **Key Mechanism**        | Direct T cell cytotoxicity (perforin/granzyme, Fas/FasL)        | Complement activation (CDC), ADCC, inflammation                     |
| **Histopathology**       | Interstitial inflammation, tubular atrophy, T cell infiltration | Microvascular inflammation (C4d deposition), endothelialitis, thrombosis |
| **Flow Cytometry**       | Increased CD8+ and CD4+ T cells in graft tissue               | Increased B cells, plasma cells, macrophages (less T cells)           |
| **C4d Deposition**       | Absent or minimal                                              | Present in peritubular capillaries (endothelium)                     |
| **Tubular Damage**       | Direct CD8+ T cell killing, cytokine effects                   | Secondary to endothelial damage, ischemia, or direct antibody effects |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute rejection in transplantation can be T-cell mediated (ACR) or antibody-mediated (AMR), or a combination. CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes directly recognize and kill allogeneic cells expressing MHC class I, including tubular epithelial cells, causing direct tubular damage. While AMR (indicated by C4d deposition) also occurs, the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltration into tubules points to ACR as the primary mechanism for the observed tubular epithelial cell damage. Differentiate ACR (T cells, direct cell killing) from AMR (antibodies, complement, endothelialitis).

---

<a id='question-056'></a>

### Question 056: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 06 (members)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of graft rejection, specifically chronic rejection mediated by alloantibodies targeting donor HLA antigens, leading to transplant vasculopathy and fibrosis. (理解移植排斥机制，特别是由针对供体HLA抗原的同种异体抗体介导的慢性排斥，导致移植血管病和纤维化。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with a history of hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus received a kidney transplant from a deceased donor 7 years ago. Initially, the graft function was excellent, but over the past 2 years, his serum creatinine has gradually increased from 1.2 mg/dL to 2.8 mg/dL. He reports no episodes of acute rejection. A recent renal biopsy reveals interstitial fibrosis, tubular atrophy, and prominent arteriolar hyalinosis and intimal thickening, consistent with chronic rejection. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in the peritubular capillaries and glomeruli. Serum analysis reveals elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I and class II antigens. The patient is currently managed with antihypertensive medications, insulin, and immunosuppressants (tacrolimus and mycophenolate mofetil). Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the chronic rejection observed in this patient?

(A) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against donor tubular epithelial cells.
(B) Complement-dependent endothelial cell injury and activation leading to transplant vasculopathy.
(C) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) by NK cells against donor endothelial cells.
(D) Formation of immune complexes depositing in the glomeruli, causing membranous nephropathy.
(E) Direct activation of donor fibroblasts by recipient T cells, leading to excessive collagen deposition.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male, history of hypertension and diabetes, 7 years post-kidney transplant. This sets the stage for chronic complications.
- **Clinical Course:** Gradual increase in serum creatinine over 2 years, indicating chronic graft dysfunction, not acute rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial fibrosis, tubular atrophy, arteriolar hyalinosis, and intimal thickening. These are classic histological features of chronic transplant rejection, specifically chronic active antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR) or chronic allograft vasculopathy.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries and glomeruli. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the complement C4 complex and is a marker of recent complement activation, strongly suggesting antibody-mediated injury.
- **Serology:** Elevated donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I and class II. This confirms the presence of antibodies targeting the donor graft.
- **Diagnosis:** Chronic rejection, likely antibody-mediated.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism responsible for the observed chronic rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Rejection:** A major cause of late graft failure. It involves both cellular (T-cell mediated) and humoral (antibody-mediated) components.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** DSAs bind to donor HLA antigens expressed on endothelial cells of the graft vasculature.
- **Mechanism of AMR:**
    - **Complement Activation:** Binding of DSA to donor HLA antigens activates the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3).
    - **Endothelial Cell Injury:** Complement activation generates C3 convertase and C5 convertase, leading to the deposition of C3d and C5b-9 (membrane attack complex, MAC) on endothelial cells. This causes endothelial cell activation, dysfunction, and injury.
    - **Inflammation:** Complement activation products (C3a, C5a) are potent chemoattractants for neutrophils and macrophages, further contributing to inflammation and endothelial damage.
    - **Transplant Vasculopathy:** Chronic endothelial injury leads to intimal thickening, hyalinosis (deposition of plasma proteins), fibrosis, and luminal narrowing of graft arterioles and capillaries. This process is termed chronic allograft vasculopathy.
    - **Peritubular Capillaritis (PTC):** Inflammation of peritubular capillaries, often associated with C4d deposition, is a hallmark of AMR.
    - **Glomerulitis:** Inflammation of glomeruli can also occur in AMR.
- **Role of C4d:** C4d deposition indicates recent complement activation, a key feature of AMR. While not perfectly specific, it is a widely used marker in transplant pathology.
- **DSA Specificity:** DSAs can target HLA class I, class II, or non-HLA antigens (e.g., MICA, endothelial cell antigens). HLA-specific DSAs are the primary drivers of AMR.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's gradual decline in graft function, histological findings (fibrosis, tubular atrophy, vasculopathy), C4d deposition, and presence of DSAs are all consistent with chronic antibody-mediated rejection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): T-cell mediated cytotoxicity primarily causes acute rejection (cellular rejection) or contributes to chronic rejection through direct attack on parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells). While T cells play a role in chronic rejection, the prominent C4d deposition and presence of DSAs strongly point towards an antibody-mediated mechanism as the primary driver in this case. T-cell cytotoxicity doesn't directly explain the vasculopathy and C4d deposition.
- (C): ADCC involves antibodies binding to target cells (e.g., endothelial cells), which are then killed by NK cells. While ADCC can contribute to AMR, the primary mechanism leading to transplant vasculopathy involves complement activation and direct endothelial injury, rather than NK cell-mediated killing. C4d deposition is a hallmark of complement activation, not ADCC.
- (D): Immune complex deposition typically occurs in systemic diseases like lupus nephritis or post-infectious glomerulonephritis, leading to membranous nephropathy or other glomerulonephritis patterns. While immune complexes can form in transplant recipients, the characteristic features of chronic rejection (vasculopathy, fibrosis) and the specific finding of C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries and glomeruli in the context of DSAs point towards AMR targeting endothelial cells directly, not primarily immune complex deposition in the glomeruli causing membranous nephropathy.
- (E): Direct activation of fibroblasts by T cells can contribute to fibrosis in chronic rejection, but it doesn't explain the prominent vascular changes (hyalinosis, intimal thickening) or the C4d deposition, which are central features of antibody-mediated injury to the endothelium. The primary insult in this case is antibody-mediated endothelial damage leading to downstream fibrosis and vasculopathy.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (B) - Complement-mediated Endothelial Injury | Option (A) - T-cell Cytotoxicity | Option (C) - ADCC | Option (D) - Immune Complex Deposition | Option (E) - T-cell Fibroblast Activation |
|-------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------|---------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator**    | Antibodies (DSA)                                            | T cells (CTLs)                   | Antibodies      | Immune Complexes                      | T cells                                  |
| **Target Cell**         | Endothelial cells                                           | Tubular epithelial cells         | Endothelial cells| Glomerular cells                       | Fibroblasts                              |
| **Key Mechanism**       | Complement activation (C4d deposition), endothelial injury  | Perforin/granzyme release       | NK cell killing | Immune complex deposition             | Cytokine release (e.g., TGF-β)            |
| **Histopathology**      | Vasculopathy (hyalinosis, intimal thickening), PTC, C4d+   | Tubulitis, interstitial infiltrate| Variable        | Membranous nephropathy, GN patterns   | Fibrosis                                 |
| **Role in Chronic Rej.**| Major driver of chronic AMR and vasculopathy                | Contributes, but less prominent in this scenario | Contributes | Less common mechanism in chronic rej. | Contributes to fibrosis                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is a major cause of late graft failure, characterized by DSAs, complement activation (indicated by C4d deposition), endothelial injury, and transplant vasculopathy. The key mechanism involves DSAs binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells, activating complement, leading to endothelial cell damage, inflammation, and ultimately fibrosis and vascular narrowing. Differentiate AMR from T-cell mediated rejection (acute cellular rejection, chronic cellular rejection) and other causes of chronic graft dysfunction.

<a id='question-057'></a>

### Question 057: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 07 (species)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Xenotransplantation rejection involves pre-existing antibodies against non-human antigens (hyperacute rejection) and complement activation, leading to rapid graft destruction. (异体移植排斥涉及对非人类抗原的预存抗体（超急性排斥）和补体激活，导致移植器官迅速破坏。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy is scheduled for a kidney transplant. Due to a severe shortage of human donor organs, he is offered a kidney from a genetically modified pig (Porcine Kidney for Transplantation, PKT). The pig kidney has been engineered to lack α-1,3-galactose residues, a major target for pre-existing human anti-pig antibodies. However, the patient develops severe hypotension, disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), and renal failure within minutes of the pig kidney being connected to his vasculature during the transplant surgery. A biopsy of the transplanted kidney shows diffuse thrombosis of glomerular capillaries, endothelial cell swelling, and deposition of C3d along the endothelium. The patient's serum is tested for antibodies against the pig kidney.

Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism responsible for this patient's rapid graft failure?

(A) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against porcine MHC class I molecules on the graft endothelium.
(B) Antibody-mediated complement activation against residual porcine antigens not targeted by the genetic modification, leading to endothelial damage and thrombosis.
(C) Neutrophil-mediated inflammation triggered by porcine complement components activating the alternative pathway.
(D) Macrophage infiltration and fibrosis mediated by porcine cytokines stimulating human cytokine receptors.
(E) Activation of the coagulation cascade by porcine tissue factor, leading to thrombosis independent of immune mechanisms.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male with ESRD needing a kidney transplant.
- **Transplant Type:** Xenotransplant (pig kidney).
- **Genetic Modification:** Pig kidney modified to lack α-1,3-galactose (reduces hyperacute rejection).
- **Clinical Course:** Rapid onset (minutes) of hypotension, DIC, renal failure after connecting the graft. This points towards hyperacute rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Glomerular capillary thrombosis, endothelial swelling, C3d deposition. These are classic signs of antibody-mediated complement activation and endothelial injury.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of rapid graft failure despite genetic modification.

The rapid onset (minutes) and the biopsy findings (thrombosis, endothelial swelling, C3d deposition) strongly suggest hyperacute rejection, an antibody-mediated process. Although the pig kidney was modified to lack α-1,3-galactose, this modification primarily targets pre-existing anti-A/B/O-like antibodies. Other porcine antigens can still elicit an immune response, especially if the patient has prior exposure (e.g., previous xenotransplant, exposure to pigs). The presence of C3d deposition confirms complement activation, which is a hallmark of antibody-mediated rejection. Therefore, antibodies against residual porcine antigens activating complement are the most likely cause.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Xenotransplantation:** Transplantation of organs/tissues between different species.
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours after transplantation. Mediated by pre-existing antibodies in the recipient against donor antigens (often carbohydrates like ABO or Galα1-3Gal).
- **Mechanism:** Pre-existing antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activating the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3). This leads to C3 convertase formation, C3 cleavage (C3a, C3b), C5 convertase formation, C5 cleavage (C5a, C5b), and formation of the Membrane Attack Complex (MAC, C5b-9). Complement activation also generates anaphylatoxins (C3a, C5a) that recruit inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages). Endothelial damage, platelet activation, coagulation cascade activation (DIC), and thrombosis occur rapidly.
- **Genetic Modification (α-1,3-galactose knockout):** Reduces hyperacute rejection mediated by anti-Gal antibodies, which are common in humans. However, other antigens (e.g., MHC class I, other carbohydrates, proteins) can still be targets.
- **Complement Activation:** The classical pathway is initiated by antibody binding to antigen. The alternative pathway can also be activated by microbial surfaces or damaged host cells, but in hyperacute rejection, the classical pathway triggered by antibodies is dominant.
- **C3d Deposition:** C3d is a breakdown product of C3b, a key component of the complement cascade. Its deposition on endothelial cells is a marker of complement activation.
- **DIC:** Disseminated intravascular coagulation, a condition characterized by widespread activation of clotting factors, leading to microthrombi formation and consumption of platelets and clotting factors, resulting in bleeding. Often seen in hyperacute rejection due to endothelial damage and complement activation.

##### 3. Option Analysis (干扰项分析)
- **(A) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against porcine MHC class I molecules on the graft endothelium.** This describes acute cellular rejection, which typically occurs days to weeks after transplantation. It involves T lymphocytes recognizing foreign MHC molecules. Hyperacute rejection is antibody-mediated and occurs much faster. While T-cell rejection can occur in xenotransplantation, it's not the mechanism for *rapid* failure within minutes.
- **(B) Antibody-mediated complement activation against residual porcine antigens not targeted by the genetic modification, leading to endothelial damage and thrombosis.** This aligns perfectly with the clinical presentation (rapid onset), biopsy findings (thrombosis, C3d deposition), and the known mechanisms of hyperacute rejection. Even with α-1,3-galactose knockout, other antigens can trigger pre-existing or newly formed antibodies, leading to complement activation and graft destruction.
- **(C) Neutrophil-mediated inflammation triggered by porcine complement components activating the alternative pathway.** While complement activation (both classical and alternative) and neutrophil infiltration occur in hyperacute rejection, the primary trigger is antibody binding to donor antigens activating the *classical* pathway. The alternative pathway might be secondarily activated, but it's not the initiating event driven by pre-existing antibodies.
- **(D) Macrophage infiltration and fibrosis mediated by porcine cytokines stimulating human cytokine receptors.** This describes chronic rejection, which occurs months to years after transplantation and involves fibrosis and scarring. It's not consistent with the rapid onset and acute thrombotic events seen here. Cytokine cross-reactivity is less common and doesn't explain the hyperacute process.
- **(E) Activation of the coagulation cascade by porcine tissue factor, leading to thrombosis independent of immune mechanisms.** While tissue factor can contribute to thrombosis, the rapid onset, diffuse nature of the thrombosis, endothelial swelling, and prominent C3d deposition strongly point towards an immune-mediated process (complement activation) as the primary driver, rather than just tissue factor exposure. Immune activation often amplifies coagulation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A):** Incorrect. This describes acute *cellular* rejection mediated by T cells, which occurs later (days/weeks) and involves MHC recognition. The vignette describes hyperacute rejection (minutes) with complement deposition, characteristic of antibody-mediated rejection.
- **(B):** Correct. This accurately describes hyperacute xenograft rejection. Pre-existing antibodies (even against antigens other than α-1,3-galactose) bind to the graft endothelium, activate complement (classical pathway), leading to endothelial damage, thrombosis, and DIC. C3d deposition confirms complement activation.
- **(C):** Incorrect. While complement activation and neutrophils are involved, the primary trigger in hyperacute rejection is antibody binding activating the *classical* complement pathway, not porcine complement components activating the alternative pathway as the primary event.
- **(D):** Incorrect. This describes *chronic* rejection, characterized by fibrosis and occurring much later (months/years). The vignette describes an acute, thrombotic event within minutes.
- **(E):** Incorrect. While tissue factor might play a role, the prominent C3d deposition and rapid onset strongly indicate an immune-mediated (antibody and complement) process as the primary cause, rather than a non-immune thrombotic event initiated solely by tissue factor.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Hyperacute Rejection (Xenograft) | Acute Cellular Rejection | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (Allo) | Chronic Rejection (Allo) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------------------|--------------------------|
| **Timing**           | Minutes to hours                 | Days to weeks            | Days to weeks                         | Months to years          |
| **Mediator**         | Pre-existing Antibodies          | T cells                  | Donor-specific Antibodies             | T cells, Antibodies      |
| **Target**           | Endothelium (donor antigens)     | Graft Parenchymal Cells  | Endothelium (donor antigens)          | Blood vessels            |
| **Mechanism**        | Complement Activation, Thrombosis | Cytotoxicity, Inflammation | Complement Activation, Thrombosis      | Fibrosis, Vascular changes |
| **Key Findings**     | Thrombosis, C3d deposition       | Infiltrates, Cell damage | Thrombosis, C3d deposition             | Fibrosis, Vascular lesions |
| **Example Scenario** | Rapid xenograft failure          | Rejection days post-transplant | Rejection days post-transplant        | Late graft dysfunction   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hyperacute rejection is a rapid, antibody-mediated process occurring within minutes to hours of transplantation, characterized by complement activation, endothelial damage, thrombosis, and DIC. It is caused by pre-existing antibodies against donor antigens. Even with genetic modifications targeting common antigens (like α-1,3-galactose), other antigens can trigger this reaction. Key differentials include acute cellular rejection (T-cell mediated, later onset) and acute antibody-mediated rejection (alloantigens, later onset). Chronic rejection involves fibrosis and occurs much later.
