<a id='question-072'></a>

### Question 072: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 22 (recognition)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological basis of graft rejection, specifically the role of T-cell recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC molecules, and the distinction between direct and indirect allorecognition pathways. (理解移植排斥的免疫学基础，特别是T细胞识别MHC分子呈递的异体抗原的作用，以及直接和间接异体识别途径的区别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor who is HLA-A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01 and HLA-DR negative. The recipient is HLA-A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01 and HLA-DR negative. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including a calcineurin inhibitor (cyclosporine) and mycophenolate mofetil. Three months post-transplant, the patient develops acute onset of fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from baseline 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL), and proteinuria. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with numerous T lymphocytes infiltrating the graft parenchyma. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells reactive to donor HLA-A*02:01 molecules. The patient's immunosuppression is intensified, and his renal function gradually improves. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the initial recognition of the donor kidney graft by the recipient's immune system?

(A) Recipient dendritic cells migrating to the donor lymph nodes, processing donor MHC class II molecules, and presenting peptides to recipient T cells.
(B) Recipient T cells directly recognizing intact donor MHC class I molecules on donor antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft.
(C) Recipient B cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on donor APCs and producing antibodies against them.
(D) Recipient macrophages phagocytosing donor cells and presenting donor MHC class II peptides via MHC class II molecules to recipient T cells.
(E) Recipient natural killer (NK) cells recognizing the absence of inhibitory signals on donor cells lacking self-MHC class I molecules.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient.
- **Graft Type:** Deceased donor kidney.
- **HLA Matching:** Both donor and recipient are HLA-A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01 and HLA-DR negative. This indicates a perfect HLA match at the loci tested, but doesn't exclude other minor histocompatibility antigens or non-HLA antigens.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Acute rejection episode 3 months post-transplant (fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, proteinuria). This timeframe is typical for acute cellular rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation with T lymphocytes, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. These findings are characteristic of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) and T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR), often occurring concurrently or sequentially. C4d deposition suggests complement activation, often associated with donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), but can also be seen in TCMR.
- **Immunological Findings:** Increased CD8+ T cells reactive to donor HLA-A*02:01 molecules. This points strongly towards a T-cell mediated rejection process targeting specific donor HLA antigens.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of *initial recognition* of the donor graft.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Graft Rejection Pathways:** Allorecognition involves the recipient's immune system recognizing the donor graft as foreign. This occurs via two main pathways:
    - **Direct Allorecognition:** Recipient T cells directly recognize intact donor MHC molecules (MHC class I and MHC class II) expressed on donor antigen-presenting cells (APCs) that have migrated from the graft to the recipient's secondary lymphoid organs (lymph nodes, spleen). This pathway is considered the primary driver of acute cellular rejection. Donor APCs present donor MHC molecules to recipient T cells. Recipient T cells must recognize both the foreign MHC molecule *and* any peptides bound to it. This requires the recipient T cell receptor (TCR) to bind to the foreign MHC-peptide complex.
    - **Indirect Allorecognition:** Recipient APCs (like dendritic cells) process donor MHC molecules (which are internalized from the graft) into peptides. These peptides are then presented on the recipient APCs' own MHC class II molecules to recipient T helper cells. This pathway resembles the response to conventional foreign antigens and is thought to be more important in chronic rejection and the development of DSAs (leading to AMR).
- **The Question Focus:** The question asks about the *initial recognition* mechanism. The clinical findings (acute rejection, T cell infiltration, CD8+ T cell reactivity to donor HLA) strongly suggest acute cellular rejection. The direct pathway is the primary mechanism for initiating acute cellular rejection.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) Describes the indirect pathway. While important, it's generally considered secondary to the direct pathway for initiating acute cellular rejection.
    - (B) Describes the direct pathway. Recipient T cells (especially CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) directly recognize intact donor MHC class I molecules on donor APCs within the graft or migrating to secondary lymphoid organs. This is the most rapid and potent pathway for initiating acute rejection.
    - (C) Describes antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition is present, the question specifically asks about the *initial recognition* by the immune system, and the prominent finding is T-cell reactivity. B cells are involved in AMR, not the primary T-cell mediated recognition described.
    - (D) Describes a variation of the indirect pathway where donor MHC class II peptides are presented by recipient MHC class II. This is part of the indirect pathway, not the direct pathway.
    - (E) Describes NK cell activity, which can contribute to hyperacute rejection (pre-formed antibodies against ABO or HLA antigens) or potentially graft dysfunction, but is not the primary mechanism for recognizing alloantigens in the context of acute cellular rejection described here.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This describes the indirect pathway of allorecognition. Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules and present peptides on their own MHC class II molecules. This pathway is important for chronic rejection and DSA development, but the direct pathway is considered the primary initiator of acute cellular rejection. The vignette's focus on acute T-cell mediated rejection makes the direct pathway more likely the *initial* recognition event.
- (B): This correctly describes the direct pathway of allorecognition. Recipient T cells directly recognize intact donor MHC molecules (class I and II) on donor APCs. This is the most potent and rapid mechanism for initiating acute cellular rejection, consistent with the clinical presentation and immunological findings (CD8+ T cell reactivity to donor HLA).
- (C): This describes the role of B cells in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition suggests complement activation often associated with DSAs, the primary immunological finding highlighted is T-cell mediated rejection (T cell infiltration, CD8+ T cell reactivity). B cells recognize antigens, not intact MHC molecules directly in this context.
- (D): This is another description related to the indirect pathway, where donor MHC class II peptides are presented by recipient MHC class II molecules. Similar to (A), this is part of the indirect pathway, not the direct pathway responsible for the initial, potent T-cell activation in acute rejection.
- (E): NK cells recognize cells lacking self-MHC class I molecules (missing-self hypothesis) or cells expressing stress ligands. While NK cells can play a role in graft rejection, especially in hyperacute rejection or situations with altered MHC expression, the primary mechanism for recognizing foreign MHC molecules in this acute cellular rejection scenario is T-cell mediated via the direct or indirect pathways.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Direct Allorecognition (Option B)                                  | Indirect Allorecognition (Option A, D)                               | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (Option C)                               | NK Cell Rejection (Option E)                                       |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Recognizing Cell** | Recipient T cells                                                    | Recipient T cells                                                    | Recipient B cells                                                     | Recipient NK cells                                                   |
| **Target Antigen**   | Intact donor MHC molecules (Class I & II) on donor APCs              | Processed donor MHC peptides presented on recipient MHC Class II       | Donor MHC molecules (often complexed with other antigens)              | Cells lacking self-MHC Class I or expressing stress ligands          |
| **APC Type**         | Donor APCs (e.g., dendritic cells)                                   | Recipient APCs (e.g., dendritic cells)                               | Donor cells, recipient APCs                                           | Any nucleated cell                                                   |
| **MHC Restriction**  | Donor MHC molecules                                                  | Recipient MHC Class II molecules                                       | N/A (Antibodies bind directly)                                        | N/A (NK cells use inhibitory/activating receptors)                   |
| **Timing**           | Rapid, primary driver of acute cellular rejection                    | Slower, important for chronic rejection, DSA development              | Can be acute or chronic                                               | Primarily hyperacute or specific situations                         |
| **Key Cells**        | CD4+ and CD8+ T cells                                                | CD4+ T cells                                                          | B cells, plasma cells                                                 | NK cells                                                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary mechanism for initiating acute cellular graft rejection is the direct pathway, where recipient T cells recognize intact donor MHC molecules on donor APCs. This contrasts with the indirect pathway, where recipient APCs present processed donor MHC peptides. Antibody-mediated rejection involves B cells and antibodies, while NK cell rejection targets cells lacking MHC class I. Understanding these distinct pathways is crucial for differentiating types of graft rejection.

---

<a id='question-073'></a>

### Question 073: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 23 (transplanted)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis and immune evasion mechanisms of *Legionella pneumophila*, specifically its intracellular survival within macrophages and modulation of host cell signaling pathways, particularly the inhibition of phagosome-lysosome fusion. (理解 *Legionella pneumophila* 的发病机制和免疫逃避机制，特别是其在巨噬细胞内的生存以及对宿主细胞信号通路，特别是吞噬体-溶酶体融合的抑制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 5-day history of fever, cough, shortness of breath, and myalgias. He recently returned from a cruise ship vacation where he spent time near the ship's air conditioning system. He has a history of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) and is a current smoker. On examination, his temperature is 39.5°C (103.1°F), respiratory rate is 28 breaths/min, and oxygen saturation is 90% on room air. Chest X-ray reveals a right lower lobe consolidation. Sputum Gram stain shows numerous gram-negative rods, some appearing intracellular within alveolar macrophages. Sputum culture is pending. The patient is started on empiric antibiotics, including a macrolide. Laboratory tests show hyponatremia (serum sodium 128 mEq/L) and elevated liver enzymes. Urine antigen testing for *Legionella pneumophila* serogroup 1 is positive.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the intracellular survival of *Legionella pneumophila* within alveolar macrophages?

(A) Production of a capsule that inhibits phagosome maturation.
(B) Inhibition of phagosome-lysosome fusion via modification of the phagosome membrane.
(C) Secretion of a toxin that induces apoptosis of the host macrophage.
(D) Replication within the cytoplasm, avoiding phagosome entrapment altogether.
(E) Production of a siderophore that sequesters iron, preventing host cell defenses.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male, smoker, COPD - risk factors for severe pneumonia.
- **Exposure:** Cruise ship, near air conditioning system - classic source for *Legionella* outbreaks (contaminated water systems).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, cough, shortness of breath, myalgias - typical pneumonia symptoms.
- **Physical Exam:** Fever, tachypnea, hypoxia - signs of severe respiratory infection.
- **Chest X-ray:** Right lower lobe consolidation - consistent with pneumonia.
- **Sputum Gram Stain:** Gram-negative rods, some intracellular - points towards an intracellular bacterial pathogen.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Hyponatremia, elevated liver enzymes - common extrapulmonary manifestations of Legionnaires' disease.
- **Urine Antigen Test:** Positive for *Legionella pneumophila* serogroup 1 - confirms the diagnosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of intracellular survival.

The vignette strongly suggests Legionnaires' disease caused by *Legionella pneumophila*. The key question is how this bacterium survives inside macrophages.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- *Legionella pneumophila* is an intracellular gram-negative bacterium.
- Upon phagocytosis by alveolar macrophages, *Legionella* resides within a membrane-bound vacuole called the Legionella-containing vacuole (LCV).
- A crucial virulence mechanism is the inhibition of phagosome-lysosome fusion.
- *Legionella* injects effector proteins via a Type IV secretion system (Dot/Icm system) into the host cell cytoplasm.
- One key effector protein is AnxA (Ankyrin-repeat protein A), which binds to host cell proteins (like RabGTPases) and modifies the LCV membrane.
- This modification prevents the recruitment of late endosomal/lysosomal markers (like LAMPs) and fusion with lysosomes.
- The LCV thus remains a neutral, replicative niche, protected from the harsh lysosomal environment.
- This mechanism allows *Legionella* to survive and multiply within the macrophage.
- Other virulence factors include enzymes that disrupt host cell signaling pathways and modulate inflammatory responses.

##### 3. Mechanism of Action of Dot/Icm System:
- The Dot/Icm (Defective Organelle Trafficking/Intracellular Multiplication) system is a Type IV secretion system.
- It translocates over 300 effector proteins from the bacterial cytoplasm into the host cell cytoplasm.
- These effectors manipulate host cell processes, including vesicle trafficking, signal transduction, and cytoskeletal organization.
- Key effectors involved in inhibiting phagosome-lysosome fusion include AnxA, SidC, and VipA.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Production of a capsule that inhibits phagosome maturation.
    - *Incorrect*. While some bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*) use capsules to inhibit phagocytosis or phagosome maturation, *Legionella* does not possess a prominent capsule. Its primary mechanism is inhibiting phagosome-lysosome *fusion*, not maturation itself. The capsule of *S. pneumoniae* interferes with C3b deposition and opsonization, hindering phagocytosis. *M. tuberculosis* capsule (mycolic acids) inhibits phagosome-lysosome fusion.
- (C) Secretion of a toxin that induces apoptosis of the host macrophage.
    - *Incorrect*. While *Legionella* does secrete toxins and effector proteins that modulate host cell function, its primary survival strategy is intracellular replication within a modified phagosome, not inducing macrophage apoptosis. Apoptosis would release the bacteria, making them susceptible to extracellular defenses. Some bacteria like *Francisella tularensis* can induce macrophage apoptosis.
- (D) Replication within the cytoplasm, avoiding phagosome entrapment altogether.
    - *Incorrect*. *Legionella* is phagocytosed and resides within a membrane-bound vacuole (LCV). It does not replicate freely in the cytoplasm like *Chlamydia* or *Rickettsia* (although *Chlamydia* and *Rickettsia* also modify the phagosome). *Legionella* actively modifies the LCV to prevent its destruction.
- (E) Production of a siderophore that sequesters iron, preventing host cell defenses.
    - *Incorrect*. While *Legionella* requires iron for growth and possesses siderophores to acquire it from the host, this is not its primary mechanism for intracellular survival. Siderophores are common virulence factors among many bacteria (e.g., *E. coli*, *Pseudomonas*, *Staphylococcus*) but do not explain how *Legionella* avoids lysosomal killing. Iron sequestration helps bacteria grow but doesn't prevent phagosome fusion with lysosomes.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | *Legionella pneumophila*                                  | *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*                               | *Streptococcus pneumoniae*                             | *Chlamydia pneumoniae*                                  |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- |
| Intracellular Location  | Phagosome (LCV)                                           | Phagosome (modified)                                      | Phagosome                                                 | Phagosome (inclusion)                                     |
| Survival Mechanism      | Inhibits phagosome-lysosome fusion (Dot/Icm effectors)     | Inhibits phagosome-lysosome fusion (cord factor, ESAT-6)   | Capsule inhibits phagocytosis/maturation                  | Inhibits phagosome-lysosome fusion (Toxoplasma-like)      |
| Key Virulence Factor    | Dot/Icm system, AnxA, SidC                               | Cord factor, ESAT-6, Type VII secretion system             | Capsule, Pneumolysin                                       | Elementary body formation, inhibition of phagolysosome fusion |
| Gram Stain              | Gram-negative rod, often intracellular                    | Acid-fast rod, often intracellular                         | Gram-positive diplococci, often extracellular              | Gram-negative coccobacillus, obligate intracellular        |
| Typical Environment     | Water systems (cooling towers, plumbing)                  | Human lungs                                               | Human respiratory tract                                    | Human respiratory tract                                    |
| USMLE Association       | Cruise ships, contaminated water, hyponatremia, urine Ag | Granulomas, caseation necrosis, acid-fast stain            | Capsule, optochin sensitivity, bile solubility            | Atypical pneumonia, often milder than *Legionella*         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*Legionella pneumophila* survives within macrophages by inhibiting phagosome-lysosome fusion using its Dot/Icm Type IV secretion system, preventing exposure to lysosomal enzymes. This is a key mechanism for intracellular bacterial pathogens. Differentiate this from bacteria that replicate freely in the cytoplasm (*Chlamydia*, *Rickettsia*) or those whose primary virulence factor is a capsule (*S. pneumoniae*, *M. tuberculosis* - although *M. tuberculosis* also inhibits fusion). Remember the clinical association with contaminated water sources (cruise ships, cooling towers) and extrapulmonary symptoms like hyponatremia and diarrhea.

<a id='question-074'></a>

### Question 074: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 24 (foreign)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis and immune response to foreign body giant cell reaction in the context of transplantation, specifically focusing on the role of macrophages and cytokines in granuloma formation around inert foreign materials. (理解移植中异物巨细胞反应的发病机制和免疫反应，特别是巨噬细胞和细胞因子在异物周围肉芽肿形成中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male patient undergoes a total hip arthroplasty using a cobalt-chromium alloy implant. Six weeks post-surgery, he presents with localized pain, swelling, and warmth around the prosthetic joint. Radiographs show no signs of loosening or infection. Aspiration of the joint fluid reveals a high white blood cell count (15,000/µL) with a predominance of neutrophils and macrophages. Histopathological examination of a biopsy taken from the periprosthetic tissue shows a dense infiltrate of macrophages, some of which have fused to form multinucleated giant cells. Scattered lymphocytes are also present. Polarized light microscopy reveals birefringent particles consistent with wear debris from the implant. The patient's systemic inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP) are elevated. Which of the following cellular processes is MOST directly responsible for the formation of the observed giant cells and the subsequent inflammatory response?

(A) Complement-mediated lysis of the implant material leading to the release of DAMPs.
(B) T-helper cell-dependent activation of macrophages via IFN-γ secretion, promoting phagocytosis and fusion.
(C) B-cell mediated antibody production against the implant material, forming immune complexes that activate mast cells.
(D) Direct cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) killing of macrophages presenting implant antigens, leading to tissue damage.
(E) Neutrophil extracellular trap (NET) formation around the implant, trapping bacteria and exacerbating inflammation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male, 6 weeks post-total hip arthroplasty with cobalt-chromium implant. Symptoms: localized pain, swelling, warmth. No loosening/infection on X-ray.
- **Lab Findings:** Joint fluid: high WBC (15,000/µL), neutrophils & macrophages predominant. Systemic inflammation: elevated ESR/CRP.
- **Histopathology:** Dense macrophage infiltrate, multinucleated giant cells, scattered lymphocytes. Birefringent particles (wear debris).
- **Key Clues:** The presence of a foreign body (implant), the timeline (6 weeks), the inflammatory infiltrate (macrophages, giant cells, lymphocytes), elevated inflammatory markers, and the absence of overt infection point towards a foreign body reaction, specifically a chronic inflammatory response involving granuloma formation around the implant. The question asks for the *most direct* cellular process responsible for giant cell formation and the inflammatory response.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Foreign Body Reaction:** Implants trigger an innate immune response. The implant surface adsorbs proteins (opsonization), activating complement and triggering macrophage phagocytosis.
- **Macrophage Activation:** Macrophages attempt to phagocytose the foreign material. When phagocytosis is incomplete (due to size or inertness of the material), macrophages become activated.
- **Giant Cell Formation:** Activated macrophages release cytokines, including TNF-α and IL-1. These cytokines recruit more macrophages and lymphocytes. Crucially, IFN-γ, primarily produced by T-helper cells (Th1 type) recognizing antigens presented by macrophages (or potentially reacting to the foreign material itself), is a potent activator of macrophages. IFN-γ promotes macrophage fusion, leading to the formation of multinucleated giant cells (foreign body giant cells). These giant cells are an attempt to engulf larger particles of the foreign material.
- **Granuloma Formation:** The collection of activated macrophages, giant cells, lymphocytes, and fibroblasts forms a granuloma, which attempts to wall off the foreign material.
- **Wear Debris:** The birefringent particles represent wear debris from the implant, which further contributes to the chronic inflammatory response by acting as a persistent irritant and antigen source.
- **Option B Rationale:** T-helper cells (specifically Th1) are key players in chronic inflammation and granuloma formation. Their secretion of IFN-γ directly drives macrophage activation and fusion into giant cells, which is the hallmark histological finding described in the vignette. This is the most direct mechanism described among the options for the observed giant cell formation and associated inflammation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Complement activation does occur on implant surfaces, but its primary role is opsonization and initial macrophage recruitment. While complement activation can lead to inflammation, it is not the *most direct* cause of giant cell formation. DAMPs are typically released from damaged host cells, not directly from inert implant materials, although wear debris can act as a persistent irritant.
- (C): B-cell mediated antibody production can occur against implant materials, but this is generally considered less important than the innate and cell-mediated responses in the initial foreign body reaction. Immune complex formation and mast cell activation are more characteristic of Type III hypersensitivity reactions, which are not the primary mechanism here. While antibodies might play a role later, the acute/subacute reaction described is dominated by macrophages and T cells.
- (D): CTLs are involved in killing infected or cancerous cells. They are not the primary drivers of foreign body giant cell formation. While some T cells are present, their role is more likely related to Th1-mediated macrophage activation rather than direct CTL killing of macrophages. Killing macrophages would likely *reduce* inflammation, not cause giant cell formation.
- (E): NETs are primarily formed by neutrophils to trap pathogens. While neutrophils are present in the joint fluid, the dominant cell type in the periprosthetic tissue biopsy is macrophages, and giant cell formation is a macrophage-specific process. NETs are not directly involved in forming giant cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Foreign Body Reaction (Option B)                                  | Complement Activation (Option A)                               | Antibody/Mast Cell (Option C)                             | CTL Killing (Option D)                                  | NET Formation (Option E)                                |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Cells**    | Macrophages, T-helper cells (Th1)                                  | Macrophages, Complement proteins                               | B cells, Mast cells                                       | Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs)                            | Neutrophils                                               |
| **Key Cytokine**     | IFN-γ                                                              | C3a, C5a (indirectly)                                           | IgE (if mast cells involved)                              | IFN-γ, TNF-α (released by CTLs, but not primary mechanism) | -                                                         |
| **Key Event**        | Macrophage activation & fusion into giant cells, granuloma formation | Opsonization, initial inflammation, recruitment               | Immune complex formation, mast cell degranulation          | Killing of target cells                                   | Extracellular DNA release to trap microbes                |
| **Histology**        | Giant cells, macrophages, lymphocytes, granuloma                   | -                                                               | -                                                         | -                                                         | -                                                         |
| **Relevance to Case** | Directly explains giant cell formation and chronic inflammation    | Contributes to initial response, not giant cells directly       | Less prominent in typical foreign body reaction            | Not the primary mechanism for giant cells                 | Not the primary mechanism for giant cells                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The formation of foreign body giant cells is a hallmark of the chronic inflammatory response to inert foreign materials. This process is primarily driven by macrophage activation, particularly through IFN-γ secreted by T-helper cells (Th1 response). This contrasts with granulomas formed in response to persistent infections (e.g., tuberculosis, sarcoidosis) where the antigen is microbial, or immune complex-mediated reactions. Key differentials include infection (would show bacteria/fungi, different histology), aseptic loosening (no significant inflammation), and hypersensitivity reactions (different cell types/mediators).

<a id='question-075'></a>

### Question 075: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 25 (determined)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of action of calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) in preventing T-cell activation following transplantation, specifically their effect on intracellular signaling pathways involving calcineurin, NFAT, and IL-2 transcription. (理解钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素、他克莫司）在移植后预防T细胞活化作用的机制，特别是它们对涉及钙调神经磷酸酶、NFAT和IL-2转录的细胞内信号通路的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male patient undergoes a kidney transplant due to end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy. Post-transplant, he is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three months later, he presents with symptoms suggestive of a viral infection: fever, malaise, and a diffuse maculopapular rash. Laboratory tests reveal leukopenia and elevated liver enzymes. A skin biopsy shows atypical keratinocytes with large, intranuclear inclusions (Cowdry type A bodies). Viral PCR confirms the presence of Cytomegalovirus (CMV). The patient's immunosuppression is temporarily reduced, and antiviral therapy (ganciclovir) is initiated. The physician reviews the patient's immunosuppressive regimen and notes that cyclosporine is a key component. Cyclosporine exerts its immunosuppressive effect by inhibiting which of the following intracellular signaling pathways?

(A) JAK-STAT pathway, preventing phosphorylation of STAT proteins required for cytokine receptor signaling.
(B) NF-κB pathway, blocking the degradation of IκB and subsequent nuclear translocation of NF-κB.
(C) Calcineurin-NFAT pathway, preventing the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT required for IL-2 transcription.
(D) MAPK pathway, inhibiting the activation of MAP kinases involved in T-cell proliferation and differentiation.
(E) mTOR pathway, blocking the phosphorylation of S6 kinase and 4E-BP1, thereby inhibiting protein synthesis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient (immunosuppressed).
- **Immunosuppression:** Cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone.
- **Presentation:** Symptoms of viral infection (fever, malaise, rash), leukopenia, elevated liver enzymes, skin biopsy showing Cowdry type A inclusions.
- **Diagnosis:** Confirmed CMV infection, a common opportunistic infection in transplant recipients due to immunosuppression.
- **Question Focus:** Mechanism of action of cyclosporine, a key immunosuppressant in the regimen.
- **Key Clue:** The question asks specifically about the intracellular signaling pathway inhibited by cyclosporine.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Activation:** T-cell activation requires two signals. Signal 1 is TCR engagement with MHC-peptide complex, leading to activation of intracellular tyrosine kinases (Lck, Zap70) and ultimately the activation of the transcription factor NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells). Signal 2 involves costimulatory molecules (e.g., CD28-B7).
- **Calcineurin Pathway:** Signal 1 triggers an increase in intracellular calcium (Ca2+) levels. This Ca2+ binds to calmodulin. The Ca2+-calmodulin complex activates calcineurin, a calcium-dependent phosphatase.
- **NFAT Dephosphorylation & Translocation:** Activated calcineurin dephosphorylates NFAT (specifically NFATc3). In its phosphorylated state, NFAT resides in the cytoplasm. Dephosphorylation allows NFAT to translocate to the nucleus.
- **IL-2 Transcription:** In the nucleus, NFAT binds to the promoter region of the IL-2 gene (and other cytokine genes like IL-4, TNF-α, IFN-γ) along with other transcription factors (e.g., AP-1). This complex initiates the transcription of IL-2 mRNA.
- **IL-2 Function:** IL-2 is a crucial cytokine for T-cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival. It acts in an autocrine and paracrine manner, binding to the IL-2 receptor (IL-2R) on T-cells.
- **Cyclosporine Mechanism:** Cyclosporine binds to an intracellular protein called cyclophilin (an immunophilin). This complex (cyclosporine-cyclophilin) then binds to and inhibits calcineurin. By inhibiting calcineurin, cyclosporine prevents the dephosphorylation of NFAT. NFAT remains in the cytoplasm, cannot translocate to the nucleus, and therefore cannot initiate IL-2 transcription. This blocks T-cell activation and proliferation.
- **CMV Infection Context:** The patient's CMV infection highlights the consequence of immunosuppression. By blocking T-cell activation, cyclosporine prevents the immune system from effectively controlling CMV replication.
- **Other Immunosuppressants:**
    - Mycophenolate mofetil inhibits inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMPDH), blocking de novo purine synthesis, which is crucial for lymphocyte proliferation.
    - Prednisone is a corticosteroid that has broad anti-inflammatory and immunosuppressive effects, including inhibiting NF-κB activation and cytokine production.
    - Tacrolimus (another calcineurin inhibitor) works similarly to cyclosporine but binds to FKBP12 instead of cyclophilin to inhibit calcineurin.
    - Sirolimus (rapamycin) inhibits mTOR, blocking T-cell proliferation in response to IL-2 and other growth factors.

##### 3. Option Analysis (选项分析)
- **(A) JAK-STAT pathway:** This pathway is activated by many cytokines (e.g., interferons, interleukins) and growth factors. JAK inhibitors (e.g., tofacitinib) block this pathway. Cyclosporine does not primarily target JAK-STAT.
- **(B) NF-κB pathway:** This pathway is involved in inflammation and immune responses, often activated by TLRs and TNF-α. Corticosteroids inhibit NF-κB activation. Cyclosporine does not primarily target NF-κB.
- **(C) Calcineurin-NFAT pathway:** This is the correct pathway. Cyclosporine inhibits calcineurin, preventing NFAT dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation, thus blocking IL-2 transcription and T-cell activation.
- **(D) MAPK pathway:** Mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathways are involved in cell proliferation, differentiation, and stress responses. While T-cell activation involves MAPK signaling, cyclosporine's primary target is not the MAPK pathway itself.
- **(E) mTOR pathway:** The mammalian target of rapamycin (mTOR) pathway is involved in cell growth, proliferation, and survival, particularly in response to growth factors and nutrients. Sirolimus (rapamycin) inhibits mTOR. Cyclosporine does not primarily target mTOR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) JAK-STAT pathway:** Incorrect. While important for cytokine signaling, this pathway is not the primary target of cyclosporine. JAK inhibitors are a different class of immunosuppressants. Test-takers might confuse the general concept of intracellular signaling pathways involved in immunity.
- **(B) NF-κB pathway:** Incorrect. NF-κB is crucial for inflammation and cytokine gene expression, but its inhibition is a primary mechanism of corticosteroids, not cyclosporine. Test-takers might confuse the mechanisms of different immunosuppressants.
- **(D) MAPK pathway:** Incorrect. MAPK pathways are involved in T-cell activation, but cyclosporine acts upstream of this pathway by blocking the initial signal transduction cascade involving calcineurin and NFAT. Test-takers might focus on the downstream effects of T-cell activation without pinpointing cyclosporine's specific target.
- **(E) mTOR pathway:** Incorrect. mTOR is involved in cell growth and proliferation and is the target of sirolimus (rapamycin), another immunosuppressant. Test-takers might confuse the targets of different immunosuppressive drugs used in transplantation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Cyclosporine Mechanism                                  | Sirolimus Mechanism                                     | Corticosteroids Mechanism                             | JAK Inhibitors Mechanism                             |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Target**   | Calcineurin                                            | mTOR                                                     | NF-κB (indirectly), broad anti-inflammatory effects | JAK kinases                                           |
| **Intracellular Protein** | Cyclophilin                                           | FKBP12                                                   | Glucocorticoid receptor                               | JAK family kinases                                     |
| **Key Pathway Blocked** | Calcineurin-NFAT (IL-2 transcription)                  | mTOR (protein synthesis, cell cycle progression)         | NF-κB (transcription factor activation)                | JAK-STAT (cytokine signaling)                         |
| **Effect on T-cells** | Blocks activation and proliferation (IL-2 dependent) | Blocks proliferation (IL-2 dependent)                   | Broad immunosuppression, anti-inflammatory           | Blocks cytokine signaling, inhibits T-cell activation |
| **Clinical Use**     | Transplant rejection prevention, autoimmune diseases   | Transplant rejection prevention, drug-eluting stents    | Autoimmune diseases, inflammation, transplant        | Rheumatoid arthritis, myeloproliferative neoplasms    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cyclosporine is a calcineurin inhibitor that prevents T-cell activation by blocking the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT, thereby inhibiting IL-2 transcription. This mechanism is distinct from other immunosuppressants like sirolimus (mTOR inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), and corticosteroids (NF-κB inhibitor). Understanding the specific intracellular targets of these drugs is crucial for managing transplant patients and recognizing potential side effects or drug interactions. Key differentials include CMV infection (opportunistic infection due to immunosuppression) and distinguishing the mechanisms of action of various immunosuppressants.

<a id='question-076'></a>

### Question 076: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 26 (extremely)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of action of calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) in preventing T-cell activation following transplantation, specifically their effect on intracellular signaling pathways and cytokine production. (理解钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素、他克莫司）在移植后预防T细胞活化作用的机制，特别是它们对细胞内信号通路和细胞因子产生的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. Post-transplant, he is started on a regimen of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone to prevent acute rejection. Three months after transplantation, the patient presents with a 2-week history of worsening fatigue, fever (38.5°C), and a non-productive cough. Physical examination reveals diffuse bilateral crackles on lung auscultation. Chest X-ray shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis reveals numerous fungal hyphae with septate, branching morphology, consistent with *Aspergillus* species. The patient's immunosuppressive medications are temporarily held. Cultures of BAL fluid grow *Aspergillus fumigatus*. The patient is subsequently treated with voriconazole and gradually improves. Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains why this patient was susceptible to this opportunistic fungal infection?

(A) Tacrolimus inhibits the production of interleukin-2 (IL-2) by blocking the activation of the nuclear factor of activated T-cells (NFAT) transcription factor, thereby impairing cell-mediated immunity against intracellular pathogens.
(B) Mycophenolate mofetil inhibits inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMPDH), leading to decreased production of guanosine nucleotides required for lymphocyte proliferation, thus impairing humoral immunity against extracellular bacteria.
(C) Prednisone, a glucocorticoid, suppresses inflammation by inhibiting the production of pro-inflammatory cytokines like TNF-α and IL-1β, thereby increasing susceptibility to viral infections.
(D) Tacrolimus inhibits the activation of the mammalian target of rapamycin (mTOR) pathway, leading to decreased protein synthesis and impaired B-cell function, increasing susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.
(E) Mycophenolate mofetil inhibits dihydroorotate dehydrogenase (DHODH), leading to decreased pyrimidine synthesis and impaired T-cell function, increasing susceptibility to protozoal infections.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient (immunosuppressed).
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone.
- **Presentation:** Fever, cough, fatigue, bilateral crackles, diffuse interstitial infiltrates on CXR (suggestive of pneumonia).
- **Diagnosis:** *Aspergillus fumigatus* infection confirmed by BAL fluid analysis and culture. *Aspergillus* is an opportunistic mold.
- **Question:** Mechanism explaining susceptibility to this *specific* opportunistic fungal infection in the context of the given immunosuppression regimen.
- **Key Clue:** The question asks for the mechanism related to the *specific* immunosuppressants and the *specific* pathogen (*Aspergillus*). *Aspergillus* is primarily controlled by cell-mediated immunity (T-cells, macrophages). Tacrolimus is a potent inhibitor of T-cell activation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor. It binds to an intracellular protein (FKBP12). This complex then inhibits calcineurin, a calcium-dependent phosphatase. Calcineurin normally dephosphorylates NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells). Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus and acts as a transcription factor, promoting the expression of genes encoding cytokines, particularly IL-2. By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus prevents NFAT dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation, thus blocking IL-2 production and subsequent T-cell proliferation and activation. This severely impairs cell-mediated immunity.
- ***Aspergillus* Pathogenesis:** *Aspergillus* is an environmental mold. In immunocompetent individuals, it is usually cleared by phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) and T-cell mediated immunity. In severely immunocompromised patients (e.g., transplant recipients on potent immunosuppression), *Aspergillus* can cause invasive disease, often manifesting as pulmonary aspergillosis. The primary defense against *Aspergillus* relies heavily on functional T-cells (especially Th1 and cytotoxic T-cells) and activated macrophages.
- **Linking Tacrolimus and *Aspergillus*:** Tacrolimus's potent inhibition of T-cell activation directly compromises the host's ability to control *Aspergillus* infection, making the patient highly susceptible. The vignette describes a classic opportunistic infection in a transplant patient on tacrolimus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** Tacrolimus inhibits calcineurin, preventing NFAT activation and IL-2 production. IL-2 is crucial for T-cell proliferation and activation, which are essential for controlling intracellular pathogens like *Aspergillus*. This mechanism directly explains the patient's susceptibility.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Mycophenolate mofetil inhibits IMPDH, primarily affecting lymphocyte proliferation (both T and B cells) by depleting guanine nucleotides. While it contributes to immunosuppression, its primary effect is on proliferation, not specifically the IL-2 pathway targeted by tacrolimus, which is most critical for defense against *Aspergillus*. Also, it primarily impairs humoral immunity (B-cells) and lymphocyte proliferation, not specifically the T-cell activation pathway most relevant to *Aspergillus* control.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Prednisone is a glucocorticoid that broadly suppresses inflammation and immune responses by inhibiting cytokine production (TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6) and lymphocyte function. While it increases susceptibility to infections, including viral infections, it doesn't specifically target the IL-2/NFAT pathway like tacrolimus. The primary mechanism described here is more related to general anti-inflammatory effects rather than the specific T-cell activation block crucial for *Aspergillus* defense.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Tacrolimus inhibits calcineurin, not mTOR. mTOR inhibitors (like sirolimus/rapamycin) inhibit mTOR, affecting T-cell proliferation and B-cell function. Tacrolimus's primary target is calcineurin/NFAT/IL-2. While mTOR inhibition also causes immunosuppression, it's not the mechanism of tacrolimus. Furthermore, impaired B-cell function is less relevant to *Aspergillus* defense than impaired T-cell function.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Mycophenolate mofetil inhibits IMPDH, not DHODH. DHODH inhibition is the mechanism of leflunomide. Mycophenolate affects guanine nucleotide synthesis, impacting lymphocyte proliferation. While it impairs T-cell function, the specific mechanism described (DHODH inhibition) is incorrect, and the primary mechanism of tacrolimus (NFAT/IL-2 inhibition) is more directly linked to susceptibility to *Aspergillus*.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Tacrolimus Mechanism (Correct)                                  | Mycophenolate Mofetil Mechanism (Distractor B)                  | Prednisone Mechanism (Distractor C)                               | Sirolimus Mechanism (Distractor D)                               | Leflunomide Mechanism (Distractor E)                             |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Target Enzyme**    | Calcineurin (Phosphatase)                                        | IMPDH (Enzyme in guanine nucleotide synthesis)                   | Broad (multiple cytokine pathways)                               | mTOR (Kinase)                                                      | DHODH (Enzyme in pyrimidine synthesis)                            |
| **Key Pathway**      | NFAT dephosphorylation -> NFAT nuclear translocation -> IL-2 transcription | Guanine nucleotide depletion -> Impaired lymphocyte proliferation | Inhibition of pro-inflammatory cytokine production & lymphocyte function | Inhibition of cell cycle progression (G1 to S phase) & protein synthesis | Pyrimidine depletion -> Impaired lymphocyte proliferation         |
| **Primary Effect**   | Blocks T-cell activation & IL-2 production                       | Inhibits lymphocyte proliferation (T & B cells)                   | Broad immunosuppression & anti-inflammation                       | Inhibits T-cell proliferation & B-cell function                  | Inhibits lymphocyte proliferation (T & B cells)                   |
| **Immune Cell Impact** | Primarily T-cells (especially activation & IL-2 production)       | Primarily lymphocytes (proliferation)                            | Broad (lymphocytes, macrophages, etc.)                             | Primarily T-cells & B-cells (proliferation)                       | Primarily lymphocytes (proliferation)                            |
| **Susceptibility**   | Opportunistic infections requiring cell-mediated immunity (*Aspergillus*, CMV, PJP) | Opportunistic infections requiring lymphocyte proliferation (CMV, EBV) | Broad range, including viral, bacterial, fungal infections        | Opportunistic infections requiring cell-mediated immunity (CMV, PJP) | Opportunistic infections requiring lymphocyte proliferation (CMV, EBV) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tacrolimus (and cyclosporine) are calcineurin inhibitors that block T-cell activation by preventing NFAT nuclear translocation and IL-2 production. This mechanism specifically impairs cell-mediated immunity, making patients susceptible to opportunistic infections like *Aspergillus*, Pneumocystis jirovecii (PJP), and CMV. Remember the key pathway: Tacrolimus -> Calcineurin Inhibition -> NFAT Dephosphorylation Block -> IL-2 Production Block -> Impaired T-cell Activation. Differentiate this from mycophenolate (IMPDH inhibitor, affects proliferation) and sirolimus (mTOR inhibitor, affects proliferation).

<a id='question-077'></a>

### Question 077: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 27 (polymorphic)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying chronic rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of alloantibodies targeting donor HLA antigens and the resulting endothelial cell activation and damage, leading to transplant vasculopathy. (理解实体器官移植中慢性排斥的免疫机制，特别是针对供体HLA抗原的同种抗体的作用以及由此导致的内皮细胞激活和损伤，最终导致移植血管病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male received a kidney transplant from a deceased donor 7 years ago for end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy. The initial post-transplant course was uneventful, with stable graft function and normal serum creatinine levels. Over the past 2 years, his serum creatinine has gradually increased from 1.2 mg/dL to 2.8 mg/dL. A recent renal biopsy reveals interstitial fibrosis, tubular atrophy, and significant arteriolar hyalinosis and glomerulopathy. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries and glomeruli, and electron microscopy reveals double contours along the glomerular basement membrane. Serum analysis shows elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I and class II antigens. The patient's current immunosuppressive regimen includes a calcineurin inhibitor, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the progressive graft dysfunction observed in this patient?

(A) T-cell mediated acute rejection involving cytotoxic CD8+ T lymphocytes directly attacking donor tubular epithelial cells.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection characterized by complement activation and endothelial cell injury due to DSA binding to donor HLA antigens on the graft endothelium.
(C) Chronic rejection driven by the accumulation of non-specific inflammatory cells leading to fibrosis and scarring without specific antibody or T-cell involvement.
(D) Graft-versus-host disease where donor T cells attack the recipient's tissues, causing widespread inflammation and organ damage.
(E) Acute tubular necrosis caused by ischemia-reperfusion injury during the initial transplant procedure, leading to delayed graft function and eventual chronic damage.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient 7 years ago for diabetic nephropathy. This establishes the context of chronic allograft dysfunction.
- **Timeline:** Gradual increase in serum creatinine over 2 years, indicating a chronic process rather than an acute event.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial fibrosis, tubular atrophy, arteriolar hyalinosis, glomerulopathy. These are classic features of chronic allograft nephropathy (CAN).
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries and glomeruli. C4d is a marker of complement activation, specifically the classical pathway, strongly suggesting antibody-mediated injury.
- **Electron Microscopy:** Double contours along the glomerular basement membrane. This is another sign of immune complex deposition and complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection.
- **Serology:** Elevated donor-specific antibodies (DSA) against HLA class I and class II antigens. This confirms the presence of antibodies targeting the donor graft.
- **Immunosuppression:** Patient is on standard triple therapy (CNI, MMF, prednisone), suggesting the rejection process is overcoming immunosuppression.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for progressive graft dysfunction.

These clues collectively point towards chronic antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR) as the underlying cause of the patient's worsening kidney function. The presence of DSA, C4d deposition, double contours, and histological features of CAN are hallmarks of this process.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Antibody-Mediated Rejection (CAMR):** This is a major cause of late allograft loss. It is characterized by the development of DSAs against donor HLA antigens. These antibodies bind to the graft endothelium, activating the classical complement pathway (leading to C3 convertase formation and C4d deposition) and triggering Fc receptor-mediated effector functions on immune cells (e.g., NK cells, macrophages).
- **Mechanism:** DSA binding to donor HLA class I and II molecules on endothelial cells leads to endothelial cell activation, proliferation, and apoptosis. Complement activation generates anaphylatoxins (C3a, C5a) and the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9), causing endothelial damage. This damage leads to microvascular inflammation (peritubular capillaritis, glomerulitis), thrombosis, and ultimately, transplant vasculopathy (arteriolar hyalinosis, glomerular sclerosis). The resulting ischemia and inflammation contribute to interstitial fibrosis and tubular atrophy.
- **Key Players:** DSAs (IgG antibodies against donor HLA), Complement (classical pathway activation, C4d deposition, MAC formation), Endothelial cells (target of injury), Immune cells (NK cells, macrophages recruited by complement and Fc receptors).
- **Histopathology:** Glomerulopathy, peritubular capillaritis, C4d deposition, double contours, transplant vasculopathy (arteriolar hyalinosis).
- **Relevance to Vignette:** The patient's clinical presentation (late onset, gradual decline), biopsy findings (fibrosis, atrophy, hyalinosis, glomerulopathy, C4d, double contours), and serology (elevated DSA) are all consistent with CAMR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **T-cell mediated acute rejection:** This typically occurs within the first few months post-transplant and is characterized by T-cell infiltration (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells attacking tubular cells, CD4+ helper T cells orchestrating the response) and acute tubular injury. While T-cells can contribute to chronic rejection, the prominent features here (DSA, C4d, double contours, vasculopathy) strongly point to an antibody-mediated process, not primarily acute T-cell mediated rejection. The timeline (7 years post-transplant) also makes acute rejection less likely as the primary cause of *progressive* dysfunction over 2 years.
- (C): **Chronic rejection driven by non-specific inflammation:** While chronic inflammation and fibrosis are present, this option incorrectly suggests it occurs *without* specific antibody or T-cell involvement. The presence of DSA and C4d deposition clearly indicates a specific immune response targeting the graft. Chronic rejection is often a complex process involving both cellular and humoral immunity, but the specific markers in this case point strongly towards antibody mediation.
- (D): **Graft-versus-host disease (GVHD):** This occurs when immunocompetent donor T cells (in a bone marrow or hematopoietic stem cell transplant) recognize the recipient's tissues as foreign and attack them. It does not occur in solid organ transplantation where the donor organ is immunologically immature or suppressed. The patient received a kidney transplant, not a stem cell transplant.
- (E): **Acute tubular necrosis (ATN) due to ischemia-reperfusion injury:** This is a common complication occurring immediately after transplantation due to warm/cold ischemia and reperfusion. While it can cause delayed graft function, it typically resolves or leads to early graft failure, not progressive dysfunction developing over 7 years with features of chronic rejection like vasculopathy and C4d deposition. The biopsy findings are also inconsistent with ATN (which shows tubular cell necrosis and sloughing, not primarily fibrosis, hyalinosis, or C4d).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Antibody-Mediated Rejection (CAMR) | Acute T-cell Mediated Rejection | Chronic T-cell Mediated Rejection | GVHD                     | Ischemia-Reperfusion Injury |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :--------------------------- |
| **Timing**              | Months to years post-transplant             | Days to months post-transplant  | Months to years post-transplant   | Post-HSCT               | Immediately post-transplant  |
| **Primary Mediator**    | DSAs (IgG)                                 | CD8+ T cells                    | CD4+ & CD8+ T cells               | Donor T cells            | Ischemia/Reperfusion         |
| **Key Histology**       | Vasculopathy, C4d deposition, double contours | Tubulitis, interstitial infiltrate | Interstitial fibrosis, fibrosis   | Skin rash, GI symptoms | Tubular necrosis, sloughing  |
| **Serology**            | Elevated DSA                               | Usually negative                | Variable                          | N/A                     | N/A                         |
| **Complement Activation** | Yes (C4d+)                                 | No                              | Variable                          | N/A                     | No                          |
| **Transplant Type**     | Solid Organ                                | Solid Organ                    | Solid Organ                      | Hematopoietic Stem Cell | Solid Organ                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic antibody-mediated rejection (CAMR) is a major cause of late solid organ graft failure, driven by DSAs targeting donor HLA antigens. Key diagnostic features include rising creatinine, histological evidence of transplant vasculopathy and glomerulopathy, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries/glomeruli, and the presence of DSAs. Differentiating CAMR from other forms of chronic rejection (e.g., T-cell mediated) or acute rejection requires careful integration of clinical, histological, and serological data. Remember that C4d deposition is a marker of complement activation, strongly suggesting an antibody-mediated process.

<a id='question-078'></a>

### Question 078: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 28 (histocompatibility)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of HLA class I and class II molecules in allogeneic transplant rejection, specifically the difference in their expression patterns and the types of T cells they activate, leading to acute cellular rejection. (理解 HLA I 类和 II 类分子在异体移植排斥反应中的作用，特别是它们表达模式的差异以及它们激活的 T 细胞类型，导致急性细胞排斥反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from an unrelated deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen is negative. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, and a significant increase in serum creatinine from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy is performed. Histopathology reveals diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltration, and focal tubular injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class II molecules on endothelial cells.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class I molecules on endothelial cells.
(C) T-cell mediated rejection involving CD4+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class II molecules on tubular epithelial cells.
(D) T-cell mediated rejection involving CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I molecules on tubular epithelial cells.
(E) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to ABO blood group antigens on endothelial cells.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, received kidney transplant from unrelated deceased donor. This sets the stage for potential alloreactivity due to HLA mismatch.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone) is used, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, either T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (ABMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, increased creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Histopathology:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltration, focal tubular injury. These findings are characteristic of acute cellular rejection (TCMR).
    - **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in ABMR, but can also be present in some forms of TCMR, particularly those involving endothelial injury or overlap syndromes. However, the predominant histological findings point towards TCMR.
    - **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells. This strongly suggests a cellular immune response is involved.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    - **Acute Cellular Rejection (TCMR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor HLA antigens.
        - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize HLA class II molecules, primarily expressed on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, and B cells, but also on some epithelial cells. They orchestrate the immune response by releasing cytokines and activating other immune cells.
        - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize HLA class I molecules, expressed on virtually all nucleated cells, including tubular epithelial cells. They directly kill target cells expressing foreign HLA class I antigens.
    - **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (ABMR):** Mediated by pre-formed or de novo donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells, activating complement and leading to endothelial injury, inflammation, and thrombosis.
        - **HLA Class I:** Expressed on endothelial cells. DSAs against HLA class I can cause ABMR.
        - **HLA Class II:** Expressed on endothelial cells, particularly under inflammatory conditions. DSAs against HLA class II can also cause ABMR.
        - **ABO Antibodies:** Pre-formed antibodies against ABO blood group antigens can cause hyperacute or acute ABMR.
- **Histopathology & Immunofluorescence:** The biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular injury) are typical of TCMR. C4d deposition suggests complement activation, which is a hallmark of ABMR, but can be seen in TCMR involving endothelialitis or overlap syndromes. The increase in CD8+ T cells points strongly towards a CD8+ T cell-mediated process.
- **Mechanism Synthesis:** The clinical presentation (3 weeks post-transplant), biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular injury), and peripheral blood analysis (increased CD8+ T cells) are most consistent with acute T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR). Specifically, CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognize foreign HLA class I molecules expressed on the graft parenchymal cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells) and directly mediate cell killing, leading to tubular injury and graft dysfunction. HLA class I molecules are expressed on almost all nucleated cells, making them targets for CD8+ T cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class II molecules on endothelial cells. This describes a form of ABMR. While C4d deposition can be seen, the predominant histological findings (lymphocytic infiltration, tubular injury) and the marked increase in CD8+ T cells are more characteristic of TCMR. ABMR typically involves more prominent endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis).
- (B) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class I molecules on endothelial cells. This also describes a form of ABMR. Similar to (A), the clinical and histological picture is more suggestive of TCMR.
- (C) T-cell mediated rejection involving CD4+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class II molecules on tubular epithelial cells. While CD4+ T cells play a role in TCMR, they primarily recognize HLA class II on APCs. Tubular epithelial cells express low levels of HLA class II under normal conditions. The marked increase in CD8+ T cells and the direct tubular injury strongly implicate CD8+ T cells recognizing HLA class I on tubular cells. CD4+ T cells contribute more to orchestrating the overall response and causing interstitial inflammation.
- (E) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to ABO blood group antigens on endothelial cells. This would typically cause hyperacute or very early acute rejection, often occurring within minutes to hours of transplantation if pre-formed antibodies are present. The patient presented 3 weeks post-transplant, making ABO incompatibility highly unlikely as the primary cause, especially since the vignette doesn't mention ABO mismatch or hyperacute rejection symptoms.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (TCMR) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (ABMR) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator** | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)       | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSA)          |
| **Target Antigen**   | HLA Class I & II                | HLA Class I & II, ABO, MICA/B             |
| **Target Cell**      | Parenchymal cells (tubules), Endothelium | Endothelial cells                       |
| **Key Histology**    | Lymphocytic infiltrate, Tubulitis | Endothelialitis, Glomerulitis, Capillaritis |
| **Key Immunofluorescence** | C4d (sometimes), CD3, CD4, CD8 | C4d (prominent)                         |
| **Typical Timing**   | Days to weeks post-transplant    | Days to weeks post-transplant             |
| **CD8+ T cells**     | Increased                       | Not typically increased                   |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Variable                        | Common                                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute kidney transplant rejection can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (ABMR). TCMR is characterized by T-cell infiltration, often with increased CD8+ cells targeting HLA class I on parenchymal cells (tubules). ABMR involves DSAs targeting HLA or other antigens on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation (C4d deposition) and endothelial injury. The vignette's combination of lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular injury, and increased CD8+ T cells points most strongly to CD8+ T cell-mediated rejection targeting HLA class I on tubular epithelial cells. Differentiate TCMR from ABMR based on histology (tubulitis vs. endothelialitis), immunofluorescence (C4d presence/intensity), and lymphocyte subsets.
