<a id='question-085'></a>

### Question 085: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 35 (complete)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of action of calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) in preventing T-cell activation following transplantation, specifically their effect on intracellular signaling pathways and cytokine production. (理解钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素、他克莫司）在移植后预防T细胞活化的作用机制，特别是它们对细胞内信号通路和细胞因子产生的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. Post-transplant, he is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three months after transplantation, the patient presents with symptoms of acute rejection, including fever, flank pain, and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals acute cellular rejection characterized by interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltration, and tubular epithelial cell injury. His serum creatinine has increased from a baseline of 1.8 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL. Laboratory tests show normal serum levels of cyclosporine. The transplant team decides to increase the dose of cyclosporine. Which of the following intracellular signaling pathways is MOST directly inhibited by cyclosporine, leading to its immunosuppressive effect?

(A) Activation of the mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathway via Ras.
(B) Phosphorylation of the transcription factor NF-κB.
(C) Dephosphorylation of the nuclear factor of activated T-cells (NFAT).
(D) Activation of the phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K)/Akt pathway.
(E) Binding of the T-cell receptor (TCR) complex to the ZAP-70 kinase.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the context for immunosuppression and potential rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Receiving cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Cyclosporine is a key immunosuppressant.
- **Clinical Signs:** Symptoms of acute rejection (fever, flank pain, decreased urine output) and rising creatinine indicate graft dysfunction.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Acute cellular rejection confirms the clinical suspicion.
- **Cyclosporine Levels:** Normal levels suggest the rejection is not due to subtherapeutic drug levels, implying either resistance or a need for increased dosing due to increased T-cell activation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most direct* intracellular signaling pathway inhibited by cyclosporine. This requires knowledge of cyclosporine's mechanism of action at the molecular level.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Activation:** T-cell activation begins with TCR engagement by peptide-MHC complexes on antigen-presenting cells (APCs). This triggers intracellular signaling cascades.
- **Calcineurin Pathway:** A crucial pathway involves the phosphatase calcineurin. TCR signaling leads to increased intracellular calcium (Ca2+). Ca2+ binds to calmodulin. The Ca2+-calmodulin complex activates calcineurin (a Ca2+-dependent phosphatase).
- **NFAT Dephosphorylation:** Activated calcineurin dephosphorylates the cytoplasmic transcription factor NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells).
- **NFAT Translocation:** Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus.
- **Cytokine Gene Transcription:** In the nucleus, NFAT binds to DNA response elements and promotes the transcription of genes encoding key T-cell cytokines, most notably Interleukin-2 (IL-2). IL-2 is essential for T-cell proliferation and differentiation.
- **Cyclosporine Mechanism:** Cyclosporine binds to an intracellular protein called cyclophilin. The cyclosporine-cyclophilin complex then binds to and inhibits calcineurin.
- **Consequence of Inhibition:** By inhibiting calcineurin, cyclosporine prevents the dephosphorylation of NFAT. Phosphorylated NFAT remains in the cytoplasm and cannot enter the nucleus.
- **Result:** This blocks the transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines, thereby suppressing T-cell activation, proliferation, and effector function, preventing graft rejection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Activation of the mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathway via Ras. The MAPK pathway (ERK, JNK, p38) is involved in T-cell activation and differentiation, but it is not the primary target of cyclosporine. Cyclosporine acts upstream of this pathway by blocking IL-2 production, which influences MAPK activation.
- (B): Phosphorylation of the transcription factor NF-κB. NF-κB is another important transcription factor involved in inflammation and immune responses. Its activation pathway involves IκB degradation and translocation to the nucleus. While NF-κB is crucial for immune responses, it is not the direct target inhibited by cyclosporine.
- (D): Activation of the phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K)/Akt pathway. The PI3K/Akt pathway is involved in cell survival, growth, and metabolism, and also plays a role in T-cell activation and function. However, it is not the primary target of cyclosporine.
- (E): Binding of the T-cell receptor (TCR) complex to the ZAP-70 kinase. ZAP-70 (Zeta-chain-associated protein kinase 70) is a tyrosine kinase associated with the TCR complex. It plays a critical role in early TCR signaling. Cyclosporine acts downstream of TCR signaling and ZAP-70 activation, specifically targeting the calcineurin-NFAT pathway.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Cyclosporine Mechanism                                  | MAPK Pathway                                     | NF-κB Pathway                                    | PI3K/Akt Pathway                                | ZAP-70 Kinase                                     |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| **Target**           | Calcineurin (via cyclophilin)                           | Ras, Raf, MEK, ERK, JNK, p38                       | IκB kinase (IKK)                                 | PI3K, Akt, mTOR                                   | TCR complex (Zeta chain)                          |
| **Effect**           | Inhibits calcineurin phosphatase activity                | Regulates cell proliferation, differentiation, apoptosis | Regulates inflammation, cell survival, proliferation | Regulates cell survival, growth, metabolism       | Initiates TCR signaling cascade                   |
| **Downstream Impact** | Prevents NFAT dephosphorylation & nuclear translocation | Involved in T-cell activation, cytokine production | Involved in cytokine production, inflammation     | Involved in T-cell survival, metabolism          | Initiates downstream signaling (PLCγ, LAT)         |
| **Relevance to Tx**  | Primary immunosuppressant, prevents T-cell activation   | Involved in T-cell function, but not direct target | Involved in immune response, but not direct target | Involved in T-cell function, but not direct target | Initiates TCR signal, but not direct target       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cyclosporine and tacrolimus are calcineurin inhibitors used to prevent organ transplant rejection. They work by blocking the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT, thereby inhibiting IL-2 production and T-cell activation. Understanding this specific intracellular mechanism is crucial. Differentiate this from other immunosuppressants like mTOR inhibitors (sirolimus, everolimus) which target the PI3K/Akt/mTOR pathway, or antimetabolites (mycophenolate mofetil, azathioprine) which inhibit DNA synthesis.

---

<a id='question-086'></a>

### Question 086: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 36 (haplotype)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of HLA haplotypes in transplantation, specifically how matching haplotypes (rather than individual alleles) can improve graft survival, and recognizing the concept of haplotype sharing between donor and recipient. (理解HLA haplotype在移植中的作用，特别是匹配 haplotype（而非单个等位基因）如何改善移植物存活率，以及识别供者和受者之间 haplotype 的共享概念。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy is scheduled for a kidney transplant. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen is negative, indicating no pre-formed antibodies against HLA antigens. Tissue typing reveals his HLA type to be A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01/DQB1*06:02/DPB1*01:01. A deceased donor kidney becomes available. The donor is a 48-year-old female who died from a motor vehicle accident. Her HLA type is A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01/DQB1*06:02/DPB1*01:01. The crossmatch test is negative. The transplant is performed successfully. Post-transplant, the patient is monitored closely. Which of the following best describes the immunological relationship between the donor and recipient in this scenario?

(A) The donor and recipient are HLA-identical at the A, B, DR, DQ, and DP loci, representing a perfect match.
(B) The donor and recipient share the same HLA haplotype, which is a common haplotype in the general population, leading to a lower risk of rejection compared to a mismatch.
(C) The donor and recipient are HLA-matched at the A, B, and DR loci, but mismatched at the DQ and DP loci, which are less critical for graft survival.
(D) The donor and recipient share the same HLA haplotype, meaning they inherited the same set of alleles on a single chromosome from a common ancestor, which is associated with better long-term graft survival than matching individual alleles across different haplotypes.
(E) The donor and recipient are HLA-matched at the A, B, DR, DQ, and DP loci, but the donor is heterozygous for the C locus (which is not provided), leading to a potential increased risk of rejection.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient needs a kidney transplant due to ESRD.
- Pre-transplant PRA is negative, meaning no pre-existing antibodies against HLA antigens.
- The patient's HLA type is given: A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01/DQB1*06:02/DPB1*01:01.
- The donor's HLA type is given: A*02:01/B*07:02/DRB1*15:01/DQB1*06:02/DPB1*01:01.
- The crossmatch is negative, indicating no pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA antigens.
- The question asks about the immunological relationship between donor and recipient.
- Comparing the patient's and donor's HLA types reveals they are identical at all listed loci (A, B, DR, DQ, DP). This means they have the same alleles at each locus.
- The key concept being tested is the difference between matching individual alleles and matching haplotypes. A haplotype refers to a set of alleles on a single chromosome that are inherited together. In this case, the donor and recipient have the *exact same* alleles at *all* listed loci. This means they inherited the same set of alleles on a single chromosome (the same haplotype) for each locus from a common ancestor. Matching haplotypes, rather than just individual alleles, is associated with better graft outcomes because it reduces the chance of minor histocompatibility antigen mismatches and potentially other unknown genetic factors linked to the haplotype.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **HLA Typing and Haplotypes:** The Human Leukocyte Antigen (HLA) system is the major histocompatibility complex (MHC) in humans. HLA genes are highly polymorphic, meaning there are many different alleles for each gene. Individuals inherit one set of HLA alleles (one chromosome) from each parent. A haplotype is a specific combination of alleles at linked loci on a single chromosome. For example, the A*02:01, B*07:02, DRB1*15:01, DQB1*06:02, and DPB1*01:01 alleles are all located close together on chromosome 6 and are often inherited together as a single haplotype block.
- **Transplantation and HLA Matching:** HLA matching is crucial for transplant success. The primary goal is to match the HLA-A, HLA-B, and HLA-DR loci, as antibodies against these antigens are most commonly associated with hyperacute and acute rejection. Matching at the DQ and DP loci can also be beneficial.
- **Haplotype vs. Allele Matching:** While matching individual alleles (e.g., matching A*02:01 to A*02:01) is important, matching entire haplotypes is even better. If the donor and recipient share the same haplotype at multiple loci, it means they inherited the same set of alleles on a single chromosome. This reduces the likelihood of mismatches at other linked loci (minor histocompatibility antigens) and potentially other genes located near the HLA region that could influence immune responses. Studies have shown that haplotype sharing is associated with improved long-term graft survival compared to matching individual alleles across different haplotypes. In this specific case, the donor and recipient are identical at all listed HLA loci, meaning they share the same haplotype for each locus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This option states they are HLA-identical at all loci, which is true based on the provided information. However, it doesn't fully capture the significance of *why* this is beneficial in terms of haplotypes. While technically correct that they are identical, option (D) provides a more nuanced and clinically relevant explanation related to the concept of haplotypes and their impact on graft survival. Option (A) is a description of the result, while (D) explains the immunological principle behind the result.
- (B): This option suggests the haplotype is common, which might be true but is irrelevant to the immunological relationship. The key point is that they *share* the same haplotype, regardless of its frequency in the population. Also, sharing a common haplotype doesn't necessarily mean a lower risk of rejection compared to a *mismatch*; it means a lower risk compared to *not* sharing the same haplotype.
- (C): This option incorrectly states they are mismatched at DQ and DP loci. The provided HLA types show they are identical at these loci. Mismatches at DQ and DP can contribute to rejection, so this statement is factually wrong based on the vignette.
- (E): This option introduces the C locus, which is not provided in the vignette, and speculates about heterozygosity, which is irrelevant given the provided information. Furthermore, even if the donor were heterozygous for the C locus, the fact that they are identical at A, B, DR, DQ, and DP loci is still highly favorable. The focus on an unmentioned locus makes this option incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Correct Answer (D) - Haplotype Sharing | Incorrect Option (A) - Allele Identity | Incorrect Option (B) - Common Haplotype | Incorrect Option (C) - DQ/DP Mismatch | Incorrect Option (E) - C Locus Heterozygosity |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| **Relationship**     | Shares same haplotype (inherited set)   | Identical alleles at each locus       | Shares a common haplotype              | Mismatched at DQ/DP                  | Donor heterozygous at C locus                 |
| **Vignette Accuracy**| Accurate description of the relationship| Accurate description of the alleles  | Irrelevant (frequency)                 | Inaccurate (types are identical)      | Irrelevant (C locus not provided)              |
| **Clinical Relevance**| Explains improved graft survival       | Describes the match, less explanatory| Irrelevant to the specific match        | Incorrectly describes the match       | Irrelevant to the specific match               |
| **Key Concept**      | Haplotype inheritance & matching       | Individual allele matching            | Haplotype frequency                   | Importance of DQ/DP matching         | C locus relevance                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding the difference between matching individual HLA alleles and matching entire HLA haplotypes is crucial in transplantation. Sharing the same haplotype implies inheriting the same set of linked alleles on a single chromosome, which is associated with better long-term graft outcomes than matching individual alleles across different haplotypes. This concept is tested frequently on Step 1. Key differentials include understanding the relative importance of different HLA loci (A, B, DR > DQ, DP > C) and the role of minor histocompatibility antigens.

<a id='question-087'></a>

### Question 087: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 37 (parent)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of action of calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) in preventing transplant rejection by inhibiting T-cell activation, specifically targeting the calcineurin pathway and subsequent NFAT dephosphorylation and translocation. (理解钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素、他克莫司）通过抑制T细胞活化来预防移植排斥反应的作用机制，具体靶向钙调神经磷酸酶途径以及随后的NFAT去磷酸化和易位。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) score is 10%, indicating low sensitization. Post-transplant, he is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he presents with fever (38.5°C), chills, and acute onset of graft dysfunction, evidenced by a rise in serum creatinine from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL and decreased urine output. Renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltrates. Viral studies are negative for CMV and EBV. Bacterial cultures of blood and urine are negative. A repeat PRA score is 20%. The patient's tacrolimus trough level is found to be subtherapeutic (3 ng/mL, target range 8-12 ng/mL). The transplant team increases the tacrolimus dose and adds intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG). The patient's renal function gradually improves over the next week. Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the therapeutic effect of increasing the tacrolimus dose in this patient?

(A) Inhibition of IL-2 production by blocking the activation of the NF-κB transcription factor.
(B) Inhibition of T-cell proliferation by preventing the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of the Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells (NFAT).
(C) Inhibition of B-cell activation and antibody production by blocking the activation of the MAPK pathway.
(D) Inhibition of macrophage activation and cytokine release by blocking the JAK-STAT signaling pathway.
(E) Inhibition of eosinophil recruitment and degranulation by blocking the activation of phospholipase C.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetic nephropathy, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Started on tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor.
- **Clinical Course:** Presents 3 weeks post-transplant with fever, chills, acute graft dysfunction (rising creatinine, decreased urine output). This is highly suggestive of acute rejection.
- **Diagnostic Findings:** Renal biopsy shows ATN and interstitial inflammation (mononuclear infiltrates), consistent with acute cellular rejection. Viral and bacterial studies are negative, ruling out common infectious causes of graft dysfunction. PRA score increased from 10% to 20%, suggesting development of donor-specific antibodies (DSA), although the biopsy points more towards cellular rejection.
- **Tacrolimus Level:** Subtherapeutic tacrolimus level (3 ng/mL). This is a critical clue indicating inadequate immunosuppression, which likely contributed to the rejection episode.
- **Treatment & Outcome:** Tacrolimus dose increased, IVIG added, and renal function improved. This confirms that improving immunosuppression (specifically tacrolimus levels) helped resolve the rejection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism by which increasing the tacrolimus dose exerts its therapeutic effect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus (also known as FK506) is a calcineurin inhibitor. It binds to an intracellular protein called FKBP12 (FK506 binding protein 12). This complex (Tacrolimus-FKBP12) then binds to and inhibits calcineurin, a calcium-dependent phosphatase.
- **Calcineurin's Role:** Calcineurin normally dephosphorylates the Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells (NFAT).
- **NFAT's Role:** Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus, where it acts as a transcription factor, binding to promoter regions of genes encoding key T-cell activation mediators, most notably Interleukin-2 (IL-2).
- **IL-2's Role:** IL-2 is a crucial cytokine for T-cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival. It acts in an autocrine and paracrine manner to amplify the T-cell response.
- **Tacrolimus Effect:** By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus prevents the dephosphorylation and subsequent nuclear translocation of NFAT. This blocks the transcription of IL-2 and other T-cell activation genes. Consequently, T-cell activation and proliferation are significantly suppressed, preventing the immune system from attacking the transplanted organ.
- **Relevance to Vignette:** The patient experienced acute rejection likely due to subtherapeutic tacrolimus levels. Increasing the dose restores the inhibition of calcineurin, prevents NFAT translocation, reduces IL-2 production, and thereby suppresses the T-cell mediated rejection response, leading to improvement in graft function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Inhibition of IL-2 production by blocking the activation of the NF-κB transcription factor. NF-κB is involved in the transcription of many inflammatory cytokines, including IL-1, TNF-α, and IL-6, but it is not the primary transcription factor regulated by the calcineurin-NFAT pathway for IL-2 production. While NF-κB is important in T-cell activation, tacrolimus's main target is the calcineurin-NFAT pathway.
- (C): Inhibition of B-cell activation and antibody production by blocking the activation of the MAPK pathway. Tacrolimus primarily targets T-cells. While it can have some indirect effects on B-cells, its main mechanism is not through inhibiting the MAPK pathway, which is involved in various cellular processes including proliferation, differentiation, and apoptosis, but is not the specific target of tacrolimus for immunosuppression. Furthermore, the vignette describes acute *cellular* rejection, characterized by T-cell infiltrates, not primarily antibody-mediated rejection.
- (D): Inhibition of macrophage activation and cytokine release by blocking the JAK-STAT signaling pathway. The JAK-STAT pathway is crucial for signaling downstream of many cytokine receptors, including those for interferons and interleukins like IL-6. While immunosuppressants like JAK inhibitors exist, tacrolimus does not primarily act by inhibiting the JAK-STAT pathway. Macrophages play a role in rejection, but the primary target of tacrolimus is T-cell activation.
- (E): Inhibition of eosinophil recruitment and degranulation by blocking the activation of phospholipase C. Phospholipase C (PLC) is involved in intracellular signaling pathways, often activated by G-protein coupled receptors, leading to the production of IP3 and DAG. This pathway is important for various cellular functions, including eosinophil activation. However, tacrolimus does not act by inhibiting PLC. Eosinophils are typically associated with allergic reactions and parasitic infections, not the primary mediators of acute cellular transplant rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Tacrolimus Mechanism                                   | NF-κB Pathway                                       | MAPK Pathway                                        | JAK-STAT Pathway                                     | Phospholipase C Pathway                             |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Target**   | T-cells                                               | Various cells (immune, endothelial, etc.)            | Various cells                                        | Various cells                                        | Various cells (especially involved in allergy/inflammation) |
| **Key Molecule**     | Calcineurin                                            | NF-κB transcription factor                           | MAP kinases (ERK, JNK, p38)                           | JAK kinases, STAT transcription factors              | Phospholipase C enzyme                               |
| **Downstream Effect**| Prevents NFAT dephosphorylation & nuclear translocation | Increases transcription of pro-inflammatory cytokines | Involved in cell growth, differentiation, apoptosis | Involved in cytokine signaling, cell growth          | Produces IP3/DAG, activates downstream effectors    |
| **Relevant to Tx Rej**| Yes (inhibits T-cell activation & IL-2 production)     | Yes (contributes to inflammation)                    | Less direct role in acute T-cell rejection            | Less direct role in acute T-cell rejection            | Not a primary target for acute T-cell rejection        |
| **Affected Cytokine**| IL-2 (primarily)                                       | IL-1, TNF-α, IL-6                                    | Variable                                             | Variable (depends on cytokine receptor)              | Variable                                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor used to prevent transplant rejection. Its mechanism involves inhibiting the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT, thereby blocking IL-2 production and T-cell activation. Remember this pathway for questions about immunosuppression in transplantation. Differentiate it from other immunosuppressants like mTOR inhibitors (sirolimus, everolimus) which block IL-2 signaling downstream, or mycophenolate mofetil which inhibits purine synthesis. Also, distinguish it from other signaling pathways like NF-κB, MAPK, JAK-STAT, and PLC, which are targets of other drugs or involved in different immune processes.

<a id='question-088'></a>

### Question 088: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 38 (virtually)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of action of calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) in preventing T-cell activation following transplantation, specifically their effect on intracellular signaling pathways involving calcineurin, NFAT, and IL-2 transcription. (理解钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素、他克莫司）在移植后预防T细胞活化的作用机制，特别是它们对涉及钙调神经磷酸酶、NFAT和IL-2转录的细胞内信号通路的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male patient receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor due to end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy. Post-transplant, he is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three months later, he presents with a 2-week history of worsening fatigue, low-grade fever (37.8°C), and a non-productive cough. Chest X-ray reveals bilateral diffuse interstitial infiltrates. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis shows numerous atypical lymphocytes with large, hyperchromatic nuclei and abundant cytoplasm, some exhibiting intranuclear inclusions. Cytomegalovirus (CMV) PCR in the BAL fluid is positive. The patient's tacrolimus level is found to be subtherapeutic (trough level 3 ng/mL, target range 8-12 ng/mL). Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains why subtherapeutic levels of tacrolimus predispose this patient to CMV infection?

(A) Tacrolimus inhibits the production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), impairing the host's ability to control viral replication.
(B) Tacrolimus blocks the activation of the complement cascade, preventing opsonization and phagocytosis of CMV-infected cells.
(C) Tacrolimus inhibits the binding of viral proteins to MHC class I molecules, preventing antigen presentation to CTLs.
(D) Tacrolimus prevents the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells (NFAT), thereby inhibiting the transcription of interleukin-2 (IL-2) and other cytokines essential for T-cell proliferation and antiviral response.
(E) Tacrolimus directly inhibits the replication of CMV by binding to viral DNA polymerase.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient (immunosuppressed).
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone.
- **Presentation:** Fatigue, low-grade fever, non-productive cough (suggestive of opportunistic infection).
- **Imaging:** Bilateral diffuse interstitial infiltrates (consistent with pneumonia, potentially viral or atypical).
- **BAL Findings:** Atypical lymphocytes with intranuclear inclusions (highly suggestive of CMV infection).
- **Diagnosis Confirmation:** Positive CMV PCR in BAL fluid.
- **Immunosuppression Status:** Subtherapeutic tacrolimus level (3 ng/mL vs. target 8-12 ng/mL).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism by which *subtherapeutic* tacrolimus increases susceptibility to CMV.

The key is linking the *mechanism of action* of tacrolimus to the *control of CMV infection*. Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor. The patient has clinical and laboratory evidence of CMV infection, and the infection occurs when the immunosuppression (tacrolimus) is inadequate. Therefore, the question asks how tacrolimus normally prevents infection, and how its absence allows infection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus binds to an intracellular protein called FKBP12 (FK506 binding protein 12). This complex then binds to and inhibits calcineurin, a calcium-dependent phosphatase.
- **Calcineurin's Role:** Calcineurin normally dephosphorylates the cytoplasmic transcription factor NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells).
- **NFAT Activation:** Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus.
- **Cytokine Transcription:** In the nucleus, NFAT binds to promoter regions of genes encoding key cytokines, most importantly IL-2, but also IL-4, IFN-γ, and TNF-α.
- **T-cell Activation:** IL-2 is crucial for T-cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival. IFN-γ is critical for activating macrophages and enhancing CTL activity, both essential for controlling intracellular pathogens like CMV.
- **Tacrolimus Effect:** By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus prevents NFAT dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation. This blocks the transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines necessary for effective T-cell mediated immunity against CMV.
- **CMV Control:** Effective control of CMV relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, particularly CTLs (CD8+ T cells) and IFN-γ production by T cells and NK cells.
- **Subtherapeutic Levels:** When tacrolimus levels are low, calcineurin is less inhibited, NFAT can translocate to the nucleus, and IL-2 and IFN-γ production can occur, but not sufficiently to mount a robust antiviral response. When levels are *very* low or absent, T-cell activation is significantly impaired, making the patient highly susceptible to opportunistic infections like CMV.

##### 3. Linking Tacrolimus to CMV Susceptibility
- The vignette describes a patient with CMV pneumonia occurring when tacrolimus levels are subtherapeutic.
- This implies that tacrolimus normally plays a role in preventing or controlling CMV infection.
- The mechanism of tacrolimus involves inhibiting T-cell activation by blocking IL-2 transcription via the calcineurin-NFAT pathway.
- Impaired T-cell activation, particularly reduced IL-2 production and IFN-γ production, weakens the host's ability to control CMV replication.
- Therefore, subtherapeutic levels of tacrolimus lead to inadequate suppression of T-cell activation, but still insufficient activation to control CMV, resulting in opportunistic infection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Tacrolimus inhibits the production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), impairing the host's ability to control viral replication.** While tacrolimus *does* inhibit IFN-γ production (as IFN-γ transcription is also NFAT-dependent), this statement is incomplete. Tacrolimus inhibits *multiple* cytokines, and the primary effect is on IL-2, which is essential for T-cell proliferation and overall T-cell response. IFN-γ inhibition is a consequence but not the sole or most central mechanism explaining the broad immunosuppression. Option (D) provides a more fundamental and comprehensive explanation involving the upstream pathway (NFAT/IL-2) that affects multiple cytokines, including IFN-γ.
- **(B) Tacrolimus blocks the activation of the complement cascade, preventing opsonization and phagocytosis of CMV-infected cells.** Tacrolimus does *not* directly affect the complement cascade. Complement activation is part of the innate immune response and is not the primary target of calcineurin inhibitors.
- **(C) Tacrolimus inhibits the binding of viral proteins to MHC class I molecules, preventing antigen presentation to CTLs.** Tacrolimus does *not* interfere with antigen processing or presentation via MHC molecules. Its action is downstream, affecting T-cell activation *after* antigen presentation.
- **(E) Tacrolimus directly inhibits the replication of CMV by binding to viral DNA polymerase.** Tacrolimus is an immunosuppressant; it does *not* have direct antiviral activity against CMV or any other virus. Its effect is solely on the host immune system.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Tacrolimus Mechanism                                                                                                | Other Immunosuppressants (e.g., Mycophenolate)                                                              | CMV Control Mechanism                                                                                                |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Target**              | Calcineurin (via FKBP12 complex)                                                                                     | Inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMP dehydrogenase)                                                          | Cell-mediated immunity (CTLs, NK cells), IFN-γ production                                                              |
| **Pathway Affected**    | Blocks NFAT dephosphorylation & nuclear translocation -> Inhibits IL-2, IFN-γ, TNF-α transcription                     | Inhibits purine synthesis -> Impairs lymphocyte proliferation                                                     | T-cell recognition of viral peptides presented on MHC I, IFN-γ activation of macrophages, antibody production (lesser) |
| **Primary Effect**      | Inhibits T-cell activation and proliferation                                                                         | Inhibits lymphocyte proliferation                                                                                | Requires intact T-cell function (especially CTLs) and cytokine production (IFN-γ, IL-2)                               |
| **Relevance to CMV**    | Impairs T-cell response needed to control CMV replication; subtherapeutic levels increase risk of CMV reactivation/infection | Impairs lymphocyte proliferation, contributing to overall immunosuppression and increased risk of opportunistic infections | Susceptible to control by robust cell-mediated immunity; opportunistic infection in immunosuppressed hosts              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Tacrolimus (and cyclosporine) are calcineurin inhibitors that block T-cell activation by preventing NFAT nuclear translocation and subsequent IL-2 transcription. This mechanism explains their immunosuppressive effects and the increased risk of opportunistic infections like CMV when levels are subtherapeutic.
- **Differentials:** Understand the mechanisms of other immunosuppressants (e.g., mycophenolate inhibits purine synthesis, prednisone affects cytokine gene transcription and lymphocyte trafficking, azathioprine is a purine analog). Recognize that CMV is a common opportunistic infection in transplant recipients due to impaired cell-mediated immunity.

<a id='question-089'></a>

### Question 089: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 39 (assures)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of action of immunosuppressive drugs used in transplantation, specifically focusing on calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine and tacrolimus) and their effect on T-cell activation. (理解移植中使用的免疫抑制药物的作用机制，特别是钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素和他克莫司）对T细胞活化的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male patient receives a kidney transplant due to end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy. Post-transplant, he is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three months after transplantation, the patient develops a diffuse maculopapular rash, fever (38.5°C), and mild diarrhea. Laboratory tests reveal elevated serum creatinine (2.5 mg/dL, baseline 1.0 mg/dL), proteinuria (3+), and a biopsy of the transplanted kidney shows evidence of acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with eosinophils. Tacrolimus levels are found to be subtherapeutic (target range 8-12 ng/mL, patient level 4 ng/mL). The patient's physician suspects acute rejection, but the clinical picture and biopsy findings are atypical. Further investigation reveals a history of recent exposure to a pet cat. The patient is subsequently diagnosed with a disseminated infection caused by *Toxoplasma gondii*. His immunosuppression is adjusted, and he is started on pyrimethamine and sulfadiazine. Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains why tacrolimus, even at subtherapeutic levels, might have contributed to the patient's susceptibility to this opportunistic infection?

(A) Tacrolimus inhibits the transcription of MHC class II molecules, preventing antigen presentation to CD4+ T cells.
(B) Tacrolimus inhibits the synthesis of interleukin-2 (IL-2) by blocking the activation of the nuclear factor of activated T-cells (NFAT) transcription factor.
(C) Tacrolimus inhibits the proliferation of B cells by blocking the CD40-CD40L interaction.
(D) Tacrolimus inhibits the production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by blocking the activation of the Janus kinase (JAK)-signal transducer and activator of transcription (STAT) pathway.
(E) Tacrolimus inhibits the cytotoxic activity of natural killer (NK) cells by blocking the interaction between CD16 and Fc receptors.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient (immunosuppressed).
*   **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Maculopapular rash, fever, diarrhea, elevated creatinine, proteinuria, kidney biopsy showing acute tubular necrosis and eosinophilic interstitial inflammation (suggestive of drug reaction or atypical rejection, but also consistent with opportunistic infection).
*   **Tacrolimus Level:** Subtherapeutic (4 ng/mL, target 8-12 ng/mL).
*   **Exposure History:** Recent exposure to a pet cat (risk factor for *Toxoplasma gondii*).
*   **Diagnosis:** Disseminated *Toxoplasma gondii* infection.
*   **Question:** Mechanism by which tacrolimus contributes to susceptibility to this infection, even at subtherapeutic levels.

The key is understanding tacrolimus's mechanism of action and its impact on the immune response, particularly against intracellular pathogens like *Toxoplasma gondii*. The vignette sets up a scenario where the patient develops an opportunistic infection (*Toxoplasma*) despite subtherapeutic tacrolimus levels. This implies that even low levels of the drug can impair the immune response necessary to control such infections.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor. It binds to an intracellular protein called FKBP12 (FK506 binding protein 12). This complex then inhibits calcineurin, a calcium-dependent phosphatase. Calcineurin normally dephosphorylates the Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells (NFAT) transcription factor. Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus, where it promotes the transcription of genes encoding cytokines, most notably Interleukin-2 (IL-2). By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus prevents NFAT dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation, thereby blocking IL-2 gene transcription.
*   **IL-2 Importance:** IL-2 is a crucial cytokine for T-cell proliferation, differentiation, and activation. It acts in an autocrine and paracrine manner to stimulate T-cell growth and function.
*   **Toxoplasma Immunity:** Control of *Toxoplasma gondii* infection relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, particularly CD4+ Th1 cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). Th1 cells produce IFN-γ, which activates macrophages to kill intracellular *Toxoplasma* tachyzoites. CTLs directly kill infected cells. Both Th1 and CTL responses are dependent on IL-2 for optimal function and proliferation.
*   **Connecting Tacrolimus to Toxoplasma:** By inhibiting IL-2 production, tacrolimus impairs the development and function of both CD4+ Th1 cells and CD8+ CTLs. This weakens the cell-mediated immune response required to control *Toxoplasma gondii*, making the patient susceptible to disseminated infection, even when tacrolimus levels are subtherapeutic. The drug's effect is dose-dependent but can still significantly impact T-cell function at lower concentrations.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Tacrolimus inhibits the transcription of MHC class II molecules, preventing antigen presentation to CD4+ T cells.** This mechanism is characteristic of *interferon-gamma (IFN-γ)*, not tacrolimus. IFN-γ downregulates MHC class II expression, impairing antigen presentation. Tacrolimus primarily affects T-cell activation *after* antigen presentation.
*   **(C) Tacrolimus inhibits the proliferation of B cells by blocking the CD40-CD40L interaction.** Blocking the CD40-CD40L interaction is the mechanism of action of *belatacept*, another immunosuppressant used in transplantation. Tacrolimus's primary effect is on T-cell activation, not directly on B-cell proliferation via CD40-CD40L.
*   **(D) Tacrolimus inhibits the production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by blocking the activation of the Janus kinase (JAK)-signal transducer and activator of transcription (STAT) pathway.** While tacrolimus ultimately impairs the *production* of IFN-γ (as it's a T-cell cytokine), its primary mechanism is not direct inhibition of the JAK-STAT pathway itself. The JAK-STAT pathway is downstream of cytokine receptor signaling (e.g., IFN-γ receptor). Tacrolimus blocks the upstream T-cell activation step (NFAT) required for IFN-γ gene transcription. Furthermore, drugs like *tocilizumab* or *baricitinib* directly target the JAK-STAT pathway.
*   **(E) Tacrolimus inhibits the cytotoxic activity of natural killer (NK) cells by blocking the interaction between CD16 and Fc receptors.** Tacrolimus primarily targets T-cell activation. While immunosuppression can indirectly affect NK cell function, this is not the direct mechanism of tacrolimus. NK cell activity is mediated through different pathways, including interactions involving CD16 (FcγRIII) and activating/inhibitory receptors.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Tacrolimus Mechanism                                   | IFN-γ Mechanism                                     | Belatacept Mechanism                               | JAK Inhibitors Mechanism                             |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Target**      | T-cells                                               | Antigen-presenting cells (APCs)                     | T-cells                                               | JAK-STAT pathway (various cells)                     |
| **Key Molecule**        | Calcineurin, NFAT                                      | IFN-γ receptor, STAT1                               | CD40L on T-cells, CD40 on APCs/B-cells              | JAK kinases                                          |
| **Effect on IL-2**      | Inhibits transcription (via NFAT)                      | No direct effect                                   | No direct effect                                   | No direct effect                                   |
| **Effect on MHC Class II** | No direct effect                                   | Downregulates expression                             | No direct effect                                   | No direct effect                                   |
| **Effect on IFN-γ**     | Indirectly inhibits production (due to impaired T-cell activation) | No direct effect                                   | No direct effect                                   | No direct effect                                   |
| **Relevance to *Toxoplasma*** | Impairs Th1/CTL response (IL-2 dependent)           | Impairs antigen presentation, Th1 response          | Impairs T-cell help for B-cells, T-cell activation | Broad immunosuppression, affects cytokine signaling |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor that blocks T-cell activation by preventing NFAT nuclear translocation and subsequent IL-2 transcription. This impairment of T-cell function, particularly IL-2-dependent Th1 and CTL responses, increases susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like *Toxoplasma gondii*. Remember that even subtherapeutic levels can have significant immunosuppressive effects. Differentiate tacrolimus's mechanism from other immunosuppressants like IFN-γ (MHC II downregulation), Belatacept (CD40-CD40L blockade), and JAK inhibitors (JAK-STAT pathway blockade).

<a id='question-090'></a>

### Question 090: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 40 (genetically)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic basis of graft rejection, specifically the role of MHC class I and class II molecules in allorecognition and the consequences of their absence or deficiency in the context of transplantation. (理解移植排斥反应的遗传基础，特别是MHC I类和II类分子在异体识别中的作用，以及它们缺乏或缺陷在移植背景下的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. The donor is matched for ABO blood group and HLA-A, -B, and -DR loci. However, the donor kidney expresses significantly reduced levels of MHC class I molecules on the surface of endothelial cells and renal tubular epithelial cells. The recipient is otherwise healthy and has no history of autoimmune disease or prior transplants. Post-transplant, the recipient develops acute kidney injury characterized by rapidly rising creatinine levels within the first week. Renal biopsy reveals diffuse endothelial cell injury, tubular cell necrosis, and interstitial edema, but no significant lymphocytic infiltration or evidence of antibody-mediated rejection (e.g., C4d deposition). Flow cytometry analysis of the donor kidney tissue confirms the absence of MHC class I expression on endothelial cells and tubular epithelial cells. The recipient's serum contains antibodies reactive against MHC class I molecules.

Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism underlying this patient's acute kidney injury following transplantation?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection due to pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies against donor MHC class II molecules.
(B) T-cell mediated rejection due to direct allorecognition of donor MHC class I molecules by recipient T cells.
(C) Antibody-mediated rejection due to the formation of de novo anti-MHC class I antibodies against the donor kidney endothelium.
(D) Antibody-mediated rejection due to the formation of de novo anti-MHC class I antibodies against the donor kidney endothelium, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury.
(E) Antibody-mediated rejection due to the formation of de novo anti-MHC class I antibodies against the donor kidney endothelium, leading to NK cell-mediated lysis of endothelial cells via antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male, ESRD from diabetes, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for potential rejection.
- **Donor Matching:** Matched for ABO and HLA-A, -B, -DR. This rules out typical strong pre-formed antibody responses against these common HLA antigens.
- **Donor Kidney Anomaly:** Significantly reduced MHC class I expression on endothelial cells and tubular epithelial cells. This is a crucial clue pointing towards a specific type of rejection.
- **Post-Transplant Course:** Acute kidney injury within the first week, characterized by rising creatinine. This indicates acute rejection.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse endothelial cell injury, tubular cell necrosis, interstitial edema, *no significant lymphocytic infiltration*, *no C4d deposition*. The lack of lymphocytic infiltrate argues against typical T-cell mediated rejection, and the lack of C4d argues against classical antibody-mediated rejection involving complement activation. The endothelial and tubular injury are key findings.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Confirms absence of MHC class I on donor cells. This confirms the donor's specific genetic defect.
- **Recipient Serum:** Contains antibodies reactive against MHC class I molecules. This indicates the recipient has pre-existing or rapidly developed antibodies against MHC class I.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism.

The combination of reduced MHC class I expression on the donor graft and the presence of anti-MHC class I antibodies in the recipient strongly suggests a specific type of antibody-mediated rejection. MHC class I molecules are crucial for presenting endogenous antigens to CD8+ T cells and for inhibiting NK cell activity via inhibitory KIRs (Killer-cell Immunoglobulin-like Receptors). When MHC class I expression is absent or severely reduced on graft cells (like endothelial cells), the inhibitory signals to NK cells are lost. If the recipient has anti-MHC class I antibodies (which can occur naturally or develop rapidly post-transplant), these antibodies can bind to the remaining MHC class I molecules or potentially to other surface molecules, cross-linking them and activating NK cells via Fc receptors (CD16). This leads to NK cell-mediated lysis of the graft cells (endothelial cells in this case), causing acute rejection. This mechanism is distinct from classical antibody-mediated rejection involving complement activation (C4d deposition) or T-cell mediated rejection involving lymphocytic infiltration.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **MHC Class I Function:** MHC class I molecules (HLA-A, -B, -C in humans) present peptides derived from intracellular proteins (including viral and tumor antigens) to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). They are expressed on virtually all nucleated cells. MHC class I also interacts with inhibitory receptors (like KIRs) on Natural Killer (NK) cells, providing a "missing self" signal that prevents NK cell activation and killing of healthy cells.
- **MHC Class II Function:** MHC class II molecules (HLA-DR, -DQ, -DP in humans) present peptides derived from extracellular proteins (taken up by antigen-presenting cells like macrophages, dendritic cells, B cells) to CD4+ helper T lymphocytes. They are primarily expressed on professional APCs.
- **Graft Rejection Types:**
    - **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours, mediated by pre-formed antibodies (e.g., anti-ABO, anti-HLA) against donor antigens, leading to complement activation, endothelial damage, thrombosis, and graft failure.
    - **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (cellular rejection) or antibody-mediated (humoral rejection).
        - **T-cell mediated:** Involves infiltration of lymphocytes (CD4+ and CD8+) into the graft, causing parenchymal damage.
        - **Antibody-mediated:** Involves donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA antigens. Can be mediated by complement activation (leading to C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries) or by NK cell activation via ADCC when MHC class I is deficient on the graft.
    - **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant, characterized by fibrosis and gradual loss of graft function. Involves both cellular and humoral immunity.
- **NK Cell Activation:** NK cells are lymphocytes of the innate immune system. Their activation is regulated by a balance of activating and inhibitory signals. Inhibitory signals are primarily mediated by interactions between KIRs on NK cells and MHC class I molecules on target cells. If MHC class I expression is reduced or absent ("missing self"), the inhibitory signal is lost, and activating signals (e.g., from stress ligands or antibody binding via CD16) can trigger NK cell activation, leading to the release of cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) and cytokine production, resulting in target cell lysis.
- **Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** A mechanism where NK cells (or other effector cells like macrophages, neutrophils) recognize and kill target cells coated with antibodies. The Fc region of the antibody binds to Fc receptors (e.g., CD16 on NK cells), triggering the effector cell to release cytotoxic molecules.

##### 3. Specific Scenario Analysis:
- The donor kidney lacks MHC class I. This makes the graft cells susceptible to NK cell attack because the inhibitory signal via KIR-MHC class I interaction is absent.
- The recipient has anti-MHC class I antibodies. These antibodies can bind to the few remaining MHC class I molecules on the graft cells or potentially to other surface molecules.
- Binding of anti-MHC class I antibodies to the graft cells activates NK cells via ADCC (Fc receptor CD16 engagement).
- Activated NK cells lyse the endothelial cells and tubular epithelial cells, causing acute kidney injury.
- This mechanism explains the rapid onset of injury, the endothelial/tubular damage, the *absence* of significant lymphocytic infiltration (characteristic of T-cell rejection), and the *absence* of C4d deposition (characteristic of classical complement-mediated antibody rejection).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection due to pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies against donor MHC class II molecules. This would typically cause hyperacute or acute antibody-mediated rejection, often involving complement activation (C4d deposition) and potentially T-cell help. While possible, the vignette emphasizes MHC class I deficiency and anti-MHC class I antibodies, and the lack of C4d makes classical complement-mediated rejection less likely. Also, MHC class II deficiency is less common and usually doesn't cause this specific pattern of rejection.
- (B): T-cell mediated rejection due to direct allorecognition of donor MHC class I molecules by recipient T cells. This would involve significant lymphocytic infiltration into the graft parenchyma, which is explicitly stated as absent in the biopsy findings.
- (C): Antibody-mediated rejection due to the formation of de novo anti-MHC class I antibodies against the donor kidney endothelium. This is partially correct in identifying anti-MHC class I antibodies and endothelial targeting, but it doesn't specify the *mechanism* of injury. The key mechanism in this specific scenario (MHC class I deficiency) is NK cell activation via ADCC, not necessarily complement activation or direct antibody-mediated cytotoxicity. Option (D) includes complement activation, which is not supported by the lack of C4d. Option (E) correctly identifies the ADCC mechanism involving NK cells.
- (D): Antibody-mediated rejection due to the formation of de novo anti-MHC class I antibodies against the donor kidney endothelium, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury. This describes classical antibody-mediated rejection. However, the biopsy findings specifically mention the *absence* of C4d deposition, which is a hallmark of complement activation in antibody-mediated rejection. Therefore, this mechanism is unlikely.
- (E): Antibody-mediated rejection due to the formation of de novo anti-MHC class I antibodies against the donor kidney endothelium, leading to NK cell-mediated lysis of endothelial cells via antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). This option accurately describes the mechanism of rejection in the context of MHC class I deficiency on the graft and the presence of anti-MHC class I antibodies in the recipient. The antibodies bind to the graft cells, leading to NK cell activation via ADCC and subsequent lysis of endothelial cells, explaining the clinical and pathological findings.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | MHC Class I Deficiency Rejection (Option E) | Classical Antibody-Mediated Rejection (Option D) | T-Cell Mediated Rejection (Option B) | Hyperacute Rejection (Option A) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------------------------ |
| **Primary Mediator**    | NK cells (ADCC)                             | Antibodies, Complement, Neutrophils              | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)          | Pre-formed Antibodies           |
| **Target Antigen**      | MHC Class I                                 | HLA Class I or II                               | HLA Class I (direct), Class II (indirect) | ABO, HLA                       |
| **Mechanism**           | Loss of "self", ADCC                       | Complement activation (C4d+), ADCC, direct injury | Cytokine release, direct cytotoxicity | Complement activation, thrombosis |
| **Histopathology**      | Endothelial injury, minimal infiltrate      | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, thrombosis     | Lymphocytic infiltrate, tubulitis   | Thrombosis, necrosis            |
| **Timing**              | Days to weeks (acute)                       | Days to weeks (acute)                           | Days to weeks (acute)               | Minutes to hours                |
| **MHC Class I Status**  | Deficient/Absent                            | Normal                                          | Normal                               | Normal                          |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Absent                                      | Present                                         | Absent                               | Often Present                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding the role of MHC class I in inhibiting NK cells is crucial. When MHC class I is deficient, NK cells become activated by antibodies binding to the graft endothelium, leading to ADCC and rejection. Differentiate this mechanism from classical antibody-mediated rejection (complement-dependent, C4d+) and T-cell mediated rejection (lymphocytic infiltrate). Key differentials include typical acute antibody-mediated rejection and acute cellular rejection.

<a id='question-091'></a>

### Question 091: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 41 (unrelated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of immune evasion by intracellular pathogens, specifically focusing on the inhibition of antigen presentation pathways (e.g., TAP, proteasome) and the resulting impact on T cell activation and cytokine production. (理解细胞内病原体免疫逃逸机制，特别是抑制抗原呈递途径（如TAP、蛋白酶体）以及对T细胞激活和细胞因子产生的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male, recently returned from a 3-month backpacking trip through Southeast Asia, presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of worsening fever, night sweats, and a persistent, non-productive cough. He reports no significant past medical history, including no known immunodeficiency, and denies recent antibiotic use. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.2°C (102.6°F), a heart rate of 110 bpm, and bilateral crackles auscultated in the lower lung fields. Chest X-ray shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Sputum samples are collected for analysis. Gram stain of the sputum shows no predominant organism. Acid-fast bacilli (AFB) stain is negative. However, culture of the sputum on specialized media eventually grows slow-growing, pleomorphic, weakly acid-fast bacilli. The organism is identified as *Nocardia farcinica*. The patient is started on trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole. Despite initial improvement, he develops severe, refractory pneumonia and sepsis. Further investigation reveals markedly reduced levels of both MHC class I and class II molecules on the surface of his peripheral blood monocytes and dendritic cells, along with significantly decreased production of IFN-γ by his T lymphocytes in response to *Nocardia* antigens.

Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for the impaired antigen presentation and subsequent T cell dysfunction observed in this patient?

(A) Inhibition of proteasome activity, preventing the degradation of cytosolic proteins into antigenic peptides.
(B) Inhibition of TAP (Transporter associated with Antigen Processing) function, preventing the transport of antigenic peptides into the endoplasmic reticulum.
(C) Inhibition of MHC class I synthesis, preventing the assembly of MHC class I molecules with antigenic peptides.
(D) Inhibition of MHC class II synthesis, preventing the assembly of MHC class II molecules with antigenic peptides.
(E) Inhibition of lysosomal acidification, preventing the processing of extracellular antigens by MHC class II pathway.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Young adult male with travel history to Southeast Asia, presenting with symptoms suggestive of pneumonia (fever, cough, crackles, interstitial infiltrates). This points towards an infectious etiology, potentially an opportunistic infection or a pathogen endemic to the region.
- **Microbiological Findings:** Sputum culture grows *Nocardia farcinica*, a known opportunistic pathogen, particularly in immunocompromised individuals or those with underlying lung disease. The organism is weakly acid-fast, distinguishing it from *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*.
- **Clinical Course:** Despite treatment with trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (a standard therapy for *Nocardia*), the patient develops severe, refractory disease. This suggests an underlying host factor contributing to the severity, such as an impaired immune response.
- **Immunological Findings:** The key finding is the reduced expression of MHC class I and class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) and decreased IFN-γ production by T cells. This indicates a defect in antigen presentation and T cell activation, which is crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like *Nocardia*.
- **Question Stem:** The question asks for the mechanism responsible for the impaired antigen presentation and T cell dysfunction. This requires linking the clinical and immunological findings to a specific step in the antigen processing and presentation pathway.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- *Nocardia* species are facultative intracellular bacteria. Effective control requires cell-mediated immunity, involving antigen presentation by APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages) via MHC class I (for CD8+ T cells) and MHC class II (for CD4+ T cells) molecules to activate cytotoxic and helper T cells, respectively.
- The clinical picture suggests *Nocardia* is causing severe disease despite treatment, and the immunological findings point to a defect in antigen presentation.
- *Nocardia* species, particularly *Nocardia asteroides* and *Nocardia farcinica*, are known to produce virulence factors that interfere with host immune responses. One key mechanism involves the secretion of a protein called **RipR**.
- RipR is a potent inhibitor of the **26S proteasome**, a large multi-subunit protein complex responsible for degrading ubiquitinated intracellular proteins into short peptides. These peptides are the primary source of antigens presented by MHC class I molecules.
- By inhibiting the proteasome, RipR prevents the generation of antigenic peptides from *Nocardia* proteins within the host cell cytoplasm.
- This lack of peptide generation directly impairs the loading of MHC class I molecules in the endoplasmic reticulum, leading to reduced MHC class I surface expression and defective activation of CD8+ T cells.
- While MHC class II presentation is also affected (as MHC class II presents peptides derived from extracellular antigens processed in lysosomes, and proteasome activity can also contribute to this pathway), the primary and most direct target of RipR is the proteasome, which is essential for MHC class I antigen generation. The vignette mentions reduced MHC class I *and* class II, which can occur secondarily due to impaired T cell help (CD4+ T cells activated via MHC class II are needed to help CD8+ T cells) or potentially some direct effect on MHC II processing, but the *most direct* mechanism of *Nocardia* immune evasion described is proteasome inhibition.
- Reduced IFN-γ production is a consequence of impaired T cell activation due to defective antigen presentation. IFN-γ is critical for macrophage activation and controlling intracellular pathogens.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (B) Inhibition of TAP function: TAP (Transporter associated with Antigen Processing) is responsible for transporting antigenic peptides from the cytosol into the endoplasmic reticulum, where they bind to MHC class I molecules. While TAP inhibition would also impair MHC class I presentation, *Nocardia* virulence factors primarily target the proteasome, not TAP. TAP inhibition is a mechanism used by some viruses (e.g., CMV) and certain bacteria (e.g., *Listeria monocytogenes* via ActA protein, which prevents TAP-mediated transport of MHC I to the cell surface, but not peptide transport itself).
- (C) Inhibition of MHC class I synthesis: This would prevent the production of the MHC class I heavy chain or β2-microglobulin. While some pathogens can interfere with MHC synthesis, the primary mechanism described for *Nocardia* involves inhibiting the *processing* of antigens, not the synthesis of the MHC molecule itself.
- (D) Inhibition of MHC class II synthesis: Similar to (C), this would prevent the production of MHC class II molecules. While MHC class II presentation is also impaired in the vignette, the direct mechanism of *Nocardia* virulence (RipR) targets the proteasome, which is crucial for both MHC I and MHC II antigen generation, but its primary effect is on MHC I processing. Furthermore, some pathogens specifically target MHC class II synthesis or processing (e.g., *Listeria* inhibits TAP-mediated transport of MHC I to the surface, but doesn't directly inhibit MHC II synthesis).
- (E) Inhibition of lysosomal acidification: Lysosomal acidification is essential for the degradation of extracellular antigens taken up by APCs and their subsequent presentation via MHC class II molecules. Inhibition of lysosomal acidification (e.g., by *Chlamydia*) would primarily impair MHC class II presentation, not MHC class I presentation, which relies on cytosolic processing via the proteasome.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | *Nocardia* (RipR)                                  | *Listeria monocytogenes* (ActA)                     | CMV (US proteins)                                   | *Chlamydia* (LPS)                                  |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ |
| **Primary Target**       | 26S Proteasome                                    | TAP transport to cell surface                      | TAP peptide transport, MHC I ER export             | Lysosomal acidification                             |
| **MHC Class Affected**  | Primarily MHC I (via proteasome inhibition)        | Primarily MHC I (prevents surface expression)     | Primarily MHC I (prevents peptide loading/export) | Primarily MHC II (impairs antigen processing)     |
| **Immune Evasion Goal** | Prevent cytosolic antigen processing & presentation | Prevent MHC I surface expression                  | Prevent peptide loading & MHC I surface expression | Prevent phagosome-lysosome fusion & MHC II processing |
| **Clinical Relevance**   | Severe, refractory nocardiosis                     | Severe listeriosis, especially in immunocompromised | Severe CMV disease in immunocompromised            | Chronic chlamydial infections                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*Nocardia* species evade the host immune system by secreting virulence factors like RipR, which inhibit the 26S proteasome. This prevents the degradation of cytosolic proteins into antigenic peptides, thereby impairing MHC class I antigen presentation and subsequent CD8+ T cell activation. This mechanism explains the severe, refractory disease observed in this patient despite antibiotic therapy. Key differentials for impaired antigen presentation include viral infections (CMV, EBV) and other bacterial infections (*Listeria*, *Chlamydia*) that target different steps in the pathway. Remember that proteasome inhibition primarily affects MHC class I presentation.
