<a id='question-092'></a>

### Question 092: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 42 (individuals)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of action of immunosuppressants used in organ transplantation, specifically focusing on calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine and tacrolimus) and their effect on T-cell activation. (理解器官移植中使用的免疫抑制剂的作用机制，特别是钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素和他克莫司）对T细胞活化的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male patient receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor due to end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy. Post-transplant, he is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three months after transplantation, the patient presents with symptoms of acute rejection, including fever, flank pain, and elevated serum creatinine. A kidney biopsy reveals interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates and evidence of endothelial injury. His tacrolimus trough level is found to be subtherapeutic (below the target range). The transplant team decides to increase the tacrolimus dose.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains how tacrolimus prevents acute T-cell mediated rejection in this patient?

(A) Inhibition of IL-2 receptor signaling on T cells.
(B) Inhibition of NF-κB activation in T cells.
(C) Inhibition of calcineurin-dependent dephosphorylation of NFAT, preventing IL-2 transcription.
(D) Inhibition of MHC class II molecule expression on antigen-presenting cells.
(E) Inhibition of CD40 ligand (CD40L) binding to CD40 on antigen-presenting cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient (deceased donor), end-stage renal disease (diabetic nephropathy). This sets the context of organ transplantation and the need for immunosuppression.
- **Immunosuppressive Regimen:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. This highlights the use of multiple immunosuppressants, with tacrolimus being a key agent.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Acute rejection symptoms (fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine) 3 months post-transplant. This indicates a failure of the immunosuppressive regimen to prevent T-cell mediated rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, endothelial injury. These are classic histological features of acute T-cell mediated rejection.
- **Tacrolimus Level:** Subtherapeutic. This suggests that the inadequate drug level contributed to the rejection episode.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of action of tacrolimus in preventing acute T-cell mediated rejection. This directly tests knowledge of tacrolimus pharmacology and immunology.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Activation:** T-cell activation requires two signals. Signal 1 is TCR engagement with MHC-peptide complex. Signal 2 involves costimulatory molecules (e.g., CD28-B7).
- **Signal Transduction:** Signal 1 (TCR engagement) leads to the activation of intracellular tyrosine kinases (Lck, Zap70). This initiates a signaling cascade involving ZAP-70 phosphorylation of LAT, which recruits SLP-76 and other signaling molecules.
- **Calcineurin Pathway:** A crucial downstream event is the activation of calcineurin, a calcium-dependent phosphatase. Calcineurin dephosphorylates the Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells (NFAT).
- **NFAT Translocation:** Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus.
- **IL-2 Transcription:** In the nucleus, NFAT binds to promoter regions of genes, most notably the gene for Interleukin-2 (IL-2). This promotes the transcription of IL-2.
- **IL-2 Function:** IL-2 is a critical cytokine for T-cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival. It acts via the IL-2 receptor (IL-2R) on T cells, activating downstream pathways like JAK-STAT.
- **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus (also known as FK506) is a calcineurin inhibitor. It binds to an intracellular protein called FKBP12 (FK506-binding protein 12). The tacrolimus-FKBP12 complex then binds to and inhibits calcineurin.
- **Consequence of Tacrolimus:** By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus prevents the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT. This blocks the transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines crucial for T-cell activation and proliferation. Consequently, T-cell mediated immune responses, including graft rejection, are suppressed.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Inhibition of IL-2 receptor signaling on T cells. This is the mechanism of action of drugs like basiliximab and daclizumab, which are monoclonal antibodies targeting the IL-2 receptor alpha chain (CD25). Tacrolimus acts upstream of the receptor by inhibiting IL-2 production.
- (B): Inhibition of NF-κB activation in T cells. NF-κB is a transcription factor involved in the expression of many inflammatory genes, including some cytokines. While NF-κB is important in T-cell activation, tacrolimus's primary target is the calcineurin-NFAT pathway, not NF-κB directly. Corticosteroids (like prednisone) can inhibit NF-κB activation.
- (D): Inhibition of MHC class II molecule expression on antigen-presenting cells. This is the mechanism of action of drugs like chloroquine and hydroxychloroquine, which interfere with antigen processing and presentation in lysosomes/endosomes. Tacrolimus does not directly affect MHC class II expression.
- (E): Inhibition of CD40 ligand (CD40L) binding to CD40 on antigen-presenting cells. This is the mechanism of action of belatacept, a fusion protein that blocks the interaction between CD40L on T cells and CD40 on APCs, which is crucial for T-cell costimulation. Tacrolimus does not target this pathway.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Tacrolimus (Calcineurin Inhibitor) | IL-2 Receptor Antagonists (Basiliximab, Daclizumab) | Corticosteroids (Prednisone) | Chloroquine/Hydroxychloroquine | Belatacept |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :--------- |
| **Primary Target**      | Calcineurin                        | IL-2 Receptor (CD25)                               | NF-κB, broad anti-inflammatory | Lysosomal function, MHC II processing | CD40L-CD40 interaction |
| **Mechanism**           | Inhibits NFAT dephosphorylation    | Blocks IL-2 binding                                 | Inhibits gene transcription | Inhibits antigen presentation | Blocks T-cell costimulation |
| **Effect on IL-2**      | Decreases production               | Blocks signaling                                    | Decreases production        | Decreases production           | Decreases production |
| **Effect on T-cell Activation** | Inhibits proliferation, activation | Inhibits proliferation, activation                  | Broad inhibition            | Inhibits activation            | Inhibits activation |
| **Clinical Use**        | Organ transplant rejection          | Organ transplant rejection (induction)               | Broad immunosuppression     | Malaria, autoimmune diseases   | Organ transplant rejection |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tacrolimus and cyclosporine are calcineurin inhibitors used to prevent T-cell mediated organ rejection by blocking IL-2 production. Remember that tacrolimus inhibits calcineurin, preventing NFAT dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation, thus inhibiting IL-2 gene transcription. Differentiate this mechanism from IL-2 receptor antagonists (basiliximab, daclizumab), NF-κB inhibitors (corticosteroids), MHC class II synthesis inhibitors (chloroquine/hydroxychloroquine), and CD40L inhibitors (belatacept).

<a id='question-093'></a>

### Question 093: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 43 (distinctive)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute and chronic rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically focusing on the role of T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) and antibodies (donor-specific antibodies, DSAs) in mediating graft damage, and the distinct pathological features associated with different types of rejection. (理解实体器官移植中急性和慢性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）和抗体（供体特异性抗体，DSA）在介导移植物损伤中的作用，以及不同类型排斥相关的独特病理特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) score was 10% (indicating reactivity against 10% of donor HLA antigens). He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three months post-transplant, his serum creatinine begins to rise from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 2.5 mg/dL over two weeks. A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltrates, tubular atrophy, and arteriolar hyalinosis. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Histopathology also shows evidence of endothelialitis and glomerulitis. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft. The patient's serum is tested and found positive for donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA class I and class II antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed graft dysfunction and histological findings?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells against donor endothelial cells expressing HLA class I antigens.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by DSAs binding to HLA class II antigens on donor podocytes, leading to glomerular damage.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I antigens presented on donor cells within the graft.
(D) Immune complex deposition leading to crescent formation in Bowman's capsule, characteristic of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease.
(E) T-cell mediated tubular basement membrane (TBM) injury caused by CD4+ Th1 cells releasing perforin and granzymes directly onto tubular epithelial cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplant rejection.
*   **Timeline:** 3 months post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute cellular rejection (ACR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Rising serum creatinine (from 1.2 to 2.5 mg/dL) indicates acute graft dysfunction.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, MMF, prednisone) suggests the rejection is occurring despite immunosuppression.
*   **Biopsy Findings (Light Microscopy):** Diffuse interstitial inflammation (mononuclear cells), tubular atrophy, arteriolar hyalinosis. These are features seen in both ACR and AMR, but the interstitial inflammation is prominent.
*   **Biopsy Findings (Immunofluorescence):** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This is a hallmark of AMR, indicating complement activation.
*   **Biopsy Findings (Histopathology):** Endothelialitis and glomerulitis. These are also features strongly suggestive of AMR.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft. This points towards a cellular component of rejection.
*   **Serology:** Positive DSAs against HLA class I and class II antigens. This confirms the presence of antibodies directed against the donor graft, a key feature of AMR.

The combination of rising creatinine, C4d deposition, endothelialitis, glomerulitis, increased CD8+ T cell infiltration, and positive DSAs strongly points towards acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) with a significant cellular component. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. While AMR involves antibodies (DSAs), the prominent infiltration of CD8+ T cells and the histological features (endothelialitis, glomerulitis) are characteristic of T-cell mediated damage, specifically direct cytotoxicity by CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I antigens on graft cells. The DSAs and C4d deposition indicate an antibody-mediated component is also present, but the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism given the *entire* picture, including the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate and typical features of cellular rejection alongside AMR markers. Option C directly addresses the cytotoxic role of CD8+ T cells, which are significantly increased and are known mediators of direct graft damage in cellular rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Transplant Rejection Types:**
    *   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T cells (CD4+ and CD8+). CD4+ T cells orchestrate the response, while CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing foreign MHC class I molecules. Histology shows interstitial inflammation, tubular injury, and sometimes endothelialitis.
    *   **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by DSAs binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. This activates complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruits inflammatory cells. Histology shows endothelialitis, glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis, and C4d deposition.
    *   **Chronic Rejection:** A complex process involving both cellular and humoral immunity, leading to fibrosis and progressive graft dysfunction.
*   **Mechanism of CD8+ T Cell Cytotoxicity:** CD8+ T cells recognize foreign MHC class I molecules (presenting donor antigens) on the surface of target cells (e.g., graft endothelial cells, tubular epithelial cells). Upon recognition, CD8+ T cells form an immunological synapse with the target cell and release cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes. Perforin creates pores in the target cell membrane, allowing granzymes to enter and activate caspases, leading to apoptosis of the target cell. This is known as direct cytotoxicity.
*   **Mechanism of DSAs:** DSAs (typically IgG) bind to donor HLA antigens on graft endothelial cells. This can activate the classical complement pathway, leading to C3 convertase formation, C3 cleavage, and deposition of C3d and C5b-9 (membrane attack complex, MAC). C4d is a stable fragment of C4b, a component of the C3 convertase, and its deposition in peritubular capillaries is a strong indicator of complement activation by DSAs. DSA binding can also trigger Fc receptor-mediated effector functions, such as ADCC (mediated by NK cells) or phagocytosis.
*   **Role of CD4+ T cells:** CD4+ T cells recognize foreign MHC class II molecules presented by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft or draining lymph nodes. They differentiate into various subsets (Th1, Th2, Th17, Tfh) and release cytokines that orchestrate the immune response, including activating CD8+ T cells, B cells (leading to DSA production), and macrophages.

In this case, the patient exhibits features of both ACR (increased CD8+ T cells, interstitial inflammation) and AMR (positive DSAs, C4d deposition, endothelialitis, glomerulitis). However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. The significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft directly points to their cytotoxic activity as a major contributor to the graft damage. CD8+ T cells recognize donor HLA class I antigens on graft cells and kill them directly. This mechanism aligns well with the histological findings of tubular atrophy and interstitial inflammation. While DSAs and complement activation are present (AMR component), the direct cytotoxic action of CD8+ T cells is a fundamental mechanism of cellular rejection and is strongly supported by the increased CD8+ T cell infiltration.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells against donor endothelial cells expressing HLA class I antigens. While DSAs are present and can mediate ADCC, the primary histological features (diffuse interstitial inflammation, tubular atrophy, endothelialitis, glomerulitis) and the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate are more characteristic of direct T-cell cytotoxicity and complement-mediated damage rather than ADCC being the *most likely* primary mechanism. ADCC typically involves NK cells recognizing Fc receptors on target cells coated with antibodies. While possible, it's less emphasized than direct CD8+ cytotoxicity and complement activation in this mixed picture.
- (B): Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by DSAs binding to HLA class II antigens on donor podocytes, leading to glomerular damage. While DSAs binding to HLA class II antigens activate complement (indicated by C4d deposition) and contribute to glomerular damage (glomerulitis), CDC typically involves the MAC forming pores directly in the target cell membrane, leading to lysis. The primary target of DSAs in AMR is usually the endothelium, not podocytes, although glomerular injury occurs. Furthermore, the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate suggests a significant cellular component beyond just antibody-mediated complement damage.
- (D): Immune complex deposition leading to crescent formation in Bowman's capsule, characteristic of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease. This describes a specific type of glomerulonephritis, not typical transplant rejection. Anti-GBM disease involves antibodies against the GBM itself, leading to linear IgG deposition on immunofluorescence. The vignette describes C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries and DSA reactivity against HLA antigens, not anti-GBM antibodies. Crescent formation is also not mentioned in the biopsy findings.
- (E): T-cell mediated tubular basement membrane (TBM) injury caused by CD4+ Th1 cells releasing perforin and granzymes directly onto tubular epithelial cells. While CD4+ T cells play a role in orchestrating rejection, the direct cytotoxic killing of tubular epithelial cells is primarily mediated by CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I antigens on these cells. CD4+ T cells primarily interact with APCs presenting antigens via MHC class II. While CD4+ cells can contribute to inflammation and indirectly cause tubular damage, direct cytotoxicity against tubular cells is the hallmark of CD8+ T cells. The vignette specifically highlights the increased CD8+ T cell infiltration.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                  | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Anti-GBM Disease |
| :----------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :--------------- |
| **Primary Mediator**     | T cells (CD8+)                 | DSAs                                    | Anti-GBM Abs    |
| **Key Histology**        | Interstitial inflammation, tubular atrophy, endothelialitis | Endothelialitis, glomerulitis, C4d deposition | Crescent formation, linear IgG |
| **Immunofluorescence**   | Minimal or non-specific        | C4d in peritubular capillaries, DSA staining | Linear IgG along GBM |
| **Cellular Infiltrate**  | CD8+ T cells prominent          | Neutrophils, macrophages, CD4+ T cells   | Variable        |
| **DSA Presence**         | Absent                         | Present                                 | Absent           |
| **C4d Deposition**       | Absent                         | Present (peritubular capillaries)        | Absent           |
| **Mechanism**            | Direct CD8+ cytotoxicity        | Complement activation, ADCC, endothelial injury | Immune complex deposition |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute transplant rejection can be broadly classified into cellular (T-cell mediated) and antibody-mediated (humoral) types. Cellular rejection is characterized by T-cell infiltration (especially CD8+) and direct cytotoxicity against graft cells. Antibody-mediated rejection is characterized by DSAs, complement activation (C4d deposition), endothelialitis, and glomerulitis. This case presents features of both, but the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate points to direct CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity as a major mechanism of graft injury. Differentiate transplant rejection from other causes of graft dysfunction (e.g., infection, drug toxicity) and other forms of glomerulonephritis (e.g., anti-GBM disease). Remember that C4d deposition is a key marker for AMR, but cellular rejection often coexists.

<a id='question-094'></a>

### Question 094: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 44 (differences)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying graft rejection, specifically the role of differences in MHC molecules (alloantigens) between donor and recipient in triggering T-cell mediated rejection. (理解移植排斥的免疫机制，特别是供体和受体之间MHC分子差异（同基因抗原）如何触发T细胞介导的排斥反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor who is ABO-compatible but HLA-mismatched (specifically, HLA-A*02:01 and HLA-B*07:02 from the donor, while the recipient is HLA-A*01:01 and HLA-B*08:01). The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including a calcineurin inhibitor (cyclosporine), an antimetabolite (mycophenolate mofetil), and corticosteroids (prednisone). Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops acute onset fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.5 mg/dL to 4.0 mg/dL), and proteinuria. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with numerous infiltrating lymphocytes, including CD8+ T cells, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. The patient's serum contains pre-formed anti-HLA class I antibodies against the donor's HLA-A*02:01 and HLA-B*07:02 antigens.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the acute rejection observed in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft endothelial cells.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to HLA class II molecules on graft endothelial cells.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity involving CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I molecules presented on graft cells.
(D) Complement activation via the alternative pathway leading to C3 convertase formation and subsequent membrane attack complex (MAC) deposition.
(E) Antibody-mediated rejection involving Fc receptor-mediated phagocytosis of graft endothelial cells by macrophages.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient.
*   **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, ABO-compatible, HLA-mismatched (A*02:01/B*07:02 donor vs. A*01:01/B*08:01 recipient). This HLA mismatch is crucial.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (calcineurin inhibitor, antimetabolite, steroid). Despite this, rejection occurs.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** 3 weeks post-transplant, acute onset fever, flank pain, rising creatinine, proteinuria – classic signs of acute graft rejection.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation (lymphocytes, CD8+ T cells), endothelialitis (peritubular capillaries), C4d deposition. These findings point towards both cellular and antibody-mediated rejection components.
*   **Serology:** Pre-formed anti-HLA class I antibodies (DSAs) against donor HLA-A*02:01 and HLA-B*07:02. This confirms the presence of pre-existing antibodies targeting the mismatched HLA antigens.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct mechanism responsible for the acute rejection.

The key elements are the HLA mismatch, the presence of pre-formed DSAs against HLA class I antigens, and the biopsy findings including CD8+ T cell infiltration and endothelialitis. While C4d deposition suggests antibody-mediated involvement, the presence of pre-formed DSAs against HLA class I and CD8+ T cell infiltration strongly implicate T-cell mediated cytotoxicity as a primary mechanism, especially given the HLA mismatch. The DSAs likely contribute to endothelialitis and complement activation (C4d), but the direct killing of graft cells by T cells recognizing the mismatched HLA class I is a central feature of acute cellular rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Graft Rejection:** The immune system recognizes the transplanted organ (graft) as foreign due to differences in MHC molecules (also known as Human Leukocyte Antigens or HLAs in humans) between the donor and recipient.
*   **MHC Molecules:** MHC class I molecules (HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C) are expressed on almost all nucleated cells and present endogenous peptides (e.g., viral, tumor, or self-peptides) to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). MHC class II molecules (HLA-DR, HLA-DQ, HLA-DP) are primarily expressed on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, and B cells, and present exogenous peptides to CD4+ helper T lymphocytes.
*   **T-cell Mediated Rejection (Acute Cellular Rejection):** This is primarily driven by recipient T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) on graft cells.
    *   **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules presenting peptides. Upon activation, they directly kill graft cells expressing the foreign MHC class I via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction. This is a major component of acute cellular rejection.
    *   **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules presenting peptides. They help activate CD8+ T cells and macrophages, contributing to inflammation and tissue damage.
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** This involves pre-formed or de novo antibodies (DSAs) directed against donor HLA antigens (usually class I or class II).
    *   **Mechanisms:** DSAs can cause rejection through:
        *   **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies bind to HLA molecules on endothelial cells, activating the classical complement pathway, leading to MAC formation and cell lysis.
        *   **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC):** Antibodies bind to HLA molecules on graft cells, and NK cells (or other effector cells) recognize the Fc portion of the bound antibody, leading to target cell lysis.
        *   **Fc receptor-mediated phagocytosis:** Antibodies bind to graft cells, and macrophages recognize the Fc portion, leading to phagocytosis.
        *   **Inflammation and Endothelialitis:** Antibody binding can activate endothelial cells and recruit inflammatory cells, leading to microvascular injury (endothelialitis) and thrombosis. C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries is a marker of complement activation, often seen in AMR.
*   **The Case:** The patient has HLA mismatches (A*02:01/B*07:02 donor vs. A*01:01/B*08:01 recipient) and pre-formed DSAs against the donor's HLA class I antigens. The biopsy shows CD8+ T cell infiltration and endothelialitis, along with C4d deposition. The HLA mismatch provides the alloantigens for T-cell recognition (MHC class I). The pre-formed DSAs against HLA class I contribute to antibody-mediated mechanisms (like endothelialitis and complement activation evidenced by C4d). However, the direct killing of graft cells by CD8+ T cells recognizing the mismatched HLA class I molecules is a fundamental mechanism of acute cellular rejection in this HLA-mismatched scenario. The presence of DSAs often exacerbates rejection by contributing to endothelial injury and inflammation, but the T-cell response targeting the mismatched MHC class I is a primary driver.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft endothelial cells.** While ADCC is a mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection, and DSAs are present, the vignette emphasizes the HLA mismatch and CD8+ T cell infiltration. ADCC is less likely to be the *most direct* mechanism compared to T-cell cytotoxicity targeting the mismatched MHC class I. Furthermore, the primary targets in ADCC are often endothelial cells, but T-cell cytotoxicity directly targets parenchymal cells expressing MHC class I.
*   **(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to HLA class II molecules on graft endothelial cells.** CDC is a mechanism of AMR. However, the DSAs in this patient are against HLA *class I* antigens (A*02:01 and B*07:02), not class II. While endothelial cells express HLA class II, the primary targets for DSAs in AMR are often HLA class I on endothelial cells. Also, the strong evidence of CD8+ T cell infiltration points strongly towards a T-cell mediated component.
*   **(D) Complement activation via the alternative pathway leading to C3 convertase formation and subsequent membrane attack complex (MAC) deposition.** The alternative pathway is typically activated by microbial surfaces or abnormal host cells, not directly by antibodies binding to specific HLA antigens. The C4d deposition indicates activation of the *classical* complement pathway, which is triggered by antibody binding (as seen in AMR).
*   **(E) Antibody-mediated rejection involving Fc receptor-mediated phagocytosis of graft endothelial cells by macrophages.** This is another mechanism of AMR. Similar to options A and B, while AMR contributes (evidenced by DSAs and C4d), the vignette highlights the HLA mismatch and CD8+ T cell infiltration, making T-cell mediated cytotoxicity a more central and direct mechanism in this specific context.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Option (C) T-cell Mediated Cytotoxicity (Correct) | Option (A) ADCC | Option (B) CDC (vs HLA Class II) | Option (D) Alternative Complement Pathway | Option (E) Fc Receptor Phagocytosis |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediator**    | CD8+ T cells                                      | NK cells       | Complement (via antibodies)       | Complement (alternative pathway)         | Macrophages                          |
| **Target Antigen**      | Donor HLA Class I on graft cells                  | Antibody-coated cells | HLA Class II (incorrect in vignette) | Microbial surfaces/abnormal cells        | Antibody-coated cells                |
| **Mechanism**           | Perforin/Granzyme, Fas/FasL                       | NK cell lysis  | MAC formation                     | C3 convertase formation, MAC              | Phagocytosis                         |
| **Key Clue in Vignette** | HLA mismatch, CD8+ T cell infiltrate             | DSAs present   | DSAs against Class II (incorrect) | C4d deposition (classical pathway)       | DSAs present                         |
| **Relevance**           | Major component of acute cellular rejection        | AMR mechanism  | AMR mechanism (incorrect target)  | Not primary mechanism in this context    | AMR mechanism                        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection involves both T-cell mediated (cellular) and antibody-mediated (humoral) mechanisms. HLA mismatches are the primary drivers of T-cell mediated rejection, where recipient T cells recognize donor MHC molecules. Pre-formed DSAs against HLA antigens are the hallmark of antibody-mediated rejection. While both mechanisms can occur simultaneously (mixed rejection), understanding the specific alloantigens involved (HLA class I vs. class II), the effector cells (T cells vs. NK cells/macrophages), and the resulting pathology (e.g., T-cell infiltrate vs. endothelialitis/C4d deposition) is crucial. Differentiating between cellular and humoral rejection is key for management.

<a id='question-095'></a>

### Question 095: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 45 (antigens)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of donor-specific antigens (DSAs) in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) following solid organ transplantation, specifically the target antigens and the resulting immune response. (理解供体特异性抗原 (DSA) 在实体器官移植后抗体介导的排斥反应 (AMR) 中的作用，特别是靶抗原和由此产生的免疫反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen was negative. He is started on standard immunosuppression, including a calcineurin inhibitor (tacrolimus), an antiproliferative agent (mycophenolate mofetil), and corticosteroids. Six months post-transplant, he presents with acute onset of graft dysfunction, characterized by rising serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL) and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals evidence of acute tubular injury, glomerular basement membrane duplication ("double contours"), and C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Serum analysis shows elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA class II antigens on the donor kidney endothelial cells. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes confirms the presence of DSA-secreting B cells.

Which of the following is the MOST likely target antigen for the DSAs causing this patient's acute antibody-mediated rejection?

(A) ABO blood group antigens expressed on donor endothelial cells.
(B) Major histocompatibility complex (MHC) class I antigens (HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C) expressed on donor endothelial cells.
(C) Major histocompatibility complex (MHC) class II antigens (HLA-DR, HLA-DQ, HLA-DP) expressed on donor endothelial cells.
(D) Minor histocompatibility antigens expressed on donor endothelial cells.
(E) Cytomegalovirus (CMV) antigens expressed on donor endothelial cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Pre-transplant PRA:** Negative. This indicates no pre-existing antibodies against donor HLA antigens, reducing the risk of hyperacute rejection but not eliminating the risk of AMR.
- **Timeline:** 6 months post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for the development of de novo DSAs and subsequent AMR.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Acute graft dysfunction (rising creatinine, decreased urine output). This suggests an acute rejection episode.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular injury, glomerular basement membrane duplication ("double contours"), and C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. These are classic histological features of AMR. C4d deposition indicates complement activation, a key component of AMR.
- **Serological Findings:** Elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA class II antigens. This confirms the presence of antibodies specific to the donor's HLA molecules.
- **Immunological Findings:** DSA-secreting B cells in peripheral blood. This confirms the cellular source of the DSAs.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely target antigen for the DSAs causing AMR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** AMR is a major cause of late graft failure in solid organ transplantation. It is mediated by pre-formed or de novo DSAs that bind to donor HLA antigens expressed on the graft endothelium.
- **HLA Antigens:** Human Leukocyte Antigens (HLA) are the major histocompatibility complex (MHC) molecules in humans. They are highly polymorphic and are the primary targets of DSAs.
- **HLA Class I (HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C):** Expressed on virtually all nucleated cells. DSAs against HLA class I can cause AMR, often associated with more severe graft injury.
- **HLA Class II (HLA-DR, HLA-DQ, HLA-DP):** Primarily expressed on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells, and endothelial cells. DSAs against HLA class II are common causes of AMR. Endothelial expression is crucial for AMR pathogenesis.
- **Mechanism of AMR:** DSAs bind to HLA antigens on the graft endothelium, activating complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruiting inflammatory cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages). This causes endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation, thrombosis, and ultimately graft dysfunction.
- **Target Antigens in AMR:** The most immunogenic and clinically significant targets for DSAs are HLA class I and class II antigens expressed on the graft endothelium. While minor histocompatibility antigens and non-HLA antigens can also be targets, HLA antigens are the primary drivers of AMR in this clinical context. The vignette specifically states the DSAs target HLA class II antigens.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **ABO blood group antigens:** While ABO incompatibility can cause hyperacute rejection (pre-formed antibodies), this patient had a negative PRA screen and developed AMR 6 months post-transplant. ABO antigens are not the primary target in this scenario of de novo DSA formation leading to AMR. ABO antibodies are typically IgM and cause rapid complement activation and endothelial damage.
- (B): **MHC class I antigens (HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C):** These are indeed major targets for DSAs and can cause AMR. However, the vignette explicitly states that the DSAs target HLA *class II* antigens. While both classes are important, the specific information provided points to class II.
- (D): **Minor histocompatibility antigens:** These are polymorphic peptides presented by HLA molecules. They are less immunogenic than HLA antigens themselves but can contribute to graft rejection, particularly chronic rejection. They are less likely to be the primary target causing acute AMR with strong DSA levels and C4d deposition as described.
- (E): **Cytomegalovirus (CMV) antigens:** CMV infection is a common complication post-transplant and can cause graft dysfunction (CMV nephritis). However, CMV-related graft injury is typically due to direct viral cytopathic effects or inflammation, not primarily mediated by DSAs targeting CMV antigens on the graft endothelium. While CMV infection can sometimes trigger or exacerbate AMR, the primary target antigen described in the vignette is HLA class II.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | HLA Class II Antigens (Correct Answer) | ABO Antigens (Distractor A) | HLA Class I Antigens (Distractor B) | Minor Histocompatibility Antigens (Distractor D) | CMV Antigens (Distractor E) |
|--------------------------|---------------------------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Primary Target in AMR** | Yes                                   | No (Hyperacute Rejection)   | Yes                                | Less common, more chronic rejection             | No (Direct viral effect)   |
| **Expression**           | APCs, Endothelial Cells               | Endothelial Cells           | All Nucleated Cells                | Presented by HLA molecules                       | Infected cells              |
| **Timing**               | De novo (months post-transplant)       | Pre-formed (Hyperacute)     | De novo (months post-transplant)    | Chronic                                         | Post-transplant infection   |
| **Biopsy Findings**      | C4d deposition, Double contours        | Thrombotic microangiopathy | C4d deposition, Double contours    | Less specific                                    | Viral inclusions, inflammation |
| **Vignette Specificity** | Explicitly mentioned as target        | Not mentioned               | Mentioned as a class, but Class II specified | Not mentioned                                    | Not mentioned               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is primarily driven by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA class I and class II antigens expressed on the graft endothelium. The vignette highlights the importance of HLA class II antigens as targets in AMR, confirmed by DSA levels and histological findings (C4d deposition). Differentiating AMR from T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) and other causes of graft dysfunction (e.g., infection, calcineurin inhibitor toxicity) relies on identifying DSAs and characteristic histological features. Key differentials include hyperacute rejection (pre-formed ABO antibodies) and chronic rejection (often involving minor histocompatibility antigens).

<a id='question-096'></a>

### Question 096: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 46 (expressed)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular mechanisms underlying transplant rejection, specifically the role of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) targeting HLA class I molecules on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury (抗原-抗体反应导致补体激活和内皮损伤).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male patient, John, received a kidney transplant from a deceased donor six months ago. His initial post-transplant course was uneventful, and his creatinine levels remained stable around 1.2 mg/dL. He was maintained on a standard immunosuppressive regimen consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three months post-transplant, his creatinine began to rise gradually, reaching 2.5 mg/dL over the next three months. A renal biopsy was performed, revealing evidence of acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). Immunofluorescence microscopy showed linear C4d deposition along the peritubular capillaries, and electron microscopy revealed evidence of endothelial injury with double contours and basement membrane thickening. Serum analysis revealed the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA-A and HLA-B antigens on the donor kidney cells. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood showed increased expression of CD107a (LAMP-1) on NK cells and CD8+ T cells, suggesting recent degranulation. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the endothelial cell injury observed in this patient?

(A) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by C5b-9 (MAC) formation on endothelial cells.
(B) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells binding to Fc receptors on endothelial cells.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity involving perforin and granzyme release from CD8+ T cells directly targeting endothelial cells.
(D) Activation of the alternative complement pathway by antibodies binding to non-HLA antigens on endothelial cells.
(E) Direct binding of DSAs to endothelial cells, leading to internalization and degradation of HLA class I molecules, preventing antigen presentation.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, 6 months post-kidney transplant.
- **Clinical Course:** Initial stability, followed by gradual rise in creatinine (2.5 mg/dL) over 3 months, indicating graft dysfunction.
- **Diagnosis:** Renal biopsy shows acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Biopsy Findings:** Linear C4d deposition (a marker of complement activation) along peritubular capillaries, endothelial injury (double contours, basement membrane thickening).
- **Serology:** Presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA-A and HLA-B.
- **Immunology:** Increased CD107a (LAMP-1) expression on NK cells and CD8+ T cells, indicating recent degranulation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct mechanism of endothelial cell injury.

The key clues point towards antibody-mediated rejection. The presence of DSAs targeting HLA antigens, C4d deposition (indicating complement activation), and endothelial injury are hallmarks of AMR. The question asks for the *most direct* mechanism of endothelial injury in this context.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Occurs when recipient antibodies (pre-formed or de novo DSAs) target donor HLA antigens, primarily expressed on endothelial cells.
- **Mechanism:** DSAs bind to HLA class I molecules on the surface of donor endothelial cells. This binding activates the classical complement pathway.
- **Classical Complement Pathway:** C1q binds to the Fc portion of IgG antibodies bound to the antigen (HLA). This activates C1r and C1s, which cleave C4 and C2, leading to the formation of the C3 convertase (C4b2a). The C3 convertase cleaves C3 into C3a and C3b. C3b binds to the surface and participates in the formation of the C5 convertase (C4b2a3b). The C5 convertase cleaves C5 into C5a and C5b. C5b initiates the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), C5b-9.
- **Endothelial Injury:** The MAC (C5b-9) inserts into the endothelial cell membrane, forming pores that disrupt osmotic balance, leading to cell lysis and endothelial injury. This injury manifests histologically as double contours, basement membrane thickening, and ultimately graft dysfunction.
- **Other Mechanisms:** While other mechanisms contribute to AMR (e.g., ADCC, NK cell activation, inflammation), the direct lytic effect of the MAC on endothelial cells is considered a primary mechanism of endothelial injury in DSA-mediated AMR, especially when C4d deposition is present.
- **CD107a (LAMP-1):** This marker is associated with cytotoxic granule release (degranulation) by NK cells and CD8+ T cells. While these cells can be activated in AMR and contribute to injury, the primary target of DSAs is the endothelium, and the direct lytic effect via complement is central.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) via MAC formation (C5b-9) is a direct consequence of classical complement activation triggered by DSAs binding to HLA class I on endothelial cells. The presence of C4d deposition strongly supports this mechanism. This directly causes endothelial cell lysis and injury.
- (B): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) involves NK cells or other cytotoxic cells (like macrophages or neutrophils) binding to the Fc portion of antibodies coating target cells via Fc receptors (e.g., CD16 on NK cells). While NK cells can be activated in AMR (as suggested by increased CD107a), and ADCC can contribute, the primary mechanism directly linked to HLA-targeted DSAs and C4d deposition is CDC. ADCC typically requires Fcγ receptors on the effector cell and the target cell (endothelial cells have low FcγR expression).
- (C): T-cell mediated cytotoxicity is characteristic of cellular rejection (TCR-mediated rejection), where CD8+ T cells recognize foreign MHC class I molecules (or processed antigens presented on MHC I) on graft cells. This involves perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction. While T cells can be involved in AMR, the primary driver here is DSA targeting HLA, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury, not direct T-cell cytotoxicity against endothelial cells.
- (D): The alternative complement pathway is typically activated by microbial surfaces or certain self-molecules, not usually by antibodies binding to HLA antigens. While antibodies can sometimes activate the alternative pathway, the classical pathway is the primary route activated by DSAs binding to HLA. Furthermore, the question specifies DSAs targeting HLA antigens, which strongly implicates the classical pathway.
- (E): While DSAs bind to HLA class I molecules on endothelial cells, their primary effect in this context is to trigger complement activation leading to endothelial injury, not internalization and degradation of HLA molecules. Internalization/downregulation of HLA can occur, potentially contributing to rejection by reducing antigen presentation, but it's not the *most direct* mechanism of the observed endothelial injury (double contours, C4d deposition).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) - CDC | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) - ADCC | Cellular Rejection (TCR-mediated) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator** | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)        | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)        | Donor-Reactive T cells (CD8+)      |
| **Target Antigen**   | Donor HLA (esp. Class I on endothelium) | Donor HLA (esp. Class I on endothelium) | Donor MHC Class I (on graft cells) |
| **Key Mechanism**    | Complement Activation (Classical) -> MAC | NK cell/Macrophage binding via FcγR     | Perforin/Granzyme release          |
| **Hallmark Finding** | C4d deposition, Endothelial injury     | NK cell activation, Endothelial injury     | Infiltrating T cells, Tubulitis    |
| **Complement Role**  | Central (Classical Pathway)             | Minor/Indirect                          | None                              |
| **Cell Type**        | Endothelial cells (primary target)      | Endothelial cells (less direct)         | Graft Parenchymal cells           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary mechanism of endothelial cell injury in acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) triggered by DSAs targeting HLA class I is complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) via the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9). Key findings supporting this include the presence of DSAs, C4d deposition along peritubular capillaries, and evidence of endothelial injury. Differentiate AMR from cellular rejection (TCR-mediated) which involves T-cell infiltration and direct cytotoxicity, and from other forms of AMR involving ADCC or inflammation.

<a id='question-097'></a>

### Question 097: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 47 (cells.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific immune cells (specifically, CD8+ T cells) in graft rejection, particularly in the context of MHC mismatch and the mechanism of target cell killing. (理解特定免疫细胞（特别是CD8+ T细胞）在移植排斥反应中的作用，特别是在MHC不匹配的背景下以及靶细胞杀伤的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an HLA mismatch at the A and B loci. Post-transplant, he is started on immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Despite this regimen, he develops acute rejection 6 months after transplantation. A biopsy of the transplanted kidney reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates, and evidence of tubular cell damage. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, indicating antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). However, the patient's serum creatinine begins to rise rapidly, and repeat biopsy shows significant interstitial inflammation dominated by CD8+ T lymphocytes infiltrating tubules and arterioles, along with evidence of direct tubular cell cytotoxicity (tubular epithelial cell apoptosis and necrosis). Flow cytometry analysis of the infiltrating lymphocytes confirms a predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. The patient's immunosuppression is intensified, but graft function continues to decline.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity observed in this patient's kidney graft rejection?

(A) Fas ligand (FasL) binding to Fas receptor on target cells, triggering apoptosis.
(B) Release of TNF-α by CD8+ T cells, leading to target cell necrosis.
(C) Perforin and granzyme B released by CD8+ T cells, inducing apoptosis in target cells.
(D) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft cells.
(E) Complement-mediated lysis of target cells via the membrane attack complex (MAC).

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for transplantation immunology.
- **Graft Type & Mismatch:** Kidney transplant from a deceased donor with HLA A and B mismatch. HLA mismatch is a major trigger for T cell-mediated rejection.
- **Initial Immunosuppression:** Cyclosporine (calcineurin inhibitor, inhibits T cell activation), mycophenolate mofetil (inhibits purine synthesis, affects lymphocyte proliferation), prednisone (corticosteroid, broad immunosuppressant). Standard post-transplant regimen.
- **Acute Rejection:** Occurs 6 months post-transplant, despite immunosuppression. This indicates failure of immunosuppression or a particularly strong immune response.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, inflammatory infiltrates, tubular damage. General signs of rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Suggests antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), often involving donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) against HLA antigens.
- **Second Biopsy Findings:** Significant interstitial inflammation dominated by CD8+ T lymphocytes infiltrating tubules and arterioles, direct tubular cell cytotoxicity (apoptosis/necrosis). This points strongly towards T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR), specifically cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL)-mediated damage.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Infiltrating lymphocytes are predominantly CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. Confirms the presence of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and the key effector molecules they use.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct mechanism of CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity in this scenario.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell-Mediated Rejection (TCMR):** Primarily driven by T lymphocytes recognizing foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) on the graft.
- **CD8+ T Cells (CTLs):** Recognize foreign MHC class I molecules presented on the surface of graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells). Upon recognition, CTLs become activated and exert cytotoxic effects.
- **CTL Cytotoxicity Mechanisms:** CTLs employ two main pathways to kill target cells:
    1.  **Granule Exocytosis Pathway:** CTLs release granules containing perforin and granzymes. Perforin polymerizes on the target cell membrane, forming pores. Granzymes (serine proteases, particularly granzyme B) enter the target cell through these pores and activate caspases, initiating the apoptotic cascade. This is the primary mechanism for CTL-mediated killing.
    2.  **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL) on their surface. FasL binds to the Fas receptor (CD95), a death receptor, on the surface of target cells. This interaction triggers the formation of the death-inducing signaling complex (DISC) within the target cell, activating caspases and leading to apoptosis. While important, it's often considered a secondary mechanism compared to perforin/granzyme.
- **Other Mechanisms:**
    - **TNF-α:** Tumor necrosis factor-alpha is a cytokine produced by activated T cells (and other immune cells). While it can contribute to inflammation and tissue damage, it primarily induces necrosis rather than the programmed cell death (apoptosis) characteristic of CTL killing.
    - **ADCC:** Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity involves NK cells (or other effector cells) recognizing target cells coated with antibodies (e.g., IgG). The Fc receptors on the effector cell bind to the antibody Fc region, triggering the release of cytotoxic granules. This is relevant in AMR where antibodies coat graft cells, but the vignette specifically highlights CD8+ T cell infiltration and cytotoxicity, not NK cell activity.
    - **Complement-Mediated Lysis:** Involves activation of the complement cascade, leading to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), which creates pores in the target cell membrane, causing lysis (necrosis). This is a major mechanism in AMR, but not the primary mechanism of CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Fas ligand (FasL) binding to Fas receptor on target cells, triggering apoptosis. This is a valid mechanism of CTL-mediated cytotoxicity, but the perforin/granzyme pathway is generally considered the primary and most potent mechanism, especially for CD8+ T cells. The vignette emphasizes the presence of granzyme B, strongly suggesting this pathway is dominant.
- (B): Release of TNF-α by CD8+ T cells, leading to target cell necrosis. TNF-α is a cytokine involved in inflammation and can cause cell death, but it typically induces necrosis, not the apoptosis characteristic of CTL killing. Furthermore, perforin/granzyme is the direct cytotoxic mechanism.
- (D): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft cells. While the patient has evidence of AMR (C4d deposition), the primary cytotoxic cells identified in the biopsy are CD8+ T cells, not NK cells, and the mechanism described is direct T cell killing, not ADCC.
- (E): Complement-mediated lysis of target cells via the membrane attack complex (MAC). This is a key mechanism in AMR, where antibodies activate complement. However, the question specifically asks about the mechanism of CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity, which involves direct cell-cell contact and release of cytotoxic molecules, not complement activation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CD8+ T Cell Cytotoxicity (Correct Answer) | Fas/FasL Pathway (Distractor A) | TNF-α Mediated Killing (Distractor B) | ADCC (Distractor D) | Complement Lysis (Distractor E) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :----------------- | :------------------------------ |
| **Primary Effector Cell** | CD8+ T Lymphocyte (CTL)                   | CD8+ T Lymphocyte              | CD8+ T Lymphocyte (and others)       | NK Cell            | Complement System               |
| **Target Recognition**   | MHC Class I + Peptide                    | Fas Receptor (CD95)            | TNF Receptor                         | Antibody-coated cell | Antibody-coated cell            |
| **Key Molecules**        | Perforin, Granzyme B                      | FasL, Fas                      | TNF-α                                | Antibody, Fc Receptor | Antibodies, Complement Proteins |
| **Mechanism of Cell Death** | Apoptosis (via caspases)                  | Apoptosis (via caspases)       | Necrosis (primarily)                  | Apoptosis          | Lysis (Necrosis)                 |
| **Relevance to Vignette** | Directly described (CD8+ cells, Granzyme B) | Possible, but secondary        | Not the primary mechanism             | Relevant to AMR, not T cell killing | Relevant to AMR, not T cell killing |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary mechanism by which CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) kill target cells is through the release of perforin and granzymes, which induce apoptosis. Differentiate this mechanism from Fas/FasL interaction (also apoptosis), TNF-α release (necrosis), ADCC (NK cells, antibodies), and complement-mediated lysis (AMR). Remember that CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I molecules, while CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II molecules.

<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 48 (result)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of action of calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) in preventing T-cell activation following transplantation, specifically their effect on intracellular signaling pathways and cytokine production. (理解钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素、他克莫司）在移植后预防T细胞活化的作用机制，特别是它们对细胞内信号通路和细胞因子产生的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. Post-operatively, he is started on a standard immunosuppressive regimen including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, the patient develops fever, chills, and acute onset of severe lower back pain. Laboratory tests reveal elevated serum creatinine (3.5 mg/dL, baseline 1.2 mg/dL), proteinuria (3+), and microscopic hematuria. A renal biopsy is performed, showing acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and interstitial edema with mild lymphocytic infiltrate. Immunofluorescence staining is negative for C4d deposition. The patient's cyclosporine trough level is found to be subtherapeutic (5 ng/mL, target range 10-15 ng/mL). The nephrologist increases the cyclosporine dose. Which of the following intracellular signaling pathways is MOST directly inhibited by cyclosporine, leading to its immunosuppressive effect?

(A) Phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K)/Akt pathway
(B) Mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathway
(C) Nuclear factor kappa B (NF-κB) pathway
(D) Calcineurin pathway
(E) Janus kinase (JAK)/Signal transducer and activator of transcription (STAT) pathway

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the context for immunosuppression.
- **Immunosuppression:** Receiving cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Cyclosporine is a key immunosuppressant mentioned.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, chills, acute severe lower back pain 3 weeks post-transplant. These symptoms, along with elevated creatinine, proteinuria, and hematuria, are suggestive of acute rejection or infection.
- **Renal Biopsy:** ATN, interstitial edema, mild lymphocytic infiltrate, negative C4d. This pattern is consistent with T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR), specifically acute cellular rejection (ACR), although ATN can also be caused by ischemia or drug toxicity. The negative C4d rules out antibody-mediated rejection (ABMR).
- **Cyclosporine Level:** Subtherapeutic level (5 ng/mL vs. target 10-15 ng/mL). This suggests inadequate immunosuppression might be contributing to the rejection episode.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct intracellular signaling pathway inhibited by cyclosporine. This focuses on the mechanism of action of cyclosporine.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Activation:** T-cell activation requires two signals. Signal 1 is via the T-cell receptor (TCR) binding to the MHC-peptide complex on the antigen-presenting cell (APC). Signal 2 is typically provided by co-stimulatory molecules (e.g., CD28 on T-cell binding to B7 on APC).
- **Signal Transduction:** Signal 1 initiates intracellular signaling cascades. Binding of the TCR to the MHC-peptide complex leads to the activation of tyrosine kinases, such as Lck and Zap70. These kinases phosphorylate immunoreceptor tyrosine-based activation motifs (ITAMs) on the cytoplasmic tails of the CD3 complex associated with the TCR.
- **Calcineurin Activation:** Phosphorylation of ITAMs recruits and activates ZAP-70, which in turn phosphorylates the adapter protein LAT (Linker for Activation of T cells). LAT recruits and activates phospholipase Cγ (PLCγ). PLCγ hydrolyzes phosphatidylinositol 4,5-bisphosphate (PIP2) into inositol trisphosphate (IP3) and diacylglycerol (DAG).
- **Calcium Release:** IP3 binds to receptors on the endoplasmic reticulum (ER), causing the release of stored calcium (Ca2+) into the cytoplasm.
- **Calcineurin Phosphorylation:** The increased intracellular Ca2+ binds to calmodulin. The Ca2+-calmodulin complex then activates calcineurin, a calcium-dependent serine/threonine phosphatase.
- **NFAT Dephosphorylation and Nuclear Translocation:** Activated calcineurin dephosphorylates the nuclear factor of activated T-cells (NFAT), a transcription factor. Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates from the cytoplasm into the nucleus.
- **Cytokine Gene Transcription:** In the nucleus, NFAT binds to promoter regions of genes encoding key T-cell cytokines, most notably interleukin-2 (IL-2). IL-2 is crucial for T-cell proliferation and differentiation.
- **Cyclosporine Mechanism:** Cyclosporine binds to an intracellular protein called cyclophilin (an immunophilin). The cyclosporine-cyclophilin complex then binds to and inhibits calcineurin. By inhibiting calcineurin, cyclosporine prevents the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT. This blocks the transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines essential for T-cell activation and proliferation.
- **Other Pathways:**
    - **PI3K/Akt:** Involved in cell survival, growth, and metabolism. Activated downstream of TCR signaling but not the primary target of cyclosporine.
    - **MAPK:** Involved in cell proliferation, differentiation, and apoptosis. Activated downstream of TCR signaling but not the primary target of cyclosporine.
    - **NF-κB:** Involved in inflammation and immune responses. Activated by various stimuli, including TCR signaling, but its activation pathway is distinct from the calcineurin pathway and is not the primary target of cyclosporine.
    - **JAK/STAT:** Involved in cytokine signaling. Activated by cytokine receptors binding their respective ligands. Not directly inhibited by cyclosporine.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K)/Akt pathway:** This pathway is involved in T-cell survival and proliferation downstream of the TCR, but it is not the primary target of cyclosporine. Cyclosporine acts upstream of this pathway by inhibiting calcineurin.
- (B) **Mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) pathway:** This pathway is involved in T-cell proliferation and differentiation downstream of the TCR, but it is not the primary target of cyclosporine. Cyclosporine acts upstream of this pathway by inhibiting calcineurin.
- (C) **Nuclear factor kappa B (NF-κB) pathway:** This pathway is crucial for the transcription of many inflammatory cytokines and adhesion molecules. While TCR signaling can activate NF-κB, this pathway is distinct from the calcineurin/NFAT pathway and is not the primary target of cyclosporine.
- (E) **Janus kinase (JAK)/Signal transducer and activator of transcription (STAT) pathway:** This pathway is primarily involved in signaling downstream of cytokine receptors. Cyclosporine inhibits T-cell activation upstream of cytokine production, not the signaling pathway induced by cytokines themselves.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Cyclosporine Mechanism                               | PI3K/Akt Pathway                               | MAPK Pathway                                  | NF-κB Pathway                                  | JAK/STAT Pathway                               |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Target**   | Calcineurin (via cyclophilin complex)                | PI3K enzyme                                     | MAP kinases (e.g., ERK, JNK, p38)                | IκB kinase (IKK)                                | JAK kinases                                     |
| **Key Intermediate** | Calmodulin, NFAT                                     | Akt/PKB                                         | Various MAP kinase cascades                     | IκBα                                           | STAT proteins                                   |
| **Downstream Effect**| Blocks NFAT nuclear translocation, inhibits IL-2 transcription | Promotes cell survival, growth, proliferation | Promotes cell proliferation, differentiation | Promotes transcription of inflammatory genes | Mediates cytokine signaling                      |
| **Relevance to T-cell Activation** | Directly inhibits T-cell activation by blocking IL-2 | Involved in T-cell survival/proliferation     | Involved in T-cell proliferation/differentiation | Involved in T-cell activation/inflammation     | Involved in response to cytokines                 |
| **Affected by Cyclosporine?** | YES (Directly inhibited)                          | NO (Indirectly affected by reduced T-cell activation) | NO (Indirectly affected)                      | NO (Indirectly affected)                      | NO (Indirectly affected)                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cyclosporine is a calcineurin inhibitor used to prevent organ transplant rejection. It works by binding to cyclophilin, forming a complex that inhibits calcineurin. Inhibition of calcineurin prevents the dephosphorylation and nuclear translocation of NFAT, thereby blocking the transcription of IL-2 and other cytokines crucial for T-cell activation and proliferation. Understanding this specific intracellular mechanism is key for differentiating cyclosporine from other immunosuppressants like mTOR inhibitors (sirolimus, everolimus) or antimetabolites (mycophenolate mofetil).
