<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 49 (grafts)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) in recognizing and destroying allogeneic MHC molecules on graft cells, leading to graft damage. (理解器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）识别和破坏移植物细胞上的异体MHC分子，导致移植物损伤的过程。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an A blood type, while the recipient is blood type B. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including a calcineurin inhibitor (cyclosporine), an antimetabolite (mycophenolate mofetil), and corticosteroids (prednisone). Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells. The patient's serum creatinine continues to rise despite adjustments to immunosuppression.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the acute graft dysfunction in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed anti-donor ABO antibodies leading to complement activation and endothelial damage.
(B) Acute tubular necrosis secondary to ischemia-reperfusion injury during the transplantation procedure.
(C) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.
(D) Acute cellular rejection mediated by recipient T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I and class II molecules on graft cells.
(E) Infection of the transplanted kidney with a common opportunistic pathogen, such as Cytomegalovirus (CMV), causing interstitial nephritis.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for potential rejection.
- **ABO Incompatibility:** Donor is A, recipient is B. This is a major ABO incompatibility, which can lead to hyperacute or accelerated acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (calcineurin inhibitor, antimetabolite, steroids) is initiated. This aims to prevent rejection but doesn't eliminate the risk entirely.
- **Timeline:** Symptoms (fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine) develop 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection (either cellular or antibody-mediated). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, while chronic rejection occurs much later.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltration, tubular epithelial cell damage, and endothelialitis. These findings are consistent with acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR) or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). Endothelialitis is a key feature often seen in AMR but can also occur in severe ACR.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway and is a strong indicator of antibody-mediated injury, often seen in AMR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells in peripheral blood. This suggests a T-cell mediated immune response, characteristic of ACR.
- **Clinical Course:** Worsening renal function despite immunosuppression adjustment. This indicates ongoing rejection process.

The combination of clinical presentation (3 weeks post-transplant), biopsy findings (inflammation, tubular damage, endothelialitis), C4d deposition (suggesting AMR), and increased CD8+ T cells (suggesting ACR) points towards a complex rejection episode. However, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism. While C4d deposition suggests AMR, the ABO incompatibility is a known risk factor for AMR. The increased CD8+ T cells strongly suggest a cellular component. Acute cellular rejection (ACR) is a common cause of graft dysfunction in the first few weeks post-transplant, mediated by T cells recognizing donor MHC antigens. The biopsy findings are also very typical for ACR. While AMR is possible given the ABO mismatch and C4d, the prominent T-cell component (CD8+ increase, cellular infiltrate) makes ACR a very strong possibility, and often co-exists with AMR in complex cases. Given the options, ACR mediated by T cells is a primary mechanism of acute rejection in this timeframe.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T-cell mediated immune response against the donor graft.
    - **Mechanism:** Recipient T lymphocytes (primarily CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) recognize donor MHC class I and class II molecules on the surface of graft cells as foreign antigens. This recognition occurs via the T-cell receptor (TCR) binding to the peptide-MHC complex.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Directly kill graft cells expressing donor MHC class I molecules by releasing cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) or inducing apoptosis via Fas/FasL interaction.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft or on donor cells themselves. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages, recruit other inflammatory cells, and enhance the cytotoxic activity of CD8+ T cells. They can also directly participate in cell-mediated cytotoxicity.
    - **Pathology:** Characterized by interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltration (lymphocytes, macrophages), tubular epithelial cell damage, and sometimes endothelialitis.
    - **Timeline:** Typically occurs within the first few weeks to months post-transplant.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** This involves antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) directed against donor antigens, primarily HLA or ABO blood group antigens.
    - **Mechanism:** Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement (classical pathway) and/or recruiting Fc receptor-bearing cells (e.g., NK cells, macrophages) via antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). Complement activation leads to endothelial cell damage, inflammation, and microvascular injury (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis).
    - **C4d Deposition:** A marker of classical complement pathway activation, commonly found in AMR.
    - **Pathology:** Characterized by microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and endothelial cell injury.
    - **Timeline:** Can occur early (hyperacute, accelerated acute) or later (chronic).
- **Ischemia-Reperfusion Injury:** Occurs during the procurement and transplantation process due to lack of blood flow followed by restoration of blood flow. Causes direct cellular damage (necrosis, apoptosis) and inflammation. Typically manifests within the first 24-48 hours.
- **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis), and gradual loss of graft function. Involves both cellular and humoral immunity.
- **Opportunistic Infections:** Common post-transplant due to immunosuppression. CMV is a frequent cause of interstitial nephritis and graft dysfunction.

In this case, the timeline (3 weeks), biopsy findings (inflammation, tubular damage, endothelialitis), C4d deposition, and increased CD8+ T cells all point towards an acute rejection episode. While C4d suggests AMR, the ABO incompatibility is a known risk factor. However, ACR is also very common in this timeframe and is characterized by T-cell infiltration and damage, consistent with the CD8+ T cell increase and biopsy findings. Option (D) directly addresses the T-cell mediated component, which is a major mechanism of acute rejection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection involving pre-formed anti-donor ABO antibodies leading to complement activation and endothelial damage. This is plausible given the ABO incompatibility (A donor, B recipient) and the presence of C4d deposition, which indicates complement activation. Hyperacute rejection (minutes to hours) or accelerated acute rejection (days) can occur due to pre-formed ABO antibodies. However, the presentation is at 3 weeks, which is more typical for acute rejection (cellular or antibody-mediated) rather than hyperacute. While AMR is possible, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism, and ACR is also very common. The increased CD8+ T cells specifically point towards a cellular component. Furthermore, while ABO antibodies can cause AMR, they are not always pre-formed, and de novo antibodies can also develop. The vignette doesn't explicitly state pre-formed antibodies. Option (D) describes the fundamental mechanism of ACR, which is a very common cause of acute graft dysfunction.
- (B): Acute tubular necrosis secondary to ischemia-reperfusion injury during the transplantation procedure. This typically occurs within the first 24-48 hours post-transplant, presenting with a rapid rise in creatinine. The patient's symptoms developed at 3 weeks, making ischemia-reperfusion injury unlikely as the primary cause of the current dysfunction.
- (C): Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, typically occurring months to years post-transplant. The patient is only 3 weeks post-transplant, which is too early for chronic rejection. Chronic rejection involves gradual fibrosis and vascular lesions, not the acute inflammatory infiltrate described in the biopsy.
- (E): Infection of the transplanted kidney with a common opportunistic pathogen, such as Cytomegalovirus (CMV), causing interstitial nephritis. CMV infection is a common complication post-transplant and can cause interstitial nephritis, leading to graft dysfunction. However, the biopsy findings described (diffuse inflammation, tubular damage, endothelialitis) are more typical of rejection than CMV infection, which often shows characteristic viral inclusion bodies and cytopathic effects. While infection should be considered, the constellation of findings (timeline, biopsy, C4d, CD8+ T cells) strongly points towards rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Ischemia-Reperfusion Injury | Chronic Rejection | CMV Infection |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------|-------------------|---------------|
| **Timeline**            | Days to months                  | Days to months                    | Hours to days               | Months to years   | Weeks to months|
| **Mediator**            | T lymphocytes (CD8+, CD4+)      | Antibodies (anti-HLA, ABO)        | Ischemia/Reperfusion        | T cells, Abs      | Virus (CMV)   |
| **Key Pathology**       | Interstitial inflammation, tubular damage, endothelialitis | Microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, capillaritis), thrombosis, endothelial injury | Tubular necrosis, inflammation | Fibrosis, vascular changes (arteriosclerosis) | Interstitial nephritis, viral inclusions |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Usually negative                | Often positive                    | Negative                    | Often positive    | Negative      |
| **CD8+ T cells**        | Increased                       | Variable                          | Not specific                | Increased        | Variable      |
| **ABO Incompatibility** | Not primary mechanism           | Can be a cause                    | Not relevant                | Not relevant      | Not relevant  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute rejection is a major cause of graft dysfunction in the early post-transplant period. It can be mediated by T cells (ACR) or antibodies (AMR), or a combination of both. ACR is characterized by T-cell infiltration and direct cytotoxicity, while AMR involves antibody binding, complement activation, and endothelial damage. Distinguish acute rejection (weeks) from hyperacute (minutes/hours) and chronic (months/years) rejection based on timeline and pathology. Remember that C4d deposition is a marker for AMR, but ACR can also occur, especially in the context of ABO incompatibility which can trigger both. Ischemia-reperfusion injury occurs early, and infections like CMV can mimic rejection.

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Transplantation: Part 2 Focus 50 (except)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of action and resistance associated with immunosuppressive drugs used in transplantation, specifically focusing on calcineurin inhibitors (cyclosporine, tacrolimus) and their downstream effects on T-cell activation and cytokine production. (理解移植中使用的免疫抑制药物的作用机制和耐药性，特别是钙调神经磷酸酶抑制剂（环孢素、他克莫司）对T细胞激活和细胞因子产生的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male patient, 3 months post-kidney transplant, presents with acute onset of fever (38.5°C), chills, and worsening graft dysfunction (elevated serum creatinine from 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL). He is on standard triple immunosuppression: tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. His last dose of tacrolimus was 12 hours ago. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with cellular rejection. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in CD4+ T cells expressing CD25 (IL-2 receptor alpha chain) and CD69 (early T-cell activation marker). Serum tacrolimus levels are undetectable (<5 ng/mL). The patient's physician suspects tacrolimus resistance or non-adherence and considers switching to cyclosporine. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed T-cell activation despite the patient's immunosuppressive regimen?

(A) Increased expression of P-glycoprotein (MDR1) leading to enhanced efflux of tacrolimus from T cells.
(B) Mutation in the FKBP12 gene, preventing the formation of the tacrolimus-FKBP12 complex required for calcineurin inhibition.
(C) Upregulation of NF-κB signaling pathway, bypassing the need for calcineurin activation for IL-2 transcription.
(D) Increased production of IL-2 by regulatory T cells (Tregs) to compensate for the lack of tacrolimus effect.
(E) Downregulation of the IL-2 receptor (CD25) on activated T cells, preventing IL-2-mediated proliferation.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, 3 months post-kidney transplant. This sets the context of immunosuppression and potential rejection.
- **Presentation:** Acute fever, chills, worsening graft function (elevated creatinine). These are classic signs of acute rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. Tacrolimus is a calcineurin inhibitor.
- **Biopsy:** Acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with cellular rejection. Confirms acute rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD4+ T cells expressing CD25 and CD69. CD69 is an early activation marker, and CD25 (IL-2Rα) is upregulated upon T-cell activation, indicating active T-cell response despite immunosuppression.
- **Tacrolimus Levels:** Undetectable (<5 ng/mL). Suggests non-adherence or resistance.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for T-cell activation despite tacrolimus therapy. This points towards a mechanism of resistance to tacrolimus.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Tacrolimus Mechanism:** Tacrolimus binds to the intracellular protein FKBP12 (FK506-binding protein 12). This complex then binds to and inhibits calcineurin, a calcium-dependent phosphatase. Calcineurin normally dephosphorylates the transcription factor NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T-cells). Dephosphorylated NFAT translocates to the nucleus and promotes the transcription of genes encoding IL-2 and other cytokines crucial for T-cell proliferation and activation. By inhibiting calcineurin, tacrolimus prevents NFAT activation and subsequent IL-2 production, thereby suppressing T-cell mediated rejection.
- **Tacrolimus Resistance:** A major mechanism of resistance involves increased efflux of the drug from T cells, mediated by the ATP-binding cassette transporter P-glycoprotein (P-gp), also known as multidrug resistance protein 1 (MDR1) or ABCB1. Upregulation or increased activity of P-gp pumps tacrolimus out of the cell, reducing its intracellular concentration and effectiveness. This is a common cause of tacrolimus failure, especially in cases of suspected non-adherence, as the body may compensate by increasing drug efflux.
- **Option (A) Explanation:** Increased expression of P-glycoprotein (MDR1) leads to enhanced efflux of tacrolimus from T cells. This directly reduces the intracellular concentration of tacrolimus, diminishing its inhibitory effect on calcineurin and allowing T-cell activation (as evidenced by increased CD25 and CD69 expression) and rejection to occur despite the prescribed dose. This is a well-established mechanism of tacrolimus resistance.
- **Option (B) Explanation:** FKBP12 is the binding partner for tacrolimus. A mutation in FKBP12 that prevents binding would render tacrolimus ineffective. However, mutations in FKBP12 are extremely rare causes of tacrolimus resistance. The primary mechanism involves drug transport or downstream signaling.
- **Option (C) Explanation:** NF-κB is another transcription factor involved in immune responses, particularly for pro-inflammatory cytokines and co-stimulatory molecules. While NF-κB signaling is important, tacrolimus acts upstream by inhibiting calcineurin/NFAT, which is essential for IL-2 production. Bypassing calcineurin inhibition by activating NF-κB is not a known mechanism of tacrolimus resistance; rather, tacrolimus prevents NFAT-mediated transcription, which is distinct from NF-κB pathways.
- **Option (D) Explanation:** Regulatory T cells (Tregs) produce IL-2, but their primary role is immunosuppression. Increased IL-2 production by Tregs would not explain the observed T-cell activation (CD25/CD69 upregulation) and rejection. In fact, Tregs would counteract the rejection process.
- **Option (E) Explanation:** Downregulation of the IL-2 receptor (CD25) would impair T-cell proliferation and response to IL-2, which would *inhibit* T-cell activation, not cause it. The vignette shows *increased* CD25 expression, indicating activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** Increased P-gp efflux is a common and well-documented mechanism of tacrolimus resistance, leading to reduced intracellular drug levels and subsequent T-cell activation.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Mutations in FKBP12 are exceedingly rare causes of tacrolimus resistance. The most common mechanisms involve drug transport (P-gp) or downstream signaling alterations.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Tacrolimus inhibits calcineurin/NFAT pathway, which is distinct from the NF-κB pathway. While both are involved in immune responses, bypassing calcineurin inhibition via NF-κB activation is not a recognized mechanism of tacrolimus resistance.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Tregs are immunosuppressive and would not cause T-cell activation and rejection. Increased IL-2 production by Tregs is counterintuitive in this context.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Downregulation of CD25 (IL-2Rα) would impair T-cell proliferation and activation. The vignette shows *upregulation* of CD25, indicating T-cell activation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Tacrolimus Mechanism of Action | Tacrolimus Resistance (P-gp) | Cyclosporine Resistance (P-gp) | Sirolimus Mechanism | Mycophenolate Mofetil Mechanism |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------|--------------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------------------|
| Target              | Calcineurin                     | Tacrolimus Efflux           | Cyclosporine Efflux            | mTOR                | IMP dehydrogenase                |
| Binding Partner     | FKBP12                          | P-glycoprotein (MDR1/ABCB1) | P-glycoprotein (MDR1/ABCB1)    | FKBP12              | N/A                               |
| Downstream Effect   | Inhibits NFAT/IL-2 transcription | Reduced intracellular drug   | Reduced intracellular drug     | Inhibits S6K1/4E-BP1 | Inhibits purine synthesis        |
| Resistance Mechanism| P-gp efflux, CYP3A5 mutations   | Increased P-gp expression/activity | Increased P-gp expression/activity | P-gp efflux        | N/A                               |
| Clinical Consequence| T-cell activation, rejection    | T-cell activation, rejection | T-cell activation, rejection   | T-cell activation, rejection | Lymphocyte proliferation inhibition failure |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tacrolimus resistance commonly occurs due to increased drug efflux mediated by P-glycoprotein (MDR1/ABCB1). This reduces intracellular drug concentration, allowing T-cell activation and rejection. Differentiating mechanisms of resistance for different immunosuppressants (e.g., P-gp efflux for tacrolimus, cyclosporine, and sirolimus) is crucial. Understanding the specific pathway inhibited by each drug (calcineurin for tacrolimus/cyclosporine, mTOR for sirolimus, IMPDH for mycophenolate) helps contextualize resistance mechanisms.
